# Juan Luis Vives <note>original: "Lud. Vives"; referring to the influential Spanish Renaissance humanist (1493–1540).</note>

*Nature is not to be examined by the* **Lamp** *of the* **Gentiles** <note>Gentiles: Here referring to the non-Christian, classical Greek and Roman philosophers, specifically Aristotle.</note>, *which yields a light that is both dim and unreliable; instead, it must be examined by the* **Torch** *of the* **Sun**, *which* **Christ** *has brought into the darkness of the* **World**. *And for that very reason, we have had cause to write, so that we should not be forced to follow the traditions of the* **Gentiles**; *between whom and us there is such a vast difference in* **Religion**. *And to tell the truth, just as* **Aristotle's** *writings have much learning and cleverness, they also have very much obscurity. This has given some people the opportunity to search for—and imagine they see—those things which do not actually exist, etc.*

->We therefore in your light, O God, shall see light. *Psalm 36:10.* <note>In modern Bibles, this verse is usually numbered as Psalm 36:9: "in thy light shall we see light."</note><-

<summary>This page features a quote by the humanist Juan Luis Vives arguing that natural philosophy should be guided by Christian revelation rather than the "obscure" traditions of classical pagan philosophers like Aristotle.</summary>
<keywords>Juan Luis Vives, Aristotle, philosophy vs. religion, classical tradition, Psalm 36, Christian humanism</keywords>

# To those truly devoted to wisdom, from Christ
## the fountain of wisdom, greeting.

---

*Jacobus Acontius <note>Jacobus Acontius (1492–1566) was an Italian jurist, theologian, and philosopher known for his work on logic and his advocacy for religious tolerance.</note>, a most excellent man, offended by the bad habits of our "scribbling age," wished that it could be arranged that no one should write and publish anything unless it were something new. This new material should be based on the author's own observation and should contribute to the glory of God and the edification <term>original: edification; the process of instructing or improving someone morally or intellectually, often in a religious context</term> of the Church. From such work, so much fruit might be hoped for that the time spent reading it could not be better spent elsewhere. In this way, nothing would be done that had already been done, but only that which was yet to be accomplished. For few writers (he says) bring any*

<summary>In this preface, Comenius cites the philosopher Jacobus Acontius to argue that new books should only be published if they offer original, beneficial insights that serve God and the Church, rather than merely repeating old ideas.</summary>
<keywords>Jacobus Acontius, wisdom, publishing ethics, edification, originality, Jan Amos Comenius, 17th-century literature</keywords>

# The Preface.

*...thing of their own; but only steal things and words from which they make books, and so on.* Those who must examine that *jumble* <note>original: *farrago*; a confused mixture or medley</note> of books—by which we are nearly overwhelmed every year—know this to be most true. For if you look at the titles, they are always new and very impressive <note>original: *specious*; having a deceptive attraction or allure, appearing better than it actually is</note>; but if you look at the content, it is always the same material boiled over and over more than a thousand times, with <term>rehashed leftovers</term> <note>original: *Coleworts*; literally cabbage, but used here as a metaphor for repetitive, stale ideas being served again</note> crammed in until it becomes nauseating. 

And even when some new observation is offered, what is the point of writing entire books for it? These new findings are so buried among ordinary things that a person either has no desire to search for what is new in them, or cannot do so without weariness of spirit and loss of time. But it is not my intention to speak out against this disorder at great length. I come now to explain why I myself am appearing in public. And I will

<summary>The author complains that the publishing world is filled with repetitive, unoriginal books that "rehash" old ideas, making it difficult for readers to find genuine new insights. He then transitions to explaining his own motives for publishing this work.</summary>
<keywords>literary criticism, unoriginality, publishing, farrago, Coleworts, Jan Amos Comenius, 17th-century literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of his motives for publishing, moving from a critique of other authors to his own contributions to philosophy and linguistics.</meta>

# The Preface.

lay it open. In a word: I bring something new and different from the common way of *Philosophy*. I bring it in such a way that I hope it will be without hindrance or trouble <note>original: "molestation"</note> to anyone, as it contains matters of very great importance <note>original: "moment"</note> in a very few pages <note>original: "leaves"</note>. I also bring it to satisfy the desires of others in this regard. 

Last year, I gave proof of my linguistic <note>original: "Philological"</note> endeavors in the *The Door of Languages Unlocked* <note>original: *Janua Linguarum referata*</note> (or a *nursery* <note>original: "seminary"</note> *of Arts and Languages*). This work was courteously received and met with applause; it was approved by the judgment of almost everyone, as several letters addressed to me or my friends regarding that matter testify. Consequently, some people—among those who are currently devoting their desires, thoughts, and efforts <note>original: "devours"; likely a variant or error for "endeavors"</note> to reforming the method of study—began to urge me to publish my philosophical works, or at least requested that I share my ideas, especially in *Physics* <note>original: "Physicks"; at this time, this referred generally to natural science and the study of the physical world</note>. Ha- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the original catchword "ving" indicates the next page begins with "Having."</note>

# The Preface.

Having no other intention, then, but to contribute something on my own part that may be useful, if possible—or else to give others the opportunity through me to produce better works—I intended to publish <note>original: "expose to the light"</note> this *Synopsis of Physics* <note>original: "Physicks"</note>, which was recently taught <note>original: "dictated"</note> in this school. I did this so that a public assessment <note>original: "censure"</note> could be made of this work as well as my previous one. To make that possible, it seemed appropriate <note>original: "meet"</note> to give some further indication <note>original: "intimation"</note> of the purpose and scope of our project to those who will offer themselves as our critics <note>original: "censors"</note>.

After the disastrous fate of exile had forced me—who was a theologian <note>original: "Divine"</note> by profession—back into the service of the school, where I desired to conduct myself not carelessly, but in a way that would allow me to fulfill the trust placed in me, it happened that I came across, among other things, the books of Joannes Ludovicus Vives <note>A Spanish humanist and educator (1493–1540) known for his influence on educational theory</note> titled *On the Handing Down of the Disciplines* <note>original: *de tradendis disciplinis*</note>. In

<summary>Comenius explains his decision to publish his physics lectures for public evaluation, noting how his exile forced him back into teaching and led him to discover the influential educational theories of Ludovicus Vives.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Amos Comenius, Synopsis of Physics, educational reform, Joannes Ludovicus Vives, De tradendis disciplinis, exile, pedagogy, 17th-century science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's reflection on the work of Ludovicus Vives. Having discovered Vives's critiques of the educational system, the author expresses frustration that a more perfect method had not yet been established in the century since Vives wrote.</meta>

# The Preface.

When I had found in these works most wholesome advice for the restoration <note>original: "repairing"</note> of Philosophy and the entire course of studies, I began to grieve deeply that a man of such sharp intelligence <note>original: "piercing a wit"</note>, after he had noted so many obvious errors, had not set his hand to making those rough paths smooth. However, the judgment of one person regarding this excellent writer—*that Vives saw more clearly what was lacking than what was actually there*—made me consider that it is usual for God’s wisdom to reveal <note>original: "communicate"</note> things gradually. 

Yet I thought to myself that others should take this as an opportunity to work toward designing one certain and infallible path among so many deviations <note>original: "deviacions"</note> revealed to them. I wondered why men were so reluctant to attempt <note>original: "backward to essay"</note> this for a full hundred years (for I did not know then whether anyone had attempted it). But it happened that a certain learned man, to whom I communicated these complaints of mine in a more 

<summary>Comenius reflects on the work of Joannes Ludovicus Vives, noting that while Vives identified the errors of traditional philosophy, he did not provide a new system, leading Comenius to wonder why no one had attempted to fix these issues in the hundred years since.</summary>
<keywords>Joannes Ludovicus Vives, philosophy, educational reform, divine providence, pedagogical methods, 17th-century intellectual history</keywords>

familiar manner, showed me a book called *Forerunner of Restored Philosophy* <note>original: *Prodromus philosophiæ instaurandæ*</note> by *Thomas Campanella*, an *Italian*. I read this through with incredible joy, and being fired with an exceeding great hope for new insight <note>original: "Light"</note>, I eagerly leafed through his <margin>-</margin> *Realistic Philosophical Summary* <note>original: *Realis philosophia epilogistica*</note> (for so he calls it), published in four books, as well as the <margin>-</margin> books *On the Sense of Things* <note>original: *De rerum sensu*</note>, wherever I could find them. Through these, I found my desires somewhat satisfied, but not entirely. For his very foundation—<margin>-</margin> that all things were made up of only two contrary principles—bothered <note>original: "offended"</note> me. (For I was already most fully convinced of the number of three principles <margin>=</margin> based on the divine book of *Genesis*. # The Preface.

[Campa]nella <note>The text begins mid-word, continuing from the previous page's mention of Thomas Campanella, an Italian philosopher.</note> himself was not very certain of his own hypotheses. He was someone who began to waver in his claims regarding the positions of *Galileo* <note>original: *Galilaeus*</note> concerning the earth’s movement <note>original: "mobility"</note>, yet he also called them into doubt himself; this is evident enough in his *Apology for Galileo* <note>original: *Apologie*</note>.

But when I happened later upon a work by *Sir Francis Bacon, Baron of Verulam* and *Chancellor of England*, titled *The Great Restoration* <note>original: *Instauratio Magna*; this was Bacon's major project to reorganize and reform the sciences</note> (an admirable work, which I view as nothing less than a brilliant ray of light for a new age of *philosophers* now emerging), I understood that in some specific details of *Campanella*, the solid proofs <note>original: "Demonstrations"</note> required for the truth of things were lacking. Yet it saddened <note>original: "grieved"</note> me again to see that the most noble *Verulam* presented us with a true key to Nature, but did not actually unlock the secrets of Nature. He only showed us by a few examples how they were to be opened, and left the rest to depend on

<summary>The author expresses his realization that while Thomas Campanella was uncertain in his theories, Francis Bacon provided a revolutionary "key" to understanding nature, even though Bacon did not complete the work of uncovering all its secrets himself.</summary>

# The Preface.

...observations and inductions <note>original: "inductions"; the process of drawing general conclusions from specific observations, a core part of the scientific method</note> continued <margin>=</margin> for several ages. Yet I saw, nevertheless, that my hopes were not left completely in suspense: because I perceived my mind so enlightened by the light it received from those various sparks—which have now grown almost into a torch—that some great secrets of *Nature* and very obscure passages of *Scripture* (the meaning of which I did not understand before) were now clear, as if by their own accord, to the great satisfaction of my mind. For now, along with those who have discovered a more solid method of *philosophy* in this age, I saw and rested in these principles:

<margin>-</margin> *I. That the only true, natural, and clear way of philosophy is to derive all things from the senses, reason, and Scripture.*

<margin>-</margin> *II. That the Peripatetic* <note>original: "Peripatetick"; referring to the traditional school of philosophy following the teachings of Aristotle</note> *philosophy is not only deficient in many areas and complicated in many ways—full of twists and turns—but also partly incorrect, so that it is not only useless for Chris-*

<summary>The author explains how his own study of nature and the Bible became clearer by following a new philosophical method based on sensory evidence, reason, and religious text. He concludes that the traditional Aristotelian (Peripatetic) philosophy of the universities is flawed and unhelpful for Christians.</summary>
<keywords>induction, Nature, Scripture, Peripatetic philosophy, Aristotle, scientific method, senses, reason</keywords>

# The Preface.

tians, but also—without correction and perfection—harmful.

III. That <term>philosophy</term> <note>original: "philosophie"</note> may be reformed and perfected by a harmonious organization <note>original: "harmonicall reduction"; the process of bringing complex data back to its fundamental principles</note> of all things that exist and are made, aligning them with <term>senses</term>, <term>reason</term>, and <term>Scripture</term>. This can be done with such evidence and certainty (regarding all things of the greatest importance and necessity) that any mortal man seeing may see, and feeling may feel, the truth scattered everywhere.

Regarding each and every one of these observations, lest we should seem to have merely imagined <note>original: "dreamed somewhat"</note> them, there is more to be said in greater detail.

And for the first point, we establish three principles of philosophy, following **Campanella** <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), an Italian philosopher and theologian who argued that nature is a book through which God reveals himself</note> and his skillful interpreter **Tobias Adami**: *Senses, Reason, and Scripture*. But these must be used so jointly that anyone who does not wish to be left in ignorance or doubt should rely on no one of these without the others; otherwise, it will be a very direct path <note>original: "ready precipice"</note> into error. For the senses, although

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument for a three-fold method of inquiry, explaining how Reason and Scripture must supplement the inherent limitations of the Senses.</meta>

# The Preface.

it <note>Referring to the "sense" or sensory perception mentioned on the previous page.</note> makes an immediate impression upon us of the truth impressed upon things. Yet, because the sense is very often confused—either by an almost infinite number of things and the <margin>G</margin> strange complications of forms <note>original: "formes"; in this context, the physical structures or essential patterns of objects.</note>, or by being wearied and tired (sometimes due to the distance of the objects)—it is consequently dazzled and deceived. *Reason* must necessarily be employed, which may draw similar conclusions about similar things and opposite conclusions about opposite things by observing their proportions <note>original: "proportion"; the logical relationships or ratios between different objects.</note>. In this way, reason can compensate for the failings of the senses and correct their errors.

But then, because many things are remote from both sense and reason (which we cannot in any way reach through our senses, nor yet firmly enough through our reason), we are indebted to the grace of God. He has, *by His Word*, revealed to us even some secrets which it concerns us to know. Therefore, if anyone desires the true knowledge of things,

<summary>The author explains that while the senses provide an initial impression of truth, they are easily deceived and must be corrected by reason. Ultimately, because some truths are beyond both sense and reason, divine revelation through the Bible is necessary for complete understanding.</summary>

<keywords>reason, senses, divine revelation, forms, logic, philosophy of science, truth, scripture</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the "three principles of knowing": Sense, Reason, and Scripture. It explores the pitfalls of relying on only one of these sources of truth in isolation.</meta>

# The Preface.

These three principles of knowledge must, by necessity, be combined. Otherwise, he who follows the guidance of his senses alone will never be any wiser than the common people. He would not be able to conceive of the Moon as being smaller than a star, or the Sun as larger than the Earth, or that the Earth is a sphere and habitable in every part.

<margin>1</margin> On the contrary, if a man contemplates abstract things and consults only with reason without the evidence of his senses, he will be carried away by mere phantasms <note>original: "phantaſmes"; delusions or products of the imagination that have no basis in reality.</note> and will create a new world for himself—much like the Platonic and Aristotelian philosophers, and others. 

Lastly, those who heed only the Scripture and listen to neither sense nor reason are either carried away beyond the world by the loftiness of their own ideas, or else they entangle things they do not understand with a "collier’s faith" <note>original: "Colliers faith"; a term for blind, unthinking faith. It refers to a famous anecdote about a coal miner (collier) who, when asked what he believed, replied simply that he believed whatever the Church believed, without knowing what that was.</note>. Alternatively, by following the literal word, they propose things for themselves to be believed that are utterly absurd and superstitious, such as the

<summary>The author argues that true understanding requires the integration of the senses, reason, and Scripture. Relying on only one leads to scientific ignorance, philosophical delusions, or blind superstition.</summary>

<keywords>epistemology, three principles of knowing, sense perception, reason, Scripture, Platonic philosophy, Aristotelian philosophy, Collier's faith, astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of religious doctrines that ignore the physical senses, specifically targeting the doctrine of transubstantiation. It establishes a hierarchy of knowledge: starting with the senses, moving to reason, and concluding with divine revelation.</meta>

# The Preface.

Roman Catholics <note>original: "Papists"; a derogatory term for Roman Catholics common in the 17th century.</note> do in that most absurd doctrine of transubstantiation <note>original: "transubstantion"; the belief that the bread and wine in the Eucharist become the actual body and blood of Christ.</note> of theirs, and so on. So then, *the principles of knowing must be combined, so that divine revelation may provide us with belief; and reason, understanding, and sense with certainty.* And they must be used in this order (in natural things, I say): we begin with *sense* and end in *revelation* (acting, as it were, as the setting of the seal of God). For by this order, every subsequent degree will
<margin>☞</margin> receive from the previous one both *evidence*, *certainty*, and *correction* <note>original: "Emendation"</note>. For as there is nothing in the
<margin>☞</margin> *understanding* which was not first in the
<margin>☞</margin> *senses*, so there is nothing in belief which was not first in the understanding. For he who believes must know what is appropriate to be believed. Hence the *Scripture* frequently invites us to *hear, see, taste, and consider*; and affirms that faith also comes by hearing. I mentioned
<margin>☞</margin> *certainty* as well. For the closer *reason* is to *sense* (that is, by how...

<summary>The author argues that true knowledge follows a specific order: beginning with the senses, processed by the understanding, and finally confirmed by divine revelation. He asserts that even faith must be rooted in understanding and sensory evidence, as encouraged by the Scriptures.</summary>

<keywords>epistemology, transubstantiation, divine revelation, reason, sensory perception, empiricism, logic, theology</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion on the hierarchy of knowledge, specifically focusing on how sensory evidence, reason, and divine revelation interact to form certain truth. # The Preface.

<margin>☞</margin>
...as by many more experiments of the senses it may be demonstrated) it is so much the more real. Conversely, the further it moves away from sensory experience, the more it consists of empty speculation and mere imagination. However, the more divine revelation can be brought into the realm of human understanding and the evidence of experience, the more strength it gains.

<margin>☞</margin>
I said further that the preceding levels <note>The "degrees" or levels of knowledge: Sense, Reason, and Revelation.</note> were corrected by the subsequent ones, and that is indeed the case. For where the senses fail or make mistakes, they are supported and corrected by reason; and reason is corrected by revelation. For example, when the senses judge the Moon to be larger than *Saturn*, or an oar to appear broken under the water <note>A classic philosophical example of "refraction," where light bending in water tricks the eyes.</note>, etc., reason corrects this by using certain evidence from experience. Similarly, when reason has falsely concluded anything regarding invisible things, it is corrected by divine revelation. Yet that correction <note>original: "emendation"</note>—

<summary>The author explains that knowledge is most "real" when grounded in sensory experience and that a hierarchy exists where reason corrects the senses, and divine revelation corrects reason.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hierarchy of knowledge, specifically how "emendation" (correction) works when a higher form of understanding (like Reason or Faith) corrects a lower one (like Sense or Reason).</meta>

# The Preface.

<margin>—</margin>
[Emen]dation <note>This is the conclusion of the word "emendation" from the previous page.</note> is not violent and does not involve the destruction of the previous principle. Instead, it is gentle, so that the very thing being corrected acknowledges and admits the correction of its own accord and with joy; it soon brings something of its own to the process, by which that same corrected truth may become more apparent. 

For example, Reason brings nothing to correct the senses that is not soon confirmed by various experiments. The sense then confirms for itself that this is so (as the sense of touch teaches us that an oar is not actually broken under water, and the sense of sight confirms this once the oar is drawn out). 

<margin>☞</margin>
Faith presents nothing that is contrary or repugnant to Reason (though it brings that which is beyond and above Reason). 

<margin>☞</margin>
Reason not only yields to these things, being overcome by divine authority, but also finds them to be true in reality. Thus, Reason seeks and finds something of its own which may serve to...

# The Preface.

*...rudiments of the world, and not after Christ. (Galatians 5:8, 1 Peter 2:9, Colossians 2:8.)* <note>The author concludes a sequence of biblical citations arguing that faith should be based on Christ rather than worldly traditions or philosophies.</note>

At that time, everything was full of the noise of disputes—for tricky <note>original: "slippery"</note> questions and an itching desire for controversy are the very soul of Aristotelian philosophy <note>original: "Peripateticisme," referring to the followers of Aristotle</note>—and heresy sprang out of heresy. # The Preface.

...philosophy; and neither Thomas [Aquinas] nor [Duns] Scotus <note>The OCR reads "Thomas Scotus," likely a shorthand or error for the two pillars of Scholasticism, Thomas Aquinas and Duns Scotus.</note>, nor any other man, had any better success. <margin>☛</margin>
Some are afraid that if they should let go of Aristotelian <note>original: "Peripatetick"</note> philosophy, they would have no philosophy at all. This is as if one thought that when Hagar was cast out, there would have been no one left to bless Abraham with offspring <note>original: "issue"</note>! <note>In the Book of Genesis, Hagar was the bondwoman whose departure allowed the focus to shift to Sarah and the promised heir, Isaac.</note> Or that the Israelites would have starved with hunger once they had left the cooking pots <note>original: "flesh pots"</note> of Egypt; or that Moses must necessarily have grown dull-witted <note>original: "blockish"</note> when he was no longer in the company of the wise men of Memphis <note>An ancient capital of Egypt; the author suggests Moses’s wisdom came from God, not Egyptian education.</note>! On the contrary, that promised Grace will finally come; at last, that heavenly Manna will rain down; and finally, we shall be truly taught by God <note>original: "θεοδίδακτοι" (theodidaktoi)</note>, when—turning our ears away from human opinions—we listen to God alone.

<summary>The author argues that abandoning Aristotelian and Scholastic philosophy is not a loss, but a necessary step to receiving true divine wisdom, just as the Israelites had to leave Egypt to receive manna.</summary>

# The Preface.

...speaking through Nature and through His Word. And then, how solid, easy, and delightful all things will be! Since the whole course of *Philosophy* will not consist of opinions, but of truth.

<margin>☞</margin>
But greater care had to be taken regarding *method*: ensuring that all things are delivered to learners in such a way that they find knowledge flowing into them, rather than being stuffed in. This means always beginning with those things that are best known, and ending with things no less certain <note>original: "known"</note> than they are. For why should we think that impossible in *Physics* <note>original: "Phyſicks"; referring to the study of the natural world</note>, which is so excellently achieved in *Mathematics*? In that field, all *demonstrations* (*N.W.*) <note>This abbreviation likely refers to a specific author or a citation style used in the original text.</note> are brought clearly to sight. Everything is constructed from preceding facts that are better known—always beginning with such common principles that the unskillful find them tedious to hear—in such an order that the intermediate steps are never skipped, nor is a place given to anything more unknown than the starting point,

# The Preface.

Consequently, it follows that you must necessarily agree with the final conclusion as much as the first principle. And truly, there is reason to wonder why the same approach has not yet been attempted in *Metaphysics*, *Physics*, and *Theology* (since *Ethics* and *Politics* deal with more unpredictable <note>original: "contingent"; matters that depend on human choice or chance rather than fixed natural laws</note> things).

I am well aware that there is more obvious evidence in *numbers*, *measures*, and *weights* than in *qualities*, through which *Nature* exerts its power in a hidden way. However, I will not say that there is greater certainty in mathematics than in the study of nature, seeing as all things are done according to the highest reason, in a continuous order, and as if by an eternal law. 

Even in *Mathematics*, not everything is equally clear at first; yet they are tested in various ways until they can be reduced to direct observation <note>original: αὐτοψία (autopsia); literally "seeing for oneself" or an eyewitness account</note>, as I said before, and presented scientifically. *For in philosophical matters, he says nothing who proves nothing; and he proves nothing who does*

# The Preface.

And now I urge you, let this be our mission: that the schools may stop merely *persuading* and begin to *demonstrate*; stop *disputing* and begin to *examine* <note>original: "speculate"; in this context, it refers to deep observation or contemplation of truths rather than mere guessing</note>; and finally, stop *believing* and begin to *know*. 

For that *Aristotelian* maxim, "A learner must believe" <note>original Latin: *Discentem oportet credere*</note>, is as tyrannical as it is dangerous; as is that same *Pythagorean* <term>he himself said it</term> <note>original Greek: αυτὸς ἔφα (*autos epha*); original Latin: *ipse dixit*. Let no one be compelled to swear by his master’s words, but let the things themselves convince the intellect. Nor should any master be given any more credit than he can actually demonstrate, in very deed, that he deserves. 

For in a free republic <note>original: "Common-wealth"</note> there ought to be no *Kings*, but *Leaders* <note>original: "Dukes"; from the Latin *dux*, meaning a leader or guide</note> or *Generals*; no *Dictators*, but *Consuls*. And those who profess the art of instructing men are the fathers of humanity, not the sculptors of statues. Oh, when shall we see 그날!—that day when all things which ought to be known shall present themselves so clearly to our minds...

Why shouldn't these things be considered new inventions? *For instance, that threefold system <note>original: "ternarie"</note> of principles so clearly demonstrated from Scripture, Reason, and Sense? # The Preface.

There is so much light in this method of physics <note>original: "Physicks"</note> presented here that very little room is left for doubts or debates. Consequently, it helps to resolve the controversies among various authors. The opinions of everyone—including whatever truth *Aristotle* held, or what *Galen*, the *Chemists* <note>original: "Chymicks"</note>, *Campanella*, and *Francis Bacon* <note>original: "Verulamius"</note> reasonably argue against him—are brought into harmony. 

This may be clearly seen in the example of the principles they believe make up physical bodies. *Aristotle* claimed these were the four elements, while the *Alchemists* <note>original: "Syagyricks," likely a misspelling of Spagyrists</note> claimed they were *Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury* <note>original: "☿"</note>. Furthermore, this method opens a new path, not only toward understanding the arts and human inventions but also toward increasing them—which could never happen unless the foundations of truth had been discovered.

<meta>This page begins the main text with the "Prolegomena," or introduction, following the Table of Contents and Errata. It introduces the fundamental definition of "Physick" (Physics or Natural Philosophy).</meta>

# INTRODUCTION <note>original: PROLEGOMENA; from Greek, meaning preliminary remarks or a formal introduction to a subject.</note>

*Concerning the nature, foundation, and use of Physics <note>original: Physick</note>.*

I *Physics is the scientific <note>original: Scientiall</note> knowledge of natural things.*

II *A thing is natural if it exists by Nature, and not by human craft <note>original: Art</note>.*

**F**or whatever this visible world contains comes either from Nature or from craft <note>original: Art</note>. Those things come from Nature which God brought forth in the beginning, or which are produced to this very day by a power <note>original: virtue</note> implanted in them. These include the heavens, the earth, the sea, rivers, mountains, stones, metals, herbs, living creatures, and so on. Those things come from craft which humans have shaped by imposing a new form upon natural things; these include cities, houses, ponds, channels, statues, coins, garments, books, and so on—that is, things produced by the work of human ingenuity and hands. Physics <note>original: Physicks</note> has nothing to do with these things; they are assigned to the study of the arts.

<summary>This page defines physics (natural philosophy) as the scientific study of the natural world, distinguishing between "natural" things created by God and "artificial" things created by human ingenuity.</summary>
<keywords>Physics, natural philosophy, Nature vs. Art, scientific knowledge, creation, human ingenuity, natural things, artificial things</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Introduction" (Prolegomena), building on the distinction between Nature and Art (craft/human skill). It argues that the study of Physics (Natural Philosophy) is the essential foundation for all other branches of knowledge.</meta>

Now, seeing that *nature* exists before *craft* <note>original: art</note>, *craft* imitates nothing but *nature*; it does nothing except by the strength of *nature*. It necessarily follows that *nature* must be laid as the foundation for the arts, and that *nature* must first be known by those who study the arts—specifically, what things exist and by what power <note>original: virtue</note> nature operates everywhere. For once this is known, the secrets of all arts open of their own accord. Without this knowledge, everything in the arts and practical matters <note>original: prudentials</note> will be blind, dumb, and maimed. Therefore, *Physics* <note>original: Physick</note> is so necessary to be studied before the *mathematical*, *logical*, and even the *practical arts* <note>original: prudential arts; likely referring to ethics and politics</note>, that those who do otherwise might be thought to build castles in the air.

III *The nature of things is the law of being born and of dying, of acting <note>original: operating</note> and of ceasing, which* God *the Great Craftsman <note>original: Workmaster</note> has laid upon all things that exist.*

For all things are born and die; all things perform some action <note>original: operate somewhat</note>, and all things cease again, in an order and manner proper to every creature. Because this order and manner is based on the most excellent reason, it could not have been arranged except by supreme wisdom, inasmuch as it is found to be constantly imposed upon things as a law.

<summary>This page argues that because human craft merely imitates nature, the study of Physics is the essential foundation for all other sciences and arts. It also defines the "nature of things" as the divine laws of birth, death, and action imposed by God.</summary>
<keywords>Nature vs. Art, Physics as foundation, God the Workmaster, laws of nature, natural order, methodology of science</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Introduction" (Prolegomena). The previous page ended with a definition of Section III, explaining that nature is a law of birth, death, and action imposed by God upon all creatures. This page concludes that definition and begins explaining the proper method for studying the natural world.</meta>

Now, it took the name of "nature" from the first stage <note>original: degree</note> of change <note>original: mutation</note> of everything, which is: to be born.

*IV The knowledge of nature is to be obtained by searching into Nature itself.*

By searching, I say. For no one should spend his time in physics <note>original: Physicks; here meaning "natural philosophy," the study of the physical world</note> just to have his mind occupied with another person’s opinions <note>original: conceits</note>. Instead, he should apply himself to a thorough and intimate knowledge of things. Otherwise, the intellect will not be enlightened <note>original: illustrated</note> by the nature of things, but instead darkened <note>original: obumbrated</note> by speculating on imaginary notions <note>original: phantasms</note> in natural things. Therefore, we are to seek guides who may make us students <note>original: scholars</note>, not of themselves, but of nature, and who show <note>original: exhibit</note> to us not their own foolish <note>original: fond</note> reasoning, but nature itself.

*V To search Nature is to contemplate how and why everything in nature is done.*

To contemplate, I say. For just as we do not see the sun except by looking at the sun, so we do not learn nature except by looking into nature. This is what the Scripture counsels us: 
> *Ask the beasts, and they shall teach you; and the birds <note>original: fouls</note> of the air, and they shall tell you; or talk with the earth, and it shall an-* <note>The text continues on the next page with the word "answer."</note>

<summary>This page concludes the definition of nature as the process of being born and argues that true scientific knowledge must come from direct observation rather than relying on the theories of others. It invokes the biblical advice from the Book of Job to learn directly from the earth and its creatures.</summary>
<keywords>nature, physics, natural philosophy, empiricism, Job, observation, contemplation</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the method of studying nature, specifically through direct observation ("ocular demonstration"). It concludes the biblical quotation from Job and introduces the sixth aphorism regarding the relationship between simple and complex organisms.</meta>

...answer you, and the fish of the sea shall declare it to you. (*Job 12:7*) Therefore, students of natural philosophy <note>original: Philoſophy; the study of nature and the physical universe before the modern term "science" was used</note> cannot be more successfully and easily instructed than if they are taught by direct observation <note>original: ocular demonſtration</note>, wherever it can be found. I say we must contemplate everything, so that we may sift out the reasons and causes of all things everywhere. For it is certain that nature does nothing in vain; even in things of the least importance <note>original: moment</note>, indeed, sometimes much wisdom lies stored up in the very smallest things. And what is more, we cannot attain the knowledge of great things except through the knowledge of lesser things, as the following aphorism will teach us.

<margin>☛</margin>
### VI *Nature unfolds herself in the smallest things, and conceals <note>original: wraps up</note> herself in the greatest things.*

That is, in the more complex <note>original: excellent</note> creatures, many things are wound up and woven together with such hidden <note>original: occult</note> skill <note>original: artifice</note> that neither the beginnings nor the endings of actions and external qualities <note>original: accidents; in philosophy, an 'accident' is a property that does not change the essence of a thing</note> can easily be discerned. But in all simpler <note>original: courſer</note> creatures, all things are clearly manifest; this is the reason why the nature of complex things <note>original: compounds</note> cannot be known unless the nature of simple elements <note>original: simples</note> is...

<summary>This page argues that natural philosophy should be learned through direct observation ("ocular demonstration") rather than just theory. It introduces the principle that nature's complex mechanisms are best understood by first studying simpler, smaller organisms where those processes are more visible.</summary>
<keywords>natural philosophy, Job 12:7, ocular demonstration, observation, aphorism, compounds, simples, complexity</keywords>

...must be known first. Consequently, we are to begin with these observations and proceed by degrees from simpler things to the more complex <note>original: compound</note>. We shall see that the Creator himself followed this very order in producing and weaving together the nature of things.

### VII *We are to study natural philosophy <note>original: naturall Phyloſophie; the study of nature and the physical universe before the modern term "science" was used</note> by the guide of the senses and the light of Scripture.*

For sensory perception <note>original: sense</note> is the beginning not only of knowledge, but of certainty and wisdom. Just as nothing exists in the intellect that was not first in the senses, so if there is anything obscure or doubtful in the mind, we must return to the senses for evidence and certainty. But wherever sensory perception or reason fails (as in things distant in either space or time), we are indebted to the grace of God for deigning to reveal many things to us that exceed the limits of sense and reason—for example, the first creation <note>original: production</note> of the world and the structure <note>original: constitution</note> of invisible things.

Anyone who neglects either of these principles is easily entangled in errors; for the more imagination a thing has, the more vanity <note>original: vanity; here meaning emptiness, futility, or lack of substance</note> it [has...]

### CHAPTER I.

#### An Idea of the World to be created and created. <note>In this context, "Idea" refers to the divine blueprint or conceptual plan of the world, both as it was conceived and as it was realized.</note>

**T**he eternal Deity, our God who is to be adored, after the infinite glories which he enjoys in his immense eternity, was inclined <note>original: "propense"</note> by his exceeding goodness to communicate himself outside of himself. Through his exceeding *Wisdom*, he saw that his invisible nature might be expressed by certain visible images. To execute that plan, having his *Omnipotence* at hand, he decreed not to deny existence <note>original: "envy entity"; meaning to begrudge or withhold the state of being</note> to those things in which he might be expressed, and in which his *Power*, *Wisdom*, and *Goodness* might be revealed.

Therefore, he produced intelligent creatures by whom he might be known and praised: *Angels* and *men*. Both were made after his own image, but the first are pure minds, while the others are clothed with bodies. For these, he built a dwelling place—and, as it were, a school of wisdom—this universal World, along with other creatures of an inferior degree that are almost infinite. All and every one of these cry out in their own way, *“He made us, and not we ourselves”* <note>A reference to Psalm 100:3</note>. Now then, we proceed to unfold in what

> VI *And God saw the light, that it was good (verse 4).*

*...called the light Day, and the darkness he called Night, and the evening and the morning were the first day (verse 5).* That is, that light, once it had rotated and circled the World, created day and night through that motion.

The second effect of light came from its heat; specifically, whichever way the light passed, it thinned <note>original: "rarified"</note> and purified the matter, but caused it to thicken <note>original: "condensed"</note> on both sides, both above and below. "Let there be a firmament" means: let that light expand the matter, and let the thicker parts of the matter—melting and retreating from the light—form waters on both sides. Above, they form the boundary <note>original: "term"</note> of the visible World; below, they are the material suited to produce other creatures, beneath which the earth gathered like thick sediment <note>original: "dregs"</note>. This was completed on the second day.

Finally, it is due to His power that such an unchanging durability is granted to the universe, even amidst the constant changes of individual parts, so that the world is, in a sense, eternal.

In this way, the things we have briefly pointed out from the writings of *Moses* may be further clarified by the constant testimony of Scripture, reason, and the physical senses, creating a path to understand how one thing follows from another.

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding the interpretation of the first chapter of Genesis, specifically defining the "heaven" mentioned in the first verse.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<meta>Catchword: III. And</meta>

...of those words of *Moses*: *In the beginning God created the heaven.* This suggests that the invisible world was the beginning of the works of God—that is, the "heaven of heavens" along with the Angels. It appears plain that by this "heaven," we should understand the heaven of heavens and the invisible, or angelic, world.

I. *From Scripture*, which (1) mentions the heaven of heavens everywhere, but describes their production nowhere else, unless it is here. (2) *Moses* testifies that the invisible <note>While the text says "invisible," the author is likely referring to the physical expanse or firmament created on the second day, distinguishing it from the "heaven of heavens."</note> heavens were stretched out on the second day and adorned with stars on the fourth day. Therefore, another heaven must necessarily be understood in this first verse; namely, a heaven that was finished in the same moment. For the particle *<term>however</term>* <note>original Latin: "autem"</note> implies this: he created the heavens and the earth; *<term>but the earth</term>* <note>original Latin: "terra autem"</note> was without form, etc.

III. This reason proves the same: those things which are made by God are made in order. Now, an orderly process in operation is one where progress is made from simpler things to complex <note>original: "compound"</note> things. Therefore, just as man, the most complex creature, was produced last, the most simple and immaterial creatures (*Heaven and the Angels*) were produced first of all.

III *And what more could we desire?*

God himself testifies expressly that when he made the earth, the Angels stood by him as spectators. For so he says <note>original OCR: "faith"; likely "saith" (says)</note> to *Job*: *Where were you when I founded the earth, when the morning stars sang together, and all the sons of God shouted?* (*Job* 38:4, 7). He calls the Angels "morning stars" because they were a spiritual light <note>original: "beam"</note> that had newly risen; he calls them "sons of God" because they were made in the image of God. 

Therefore, when we hear that the earth was founded on the first day, it must be that the Angels were created <note>original: "produced"</note> before the earth. And if the Angels existed, then certainly the dwellings of the Angels—the heaven of heavens—existed too. This realm was created in full perfection, with all its inhabitants <note>original: "hosts"</note>, in a single moment. This is why *Moses* says no more about that heaven, but moves on to the forming of the earth—that is, the visible world—describing how the Creator took six days to organize <note>original: "digest"</note> it, just as we will now also move on <note>original: "descend"</note>.

<summary>The author uses the Book of Job to argue that Angels and the "heaven of heavens" were created before the physical Earth, explaining why Moses focused his narrative on the six-day creation of the visible world.</summary>
<keywords>Angels, Job, creation, morning stars, sons of God, heaven of heavens, Moses, visible world, spiritual light</keywords>

## CHAP. II.

### *Of the visible Principles of the World: matter, spirit, and light.*

**W**e have seen, God showing us, how the World arose out of the abyss of nothingness <note>original: "nihilitie"</note>; let us now see how it stands, so that by seeing we may learn to see, and by feeling to feel, the very truth of things.

And here, three principles of visible things are presented to us: matter, spirit, and light. They were produced on the first day as three great but raw <note>original: "rude"</note> masses, and out of those, worked in various ways, came forth various kinds of creatures. *Therefore, we must inquire further whether these three principles of all bodies have a true being and still exist*, lest any error be perhaps committed at the very beginning through any negligence whatsoever. But now, seeing that no one any longer doubts matter and light, this only remains to be proved: *that by that spirit which hovered upon the face of the waters, a certain universal spirit of the world is to be understood*, which puts life and vigor into all things created.

<term>nihilitie</term> — Nothingness; the state of being nothing.
<term>matter</term> — The physical substance that makes up the universe.
<term>spirit</term> — In this context, a vital, animating force rather than a ghostly apparition.
<term>light</term> — Considered here as a physical "principle" or building block of the world.
<term>universal spirit</term> — A concept in early modern natural philosophy suggesting a subtle substance that permeates the universe to give it life and movement.

<summary>The author identifies matter, spirit, and light as the three fundamental "principles" created on the first day, arguing that the "spirit" mentioned in Genesis is a universal life-force that animates all physical things.</summary>
<keywords>creation, principles of nature, matter, spirit, light, universal spirit, Genesis, natural philosophy</keywords>

For the newness of this opinion in physics <note>original: "phyſicks"; here referring to natural philosophy or the study of nature</note>, and because theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note> unanimously interpret that passage as referring to the person of the Holy Spirit, there is cause for doubt. But **Chrysostom** <note>Saint John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential Early Church Father</note>, (as **Aslacus** <note>Conrad Aslacus (1564–1624), a Danish theologian and astronomer</note> cites him) and **Daneau** <note>Lambert Daneau (c. 1530–1595), a French Reformed theologian</note> acknowledge that in this place a created spirit, which is as it were the soul of the world <note>original: "anima mundi"</note>, is more rightly to be understood; and it is proven strongly.

I. By **Scripture**, which testifies that a certain power <note>original: "vertue"</note> was infused by God through the whole world, sustaining and giving life <note>original: "quickening"</note> to all things, and working all things in all things; which he calls both a **spirit** and a **soul**, and sometimes the **spirit of God**, sometimes the **spirit of the creatures**.

For example (Psalm 104, verses 29–30), **David** says thus: *when you take away their spirit (that is, the spirit of living creatures, and of plants) they die, and return to their dust: but when you send forth your spirit (that is, the Spirit of God) again, they are recreated, and the face of the earth is renewed*. Furthermore, **Job** (27:3) says thus: *as long as my soul shall be in me, and the spirit of God in my nostrils;* see how the soul of man and the spirit of God are put for the same thing! This passage, when compared with the saying of **Elihu**, *the spirit of God has*

Where did these get life, I ask you, if not from that soul spread throughout the world? We find by experience that bread, wine, water, and even air are vital <note>original: "vitall"; essential for life</note> to those who feed upon them. But from where do they get that vital force, I ask, if not from this widespread soul? Now, if a certain spirit is spread in that manner through all things, it follows necessarily that it was created in the beginning in its entire mass <note>original: "masse"; bulk or whole substance</note>, just as matter and light were first produced in their great and unorganized state. Consequently, there was no need for anything to be created afterward; instead, all things are composed of those three elements and differentiated by their forms. God suggested this in *Isaiah* <note>original: "Esay"</note> 42:5, where, in declaring himself the Creator of all things, he divides them into three parts: namely, the *heavens* (that is, light), the *earth* (that is, matter), and a *life-giving <note>original: "quickning"</note> spirit*. The same occurs in *Zechariah* <note>original: "Zackary"</note> 12:1. Let us therefore hereafter avoid such a great absurdity (if not a blasphemy) as to rank the person of the Holy Spirit <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note> among created things.

*For the Analogy <note>The principle of interpreting Scripture in harmony with established doctrine</note> of our Faith teaches us to believe that the production of matter out of nothing is a work of God's omnipotence and is attributed to the Father; that the production of light (by which the world received splendor and order) is a work of wisdom attributed to the Son (*John 1:3-4*); and lastly, that the power <note>original: "virtue"</note> infused into the creatures is a work of his goodness, which is attributed to the Holy Spirit (*Psalm 143:10*). First there is matter, then a spirit to shape the matter, and lastly light or heat to activate <note>original: "in-actuate," meaning to bring into action or give form to</note> the matter under the direction of the spirit. ### The definitions of the principles.

*Matter is a physical <note>original: "corpulent"</note> substance, which is naturally unformed <note>original: "rude"</note> and dark; it is what constitutes bodies.*

*Spirit is a fine <note>original: "subtile"</note> substance, which is naturally living, invisible, and unable to be perceived by the senses <note>original: "insensible"</note>; it dwells and grows within bodies.*

*Light is a substance that is naturally visible, movable, and bright <note>original: "lucid"</note>; it penetrates matter and prepares it to receive the spirits, and so gives shape to bodies.*

Note also that matter is the first entity <note>original: "entitie," referring to the basic substance or essence of a thing</note> in the world; spirit is the first living thing; and light is the first moving thing. Thus, every physical body in the world consists of matter, through light, and within the spirit. This arrangement is intended to be an image of God, *from whom, through whom, and in whom are all things, blessed forever, Amen. (Romans 11:36)*.

### Concerning the nature of matter.

As someone truly said, no amount of effort <note>original: "diligence"</note> is too great when searching for the origins of things. For once the basic principles are correctly established, an endless number of conclusions will naturally follow, and the science will expand without limit <note>original: "in infinitum"</note>; as the creation of the world also demonstrates. After God produced these principles on the first day and combined them with the most excellent skill, He later caused such a great variety of things to emerge from them that both

Let the following aphorisms <note>original: "Aphorisms"; concise statements of scientific principles or general truths</note> concern the nature of matter.

I. ***The first matter of the world was a vapor or a fume.***

<margin>}</margin>What else could Moses' description mean when he calls it *earth, waters, the deep, darkness, a thing void, and without form*? This is also supported by reason: since the smaller bodies of the world—clouds, water, stones, metals, and everything growing on the earth—are made of condensed <note>original: "coagulated"</note> vapors (as will be shown most clearly later), why wouldn't the whole world be made of the same? Certainly, the matter of the whole can be nothing other than what is found to be the matter of its parts.

II. ***The first matter of the world was a chaos of dispersed atoms, which did not cling together in any part.***

This is proved:
1. By **reason**: for if they had clung together in any way, they would have had a specific form; but they did not, for it was *Tohu vabohu* <note>original Hebrew: "Tohu vabohu"; a phrase from Genesis 1:2 meaning "without form and void"</note>, a thing without form and empty.

Atoms. For what is dust but earth reduced into atoms? What is the air itself but a very fine breakdown <note>original: "comminution"; the act of reducing something to small particles</note> of drops of water, unperceivable by the senses? And this also shows that those threads or hairs are made of atoms, as if glued together, so that when they are dried they may be powdered <note>original: "pouldred"</note>. ### IV. *The matter is of itself invisible, and therefore dark.*

For darkness is seen in the same way when the eyes are shut as when they are open; that is, they see nothing at all. <margin>☞</margin> And this is what *Moses* says: *and darkness was upon the face of the Abyss.* <note>Genesis 1:2.</note>

### V. *The matter is of itself without form, yet it is suited to be extended, contracted, divided, united, and to receive every form and figure, just as wax is suited to receive every seal.*

For we have shown that all the bodies of the world are made of these atoms, and are broken down <note>original: "resolved"</note> into them; therefore, they are nothing else but matter clothed with various forms. This the chemists <note>original: "Chymicks"</note> demonstrate to the eye, transforming some dust at one time into liquid, at another time into a vapor, and at another time into a stone, etc.

### VI. *The matter is eternal in its duration through all forms, so that nothing of it can perish.*

For in very deed, from the making of the world until now, not so much as one crumb of matter has perished, nor has one increased. For although bodies are generated, and do perish—

### VII. *From this matter, the whole world is material and physical <note>original: "corporeall"</note>, and is called such.*

For all the bodies of the world, even the most subtle and the most luminous, are nothing but form that is partly solidified <note>original: "coagulated"</note> and partly purified <note>original: "refined"</note>. Now, the manner in which it is solidified or purified shall appear in what follows.

### *On the nature of the Spirit, or Soul of the World.*

**T**he spirit of the world is life itself, infused into the world to work all things in all. For whatever any creature does or undergoes <note>original: "suffers"</note>, it does or undergoes by the power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of this spirit, for it is given to it.

### I. To inhabit the matter.
For just as in the beginning it moved itself upon the waters <note>A reference to the Creation account in Genesis</note>, it still exists nowhere except within matter; especially in liquid and refined <note>original: "subtile"</note> matter. Therefore, within the body of a living creature, those most refined, blood-based <note>original: "sanguine"</note> vapors—which are like flames and serve as the vehicle <note>original: "charriot"</note> of life—are called "spirits." And chemists <note>original: "Chymicks"</note>, who extract a spirit from herbs, metals, or stones that looks like a small amount of water, call it the *Quintessence* <note>The "fifth essence," considered by alchemists to be the purest and most essential part of a substance</note>, because it is a more refined substance than all the four elements. However, this is not the water itself, as a physical substance, but rather that living power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of the creature from which it was extracted, living within it. Since this power cannot be completely separated from matter, it is preserved in that refined physical form. For how firmly the spirit remains attached to <note>original: "inhæres"</note> the matter will be taught toward the end. <margin>Chapters 9 and 10.</margin>

### II. To move or agitate itself through the whole matter to preserve it.
<margin>1</margin> From this, it follows that no *vacuum* <note>original: "vacuum," meaning an empty space containing no matter</note> can exist in the world. For all bodies—even the most refined (such as water, air, and the sky)—being filled with this spirit, thrive on contact and connection <note>original: "contiguity and continuity"</note>. For just as a living creature resists being cut, so also water, air, and indeed the world itself

<vocab>Quintessence, Chemists, vacuum, spirit, matter, vehicle, attached</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that the "Spirit of the World" resides within refined matter, such as the vapors of the blood or chemical quintessences, and argues that this spirit prevents the existence of a vacuum by maintaining the connection between all things.</summary>
<keywords>Spirit of the World, Quintessence, alchemy, vacuum, physiology, continuity of matter, elements</keywords>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<meta>catchword: is</meta>

itself; because of that universal spirit uniting all things within it. This spirit also makes matter close up again when a separation is made—as can be seen in the wounds of living creatures, in the cutting of water, or in the parting of the air. 

2. Every creature decays <note>original: "putrifies"</note> when that spirit is taken away (for example, if you extract the spirit of wine <note>alcohol or ethanol</note> from wine, or allow the spirit to evaporate out of a herb, etc.). However, it is preserved—and even improved—if the spirit is kept. For example, wine kept in a solid vessel under the earth or water, even for a hundred years, grows <margin>3.</margin> richer still. The spirit stirs and moves itself within the wine, and by that means, continually shapes the matter more and more, purifying it from impurities <note>original: "crudities," referring to raw, undigested, or unrefined matter</note>.

### III. *To preserve the specific designs <note>original: "Ideas"</note> or forms of things.*

For one and the same spirit of the universe is afterwards divided <note>original: "diduced"</note> into many individual types by the command of God. So it is that there is <margin>3.</margin> one spirit of water, another spirit of earth, another of metals, another of plants, another of living creatures, and so on; and within every kind, there are several distinct species. Now, this ensures that from the seed of wheat, a bean does not grow—much less a walnut, or a bird, etc.

This process comes from the spirit of the wheat, which, being contained <note>original: "included"</note> within the seed, forms for itself a body according to its nature. From this same spirit comes the maintenance of the boundaries of nature; for example, it ensures that a horse does not grow to the size of a mountain, nor remains as small as a cat.

### IV. To form for itself bodies for the use of future functions <note>original: "operations"</note>.

For example, when the spirit of a dog is contained within its seed and begins to form its young, it does not create wings, or fins, or hands, etc., because it does not need those members. Instead, it forms four feet and other body parts in a way that makes them fit for the use for which they are intended. 

Because some dogs are for pleasure, others are to guard the house or flocks, and others are for hunting—whether for hares, wild boars, or waterfowl, etc. (specifically, according to how the Creator mingled the spirits of living creatures so that they would have <term>Sympathy</term> <note>natural attraction or harmony</note> or <term>Antipathy</term> <note>natural repulsion or hostility</note> toward one another)—every creature's own spirit forms a body fit for its purpose. Therefore, as the learned believe, the use of every creature may be understood simply by looking at it, because every creature carries its own <term>signature</term> <note>the "Doctrine of Signatures" was the belief that a creature's physical appearance revealed its inner purpose or medicinal use</note> upon it.

### *Of the nature of light.*

<margin>Ꜫ</margin>
I. **THE** first light was nothing else but brightness, or a great flame, sent into the dark matter to make it visible and capable of being divided into specific forms.

In the original language <note>referring to Hebrew</note>, light and fire have the same name, *Or* and *Or* <note>original: אור and אור. While both are spelled the same, one refers to "light" and the other can refer to "flame" or "fire"</note>. This is also the origin of the Latin word *uro* <note>original: "URO", meaning "I burn"</note>. Truly, the light of heaven both shines and burns, or provides heat.

II. God put into the light a threefold power <note>original: "vertue"</note>: 
1. The power of spreading itself in every direction and illuminating all things. 
2. The power of moving the matter it touches by burning and inflaming it. 
3. The power of heating, and thereby <term>rarifying</term> <note>making a substance less dense or more "thin"</note> and <term>attenuating</term> <note>refining or thinning out</note> the matter.

Our earthly fire does all these things as well, because it is nothing more than light kindled in the lower <note>original: "inferiour"</note> matter.

III. But since that light could not extend its motion only upwards and downwards (as it would have immediately reached a boundary), it moved itself—and continues to move—in a circle. This was the beginning of days.

IV. Because the matter above was thinned out <note>original: "rarified"</note> when heat was raised by the motion of the light, the heavier <note>original: "grosser"</note> parts of the matter were forced to fall downward and to <term>conglobate</term> <note>to gather together into a sphere or ball-shaped mass</note> themselves in the middle of the Universe. This was the beginning of the earth and the water.

<summary>This page describes light as the primary creative force that organized dark matter into form, explaining its properties of illumination, motion, and heat. The author argues that the circular motion of light created time (days), while the heating of matter caused heavier elements to sink and form the earth and sea.</summary>
<keywords>cosmology, creation, light, fire, Hebrew etymology, rarification, conglobation, elements, motion of light</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of light’s role in creation, explaining how its threefold nature—light, motion, and heat—creates the fundamental opposites of the physical world and sustains all life and form.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>

V. Therefore, the *light*—through *this* threefold *power* <note>original: "vertue"</note> of *light, motion, and heat*—introduced opposites <note>original: "contrariety"</note> into the world.

For *darkness* was the opposite of *light*; *rest* the opposite of *motion*; and *cold* the opposite of *heat*. From these came other opposites as well: *moist* and *dry*, *thin* and *thick*, *heavy* and *light*, etc., which are discussed in *Chapter 4*.

VI. *Therefore, the arrangement and beauty* <note>original: "disposition and adorning"</note> *of the whole world come from the light.*

For light is the only source <note>original: "fountain"</note> of visibility, motion, and heat. If you take light out of the world, all things will return to a state of *Chaos* <note>the primordial, unformed state of the universe before creation</note>. Consider how all things lose their colors and their shapes at night when the sun is gone; likewise, living creatures and plants die in winter because the sun's influence <note>original: "operation"</note> is not strong enough, and the earth and water do nothing but freeze. What do you think would happen if the lights <note>original: "luminaries"</note> of heaven were extinguished entirely? Therefore, all *things throughout the visible world exist and are made of matter, within the spirit, but by the means of fire or light.*

<summary>This page explains how light acts as the primary organizing force of the universe, creating the fundamental opposites (like heat and cold) that define physical matter. It argues that without light, the world would lose its form and life, returning to a state of chaotic darkness.</summary>

<keywords>light, motion, heat, opposites, Chaos, creation, matter, spirit, physics, cosmology</keywords>

## CHAPTER III.

### Concerning the Motion of Things.

**T**he principles of things being established <note>original: "constituted"</note>, we are to examine the common attributes <term>original: "accidents"; in philosophy, an "accident" is a property or quality of a thing that is not essential to its nature, such as its location or color</term> of things: which are *Motion*, *Quality*, and *Change* <note>original: "Mutation"</note>.

For out of the coming together <note>original: "congresse"</note> of the principles of the World, motion first arose; out of motion, came quality; and out of quality, in turn, came various changes of things. These three have existed in all created things until now, as will be shown.

<margin> ❧ </margin>
*I. Motion is an attribute of a body, by which it is transferred from place to place.*

The doctrine of natural motions—how many there are and how they occur—is the key to understanding all natural actions; and therefore, it must be observed most diligently.

*II. Motion was given to things for the sake of creation <note>original: "generation"</note>, action, and time.*

<margin> ❧ </margin>
For creation: because nothing could be produced without composition; nor composed without coming together; nor could things come together without motion.

<summary>This page defines motion as a fundamental property of matter that arises from the interaction of basic principles. It argues that motion is the essential "key" to understanding nature, as it is required for the creation and composition of all physical things.</summary>

<keywords>motion, quality, change, mutation, accidents, natural philosophy, generation, composition</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the three reasons why motion was given to things (creation, action, and time), specifically elaborating on action and time before categorizing the types of motion.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>

For actions: because there could be no actions without motion.

For time: so that it might be the measure of the duration of things. For if you take the sun and the stars out of the world, nothing can be known—the what, where, or when. All things will be blind, dumb, and deaf.

III. *Motion is either simple or compound.*

IV. *Simple motion belongs either to spirit, to light, or to matter.*

V. *The motion of the spirit is called agitation, by which the spirit moves itself within matter, seeking to give it form <term>original: "inform"; in early modern philosophy, this meant to provide a specific shape, structure, or essence to "unformed" matter</term>.*

For the living spirit would not be alive if it should cease to move itself and strive to master <note>original: "subdue"</note> matter in any way whatsoever. This motion is the beginning of the creation <note>original: "generation"</note> and the decay <note>original: "corruption"</note> of things. For the spirit in every thing (in flesh, an apple, a grain, wood, etc.) does, by moving itself, soften the parts so that it may either receive new life, or it may escape, and the thing will rot <note>original: "putrefy"</note>.

VI. *The motion of light is called diffusion, by which light and heat spread <note>original: "diffuse"</note> themselves into all parts.*

For fire would not be fire, nor heat be heat, if it should cease to spread itself and melt <note>original: "liquefy"</note>...

<summary>This page explains how motion is essential for measuring time and enabling physical actions. It introduces a hierarchy of motion, defining "agitation" as the spirit's way of shaping matter and "diffusion" as the way light and heat spread through the world.</summary>

<keywords>motion, simple motion, compound motion, spirit, light, matter, agitation, diffusion, generation, corruption, time</keywords>

<page-num>(40)</page-num>
<sig>VII</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

...the matter. And *from this motion of the fire, all the motion of the matter draws its origin*: as the experience of the senses testifies. For gross <note>original: "groffe"; in this context, meaning dense, solid, or heavy</note> and cold things—such as wood, a stone, ice, etc.—lack motion in themselves; however, when fire is applied to them, they immediately obtain it, as can be demonstrated to the eye.

Imagine a kettle full of water with wood placed underneath it; behold, all is quiet! But kindle the wood, and you shall presently see motion: first in the wood—flame, smoke, and the coals breaking apart; soon after in the water—first evaporating, then swirling around, and finally boiling and "galloping" <note>original: "galloping"; a vivid historical description of water boiling vigorously</note>. But remove the fire again, and all the motion will cease again, little by little. 

<margin>}</margin>Similarly, in a living body (an animal), if you take away heat, not only will motion cease, but also mobility will cease as well, as the limbs become stiff. Furthermore, although there are various motions in things, the origin is everywhere the same: heat or fire. When it is contained within the world, it moves circularly; when kindled in the air, it darts this way or that, depending on how the matter is arranged or how the wind blows; and when contained within a living creature, it is driven this way or that by the strength of the <term>fantasy</term> <note>original: "phantaſie"; in early modern psychology, this was the mental faculty that translated sensory perceptions into internal images and prompted the body to move</note>.

It concludes that while motion manifests in different ways—such as the circular movement of the heavens or the voluntary movement of animals—its fundamental driver remains heat.</summary>

### VII The motion of matter is eightfold:
### expansion, contraction, aggregation, sympathy,
### continuity, impulsion, libration, and liberty.

Of these, the first two come directly from fire; the following four from other bodies; and the last two from the matter itself, <margin>3</margin>but through the mediation of the spirit of the universe. If this seems difficult to understand, it will soon appear plain through examples.

### VIII The motion of expansion is that
### by which matter, being rarified with heat, 
<margin>3</margin>### expands itself of its own accord, seeking larger room.

For it is not possible for matter, once it has been rarified <note>original: "rarified"; to become less dense, the opposite of condensation</note>, to be contained within the same space. Instead, one part pushes another so that they may stretch themselves out and gather into a larger sphere. You can see an example of this if you drop a few drops of water into a pig’s bladder and, after <margin>3</margin>tying its neck, lay it over a furnace. The bladder will be stretched out and will swell because the water, being turned into vapor by the heat, seeks more room.

### IX The motion of contraction is that by
### which matter is contracted, withdrawing 
### into a narrower space by condensation.

<summary>This page categorizes the eight types of motion in matter, specifically defining expansion as a process where heat causes matter to occupy more space, illustrated by the example of a swelling bladder.</summary>
<keywords>motion of matter, expansion, contraction, rarification, condensation, spirit of the universe, physics</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the eight types of motion, specifically providing examples of contraction and introducing the "motion of aggregation," which describes how objects are drawn to similar substances.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<meta>catchword: shall</meta>

For example, if you move the aforementioned bladder from the furnace into a cold place, the vapor will return to water, and the swelling of the bladder will subside. Or, if you put a leather strap <note>original: "thong"</note> into the fire, you shall see it become wrinkled and contracted. This happens because the softer parts are extracted by the fire, and the rest must necessarily shrink. For the same reason, cracks and gaps also appear in timber and the earth.

### X *The motion of aggregation occurs when a body is drawn toward its like substances.* <note>original: "connaturals"; things of the same nature or kind</note>

For example, our flame goes upward, while a stone goes downward. This is because the flame perceives that its natural companions (that is, subtle <note>original: "subtile"; thin, airy, or refined</note> bodies) are above, while a stone perceives that its own (that is, heavy things) are here below. *Note well* that those who are ignorant of the other types of motion commonly call this one "natural." But although it appears most clearly to the sight and seems to be the strongest and most unchangeable, it is actually quite weak. This is because it yields to all the other types of motion that follow and does not exert itself until they cease—which will become apparent to anyone who meditates on these things diligently. Yet I will add this: a drop of ink fallen upon paper "defends" itself by its roundness <note>This is an early description of surface tension</note>; yet if you touch a moist pen to it, you
