you will see the drop run up into it. Notice that it does not go downward (as it should because of its weight) but upward, so that it may join itself to a larger quantity of the same substance.

### XI *The motion of sympathy and antipathy is that by which a similar body is drawn toward its like and driven away by its opposite.*

This similarity comes from the spirit <note>In early science, "spirit" often referred to an internal vital force or a subtle, active substance that governed an object's behavior.</note> that lives within it. This motion is very obvious in some bodies, such as in the lodestone <note>original: "loadstone"; a naturally magnetized mineral</note>, which draws iron to itself or even leaps toward the iron. In other things, it is weak and barely noticeable—for example, in milk, where the cream separates itself little by little from the watery <note>original: "wheyie"</note> parts and gathers at the top. In some substances, this motion is, as it were, bound up unless it is released in some way. This is seen in melted brass <note>Likely referring here to general alloys or mixed metals</note>: when metals are separated from one another by the power of fire, and by the virtue of sympathy, each substance gathers with its own kind (lead to lead, silver to silver) and flows together into a specific place.

### XII *The motion of continuity is that by which matter follows matter, avoiding any break in substance.* <note>original: "shunning discontinuity"; this describes the principle that "nature abhors a vacuum."</note>

For example, when you suck up the air through a pipe after putting one end of it into the water, the

water will follow the air, even though it moves upward. For we said before that the world, like a living creature, would not allow itself to be cut <note>The author is referring to the concept of *horror vacui*, or the idea that nature "abhors a vacuum" and will pull matter along to ensure no empty space is created.</note>, as the living spirit unites all things.

### XIII *The motion of impulsion (or yielding) is that by which matter gives way to other matter that presses upon it.*

For example, water yields to a stone that falls into it so that the stone may sink; similarly, a stone yields to the hand that pushes it, and so on. A body will not allow itself to be penetrated; it would rather move aside if it can. If the whole body cannot move, its parts yield, as we see happen in every break, bruise, tear, wearing down, or cutting; for the weaker part always yields to the stronger.

### XIV *The motion of oscillation <note>original: "libration"; the act of swinging or balancing</note> is that in which parts move themselves back and forth so that they may be correctly positioned within the whole.*

This is seen when a balance scale moves itself, first this way and then that.

### XV *The motion of liberty <note>This refers to elasticity or the "freedom" of an object to return to its natural shape.</note> is that by which a body (or a part of it) returns to its original place after being forcibly moved but not fully torn away.*

This is seen when a branch of a tree is bent by force and, when let go, returns to its original position <note>original: "positure"</note>.

### *An example of all these motions in the formation of the Macrocosm <note>original: Macrocosme; the universe or "great world"</note> or great World.*

First, the spirit moved itself upon the waters with the motion of *Agitation*. Then the light, being sent into the matter, penetrated it in every direction with the motion of *Diffusion*. Soon after, the matter above—where the light passed through—being heated and thinned <note>original: rarified</note>, expanded <note>original: dilated</note> itself with the motion of *Dispansion* <note>the act of spreading out or expanding</note>. But below, it thickened <note>original: coagulated</note> itself with the motion of *Contraction*.

And all the more subtle parts gathered themselves upwards, while the coarser <note>original: grosser</note> parts gathered downwards, with the motions of *Aggregation* and *Sympathy*. (A more hidden <note>original: occult</note> Sympathy and Antipathy was put into things later.) Wherever one part of the matter went, others followed by the motion of *Continuity*; or if one rushed against others, they gave way by the motion of *Impulsion*. 

But the coarser <note>original: grosser</note> parts balanced <note>original: poise</note> themselves around the center—fleeing from the heat which came upon them from above—to an exact roundness <note>original: Globosity</note>, with the motion of *Libration* <note>the act of balancing or poising</note>. There was no motion of Liberty, because there was no external violence to put anything out of order.

### *An example of the same motions in the Microcosm <note>original: Microcosme; "the little world," referring to man as a miniature reflection of the universe</note>, or little World.*

<vocab>Macrocosm, Agitation, Diffusion, Expansion, Contraction, Aggregation, Sympathy, Antipathy, Continuity, Impulsion, Roundness, Balancing, Microcosm</vocab>

<meta>catchword: In</meta>

<summary>The author explains the creation of the universe ("the Macrocosm") as a series of physical motions, such as agitation, diffusion, and contraction, driven by spirit and light. This mechanical-theological framework is then prepared for application to the human body ("the Microcosm").</summary>

<keywords>Macrocosm, Microcosm, creation, natural philosophy, physics of spirit, motion, elements, agitation, diffusion, libration</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the application of physical laws of motion to the "Microcosm" or human being, specifically explaining how biological processes like digestion, circulation, and breathing correspond to the universal motions defined on previous pages.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<meta>catchword: You</meta>

In humans (and in every living creature), the food that is put into the belly grows hot with warmth <note>original: incalescency</note>; here you have the motion of *Expansion*. Then, by the motion of *Sympathy* <note>a natural attraction or affinity</note>, every part of the body attracts to itself what is good for it. But by the motion of *Antipathy* <note>a natural repulsion</note>, unnecessary things are driven out as useless and harmful to them. 

Then the blood is distributed equally to the whole body, moving upward and downward by the motion of *Libration* <note>original: Libration; the act of balancing or steadying</note>. After being absorbed <note>original: assimilated</note> by the various parts of the body, it is thickened so that it may become flesh, a membrane, a bone, etc., by the motion of *Contraction*. 

Finally, the air drawn in and let out during breathing shows the motion of *Continuity* and *Contiguity* <note>the state of being in direct contact</note>. (For when the lungs are expanded, the air enters so that there should be no empty space <note>original: vacuum</note>; but when the lungs contract, the air moves out.) 

The motion of *Liberty* <note>the ability of a substance to return to its original state after being disturbed</note> will appear if you either press down or pull up your skin; for as soon as you take away your hand, it will return to its original position. Lastly, if you fall from any place, there will be the motion of *Aggregation* <note>the tendency of like things to gather together</note>, for you will move toward the earth, as you are made of weight and earth yourself.

### XVI. If motions are combined <note>original: infolded</note>, they either increase or hinder one another's force.

<vocab>warmth, Expansion, Sympathy, Antipathy, Balancing, Contraction, Continuity, Contact, empty space, Liberty, Gathering together</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of combined motions, specifically how natural forces and external impulses interact, and introduces the concept of compound motion in living beings.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>

You have an example of the first type if you throw a stone toward the earth; in this case, the motion of *Aggregation* <note>the natural tendency of heavy objects to move toward the center of the earth</note> and *Impulsion* <note>the force applied to move an object</note> are joined together. You see an example of the latter type if you throw a stone toward heaven <note>upward</note>. In this case, the motion of *Impulsion* strives against the motion of *Aggregation*. In this struggle, the stronger force eventually overcomes the weaker—the natural motion overcomes that which is merely added <note>original: accessory</note>.

### XVII. Compound motion occurs in living creatures when they move themselves from place to place of their own accord.

Specifically, birds move by flying, fish by swimming, and beasts by running. We shall see in Chapter 10 how each of these is performed.

Also, natural philosophers <note>early scientists who studied the physical world</note> call it a "compound motion" when a thing is completely changed—either beginning to exist, ceasing to exist, or changing into another kind of being—even if it stays in the same place. However, we call these *mutations* <note>transformations or changes in state</note>, and they are to be handled in a specific chapter, the third one from this.

# CHAPTER IV.

## Concerning the Qualities of Things.

**M**atter is mixed in various ways with spirit and light through these different motions. From this varied mixture come different qualities, so that one thing is called—and indeed *is*—one specific thing, and another is called something else. We must now consider this; these <term>talities</term> <note>original: "talities"; a word derived from the Latin "talis," meaning "of such a kind." It refers to the "such-ness" or specific nature of a thing.</note>, or qualities, are either general (common to all bodies) or special (proper only to certain creatures). The general qualities are to be explained here all at once, while the others will be discussed later in their proper places.

> I. *A quality is an <term>accident</term> <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a property or characteristic that a thing possesses, but which is not essential to its existence (for example, the color of a stone).</note> of a body, by which every thing is said to be "such" or "of such a kind."*

> II. *There are qualities in every body—including those that are intangible, spiritual, and volatile, as well as those that are gross, tangible, and fixed.*

For a body is (as we saw in Chapter 2, in the description of matter, <term>Aphorism 8</term>, and of the spirit, <term>Aphorism 1</term>...)

The qualities, therefore, which we will treat of, shall be common to all these. For it may be said of a stone, and of water, and of air, and of the spirit that is enclosed in a body, that it is fat or raw <note>original: "fat or raw"; in early natural philosophy, "fat" (unctuous) and "raw" (crude) referred to the presence or absence of oily, combustible, or refined substances.</note>, hot or cold, moist or dry, thick or thin, etc.

### III *The qualities are the grounds of all forms in bodies.*

For these qualities cause a living creature to differ from a stone, a stone from wood, and wood from ice; the "form" <note>In this context, "form" refers to the essential identity and characteristics of a thing that make it what it is.</note> consists of qualities. Therefore, the doctrine of qualities is exceedingly profitable and is, as it were, the basis of natural science. Because this subject has been miserably handled until now, the light of physics <note>original: "phyſicks"; here meaning the general study of nature and the physical world.</note> has been maimed and, by that means, remains obscure.

### IV *A quality is either intrinsic and substantial, or extrinsic and accidental.*

->*Concerning the substantial qualities: Sulphur, Salt, and Mercury.*<- <note>In early modern chemistry and alchemy, these were known as the "Tria Prima" or three primary elements that made up all material things.</note>

For just as in the beginning the spirit joined <note>original: "conjoyned"</note> with the matter to produce the moving of the waters, so *Mercury* is nothing but motion. It is the first fluid thing, which cannot be fixed or contained within a limit. And *Salt* is dry, hot, and incorruptible, just like spirit and fire; it is preserved by fire and dissolved by water (or *Mercury*), but it turns into neither flame nor smoke, even though it is a most spiritual creature <note>In this context, "creature" refers to any created thing or substance.</note> and entirely incorruptible.

And *Sulphur*, what is it but matter mixed

**N. 2.** But be careful that you do not mistake these for our common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> minerals: salt, sulphur, and mercury (or quicksilver).

For these common minerals are mixed bodies: salty earth, sulphurous earth, and mercurial water. That is, they are matter in which Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury are the dominant elements, yet they have other things joined to them. For Salt has parts that are easily inflamed, and Sulphur contains some salt and some Mercury; however, the name <note>original: "denomination"</note> is taken from the principle that is most abundant.

These qualities cannot be seen as they are in their pure state, but only through the imagination. Yet, they exist in all things, as chemists <note>original: "Chymicks"</note> demonstrate to the eye. They extract raw and watery parts out of every piece of wood, stone, etc., as well as other fatty and oily parts; that which remains is salt—that is, the ashes. Thus, the physical evidence itself shows that some liquids are mercurial (such as common water and phlegm <term>flegme; here referring to a watery, non-flammable distillate</term>); others are sulphurous (such as oil and alcohol <term>spirit of wine</term>); and others are salty and sharp (such as nitric acid <term>aqua fortis</term>). We also find by experience, through the numbness and aches of our limbs <note>original: "members"</note>, that some vapors in the body are raw, while others are sharp.

VI. God produced these qualities intrinsically, so that the substance of every body might be formed. For:

| Principle | | Qualities | | Resulting Property |
|-----------|-----------|-----------|-----------|-----------|
| Mercury (☿) | | fluidity, coition <note>joining together</note>, rawness <note>original: "crudity"</note>. | | incombustibility. |
| **Sulphur** | **gives unto things** | softness, adhesiveness <note>original: "cleaving together"</note>, fatness. | **and from thence** | inflammability. |
| Salt | | consistency, hardness, brittleness <note>original: "aptness to break"</note>. | | incorruptibility. |

The fact that Mercury provides fluidity and the easy joining <note>original: "coition"</note> of matter is evident from quicksilver (liquid mercury). Because Mercury is the dominant element in quicksilver, it is extremely fluid—so much so that it cannot be easily contained <note>original: "stopped"</note> or made solid <note>original: "fixed"</note>. It is also very raw <note>original: "crude"</note>, meaning it can neither be ignited nor burned; instead, if you apply fire to it, it escapes into the air as vapor.

Furthermore, that the solidification <note>original: "coagulation"</note> of bodies comes from sulphur—acting like a glue—is shown by the fact that there is more oil in dry, solid, and dense <note>original: "close"</note> bodies than in moist ones. It is also seen because ashes (after the sulphur has been consumed by fire) will not stick together in a lump if you pour water on them; however, if you use oil or fat, they do stick together. Chemists <note>original: "Chymicks"</note> now extract oil from every stone, leaving behind nothing but ashes, where no part sticks to another any longer.

And the fact that *salt* provides consistency is shown by the bones of living creatures, from which <term>Chemists</term> <note>original: "Chymicks"</note> extract pure salt. Additionally, all dense objects leave behind many ashes (which is salt) when burned.

Therefore, God with great wisdom balanced these three qualities together in physical bodies. For if *Mercury* were missing, matter would not flow together to create <note>original: "for the generation of"</note> things. If *salt* were missing, nothing would hold together or remain solid <note>original: "be fixed"</note>. If *sulphur* were missing, the consistency of things would be unstable <note>original: "forced"</note> and easily scattered <note>original: "dissipated"</note>. Finally, if there were no sulphur in wood and other materials, we could have no fire on earth except for that of the sun <note>original: "Solar"</note>, because nothing else could be set on fire. Imagine then what great hardships <note>original: "defects"</note> human life would suffer!

### *On the accidental or external qualities of bodies.*

So much for the essential <note>original: "substantiall"</note> qualities; the accidental qualities follow.

VII. *An accidental quality is either obvious <note>original: "manifest"</note> or hidden <note>original: "occult." In early science, "occult" referred to properties like magnetism that could not be explained by the four elements.</note>.*

VIII. *An obvious quality is one that can be perceived by the senses and is therefore called "sensible."*

Examples include heat, cold, softness, and roughness.

**IX** *A <term>hidden quality</term> <note>original: "occult quality." In early modern science, an occult quality was a property of an object—like magnetism—that could be observed through its effects but whose cause was not immediately apparent to the senses.</note> is one that is known only by experience—that is, by its effect (such as the attraction of iron to a <term>loadstone</term> <note>original: "loadstone," a naturally magnetized mineral</note>, etc.). Therefore, it is called "insensible" because it cannot be directly perceived.*

**N.** *The obvious <note>original: "manifest"</note> qualities arise from the various mixtures <note>original: "temperatures"</note> of the elements and essential qualities; the hidden qualities arise directly from the unique spirit of every thing.*

**X** *Sensible qualities are five-fold, according to the number of the senses: visible, audible, <term>olfactile</term> <note>relating to smell</note>, <term>gustatile</term> <note>relating to taste</note>, and tangible. These are: color, sound, odor, flavor, and <term>tangor</term> <note>the author's invented word for the quality of being touchable</note>.*

*Let no one be offended by the unusual word **tangor**; it is invented for the sake of teaching, and logic permits it. For if we say "heat" <note>original: calor</note> comes from "to be hot" <note>original: caleo</note>; "color" <note>original: color</note> from "to cultivate or adorn" <note>original: colo</note>; "flavor" <note>original: sapor</note> from "to taste" <note>original: sapio</note>; "love" <note>original: amor</note> from "to love" <note>original: amo</note>; "flow" <note>original: fluor</note> from "to flow" <note>original: fluo</note>; "liquid" <note>original: liquor</note> from "to melt" <note>original: liquo</note>; "clanging" <note>original: clangor</note> from "to sound" <note>original: clango</note>; and "anguish" <note>original: angor</note> from "to compress or choke" <note>original: ango</note>; why not also "tangor" from "to touch" <note>original: tango</note>?*

### Of the tangible quality.

**XI** *The tangible quality (or **tangor**) is a certain arrangement <note>original: "positure"</note> of the parts of matter within a body.*

**XII** *The pairings <note>original: "copulations"</note> of these qualities are twelve <note>While the author states there are twelve, he lists seven pairs here, totaling fourteen individual qualities.</note>; for every body, in respect to touch, is: 1. thin <note>original: "rare"</note> or dense, 2. moist or dry, 3. soft or hard, 4. flexible or stiff, 5. smooth or rough, 6. light or heavy, 7. hot or cold.*

We must accurately consider each of these, regarding what they are and how they exist.

**XIII** *<term>Rarity</term> <note>original: "Rarity." In this context, it refers to the state of being thinned out or less dense.</note> is an extension of thinned-out matter through larger spaces; <term>density</term>, on the contrary, is a tighter pressing together of matter into one space.*

For all earth, water, air, and spirit <note>original: "fpirit." In early modern science, "spirit" often referred to highly refined, thin substances like vapors or gasses.</note> are sometimes more rare and sometimes more dense. We must note that no body is so dense that it does not have pores, even if they are unnoticeable <note>original: "infenfible," meaning too small to be perceived by the senses.</note>. This is apparent in wooden and earthenware vessels, which release liquids in the form of sweat. It is also seen in a lead bottle filled with water: if it is crushed with hammers or a press, it sweats out water like a most delicate dew.

**XIV** *<term>Humidity</term> (or moisture) <note>original: "humour"</note> is the liquidness of a body's parts and their tendency to be penetrated by one another; <term>siccity</term> <note>original: "ficcity," a term for dryness.</note>, on the contrary, is a firmness and an impenetrability of the parts of the body.*

So a clod hardened by either heat or cold is dry earth, but mud <note>original: "mire"</note> is moist earth; water is a humid liquid, but ice is dry water, and so on.

**XV** *<term>Softness</term> is a physical makeup <note>original: "conftitution"</note> of matter that is somewhat moist, easily yielding to the touch:*

*<term>Hardness</term> is a dryness of the matter that does not yield to the touch.*

So a stone is either hard or soft; likewise water, spirit, air, etc.

**XVI** *<term>Flexibility</term> is a compaction of the matter with a "moist glue" <note>The author uses "glue" to describe the internal binding properties or cohesive forces of a substance.</note>, so that it allows itself to be bent. <term>Stiffness</term> is a coagulation of the matter with "dry glue," so that it will not bend but instead breaks.*

For instance, iron is stiff, while steel is flexible. Similarly, some wood is flexible and other wood is stiff; but note that what is flexible is also called "tough," and what is stiff is called "brittle."

**XVII** *<term>Smooth</term> is that which, through the equality of its parts, pleasantly affects the sense of touch. <term>Rough</term> is that which, through the inequality of its parts, distracts and draws asunder <note>Pulls apart or irritates the sensory surface.</note> the touch.*

Note that in liquid things, the smooth is called "mild" and the rough is called "tart." For example, unpolished marble is rough, but when polished, it is smooth. Water is rough, while oil is mild. A vehement and cold wind is rough and sharp, while warm air is mild. Likewise, in our bodies, humors, vapors, and spirits are said to be either mild or sharp.

**XVIII** *<term>Lightness</term> is the rushing upward of a body by reason of its rarity <note>Thinness or low density.</note> and spirituosity <note>The state of being vaporous or containing "spirit"—refined, thin matter.</note>. <term>Heaviness</term> is the tendency of a dense body to move downward. Lightness appears in flame and every exhalation <note>Vapors or gases rising from the earth.</note>, while heaviness appears in water and earth.*

Note Well 1: How this motion is directed upwards and downwards by a "love of fellowship" <note>The author uses this phrase to describe the natural attraction or affinity between substances of the same kind.</note>, or an affinity for things of the same nature, was discussed in Chapter 3.

2. The inequality of heaviness or <term>ponderosity</term> <note>Weight or massiveness.</note> results from the unequal <term>condensation</term> <note>In this context, density; how tightly packed the matter is.</note> of the matter. For look, the more matter there is in a body, the heavier it is: a stone is heavier than wood, metals are heavier than stones, and among these, gold, quicksilver <note>Mercury.</note>, and lead are the heaviest of all because they are the most compact bodies.

3. Among all heavy things, gold is found to have the greatest weight, while <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>Alcohol, specifically distilled ethanol.</note> (or distilled wine) has the least. The ratio of quantity <note>The author is describing what we now call relative density or specific gravity.</note> between these two is found not to exceed the proportion of 21 parts; so, one drop of gold is no heavier than twenty-one drops of spirit of wine.

**XIX** *Heat is a motion of the most minute parts of matter vibrating <note>original: reverberated</note> against itself, penetrating and tearing the sense of touch like a thousand sharp points; but cold is a motion of the parts contracting themselves.*

Note Well 1: It appears that heat and cold are both motions and fixed qualities: 1. Because no body is found among us that is perpetually...

...perpetually hot or cold, in the same way that there are things that are naturally rare and dense, or moist and dry, etc. Instead, a thing becomes hot or cold through a process; this process is carried out by motion. 2. Second, because the senses themselves testify that during scorching, the skin and limbs are penetrated and pulled apart, whereas in cold they are blocked and bound; therefore, it is a motion. 3. Third, because whatever is frequently heated (even if it is metal) is reduced in both size and weight until it is eventually consumed. Why is that, if not because the heat, casting out a thousand <term>atoms</term> <note>The author is referring to the "corpuscular" theory of matter—the idea that all things are made of tiny, invisible particles.</note>, wears and consumes the matter away?

Now, it is called a "motion of parts" that is <term>vibrated</term> <note>original: reverberated</note> against itself. This is because something moved as a whole and in a direct line (not reflected back) does not produce heat—for example, the wind or a bird flying. However, motion that involves <term>vibration</term> <note>original: reverberation</note> or a rapid alteration does produce heat, as seen in the reflection <note>original: repercussion</note> of light, the repeated collision of bodies, or rubbing things together through friction, and so on.

3. But we must distinguish between **Hot** <note>original: *Calidum*</note>, **Heating** <note>original: *Calefactivum*</note>, and **Heatable** <note>original: *Calefactile*</note>. **Hot** <note>original: *Calidum*</note> or **Heated** <note>original: *Calefactum*</note> refers to something that is actually hot and scorches the touch, such as a flame, red-hot iron, boiling <note>original: seething</note> water, or air (which can also reach a very intense heat), and so on.

<meta>catchword: N.</meta>

<summary>This page argues that heat is a form of internal motion that can consume matter over time, and it distinguishes between being "hot" as a state and the capacity to heat or be heated.</summary>
<keywords>heat, motion, atoms, vibration, friction, thermodynamics, caloric theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the definitions of heat-related terms, specifically distinguishing between things that produce heat and things that absorb it, following the previous page's discussion on heat as a form of internal motion.</meta>

<lang>English</lang>
<page-num>60</page-num>
<meta>catchword: cause</meta>

Note well: among all things known to us, *fire is the hottest*; we have nothing that is *most cold* but ice; nevertheless, ice is far from being as cold as fire is hot.

**A heating agent** <note>original: *Calefactivum*</note> is that which can stir up heat, such as motion and whatever can produce motion—namely fire, pepper, and all pungent and bitter things taken into the body. For motion comes from fire, and fire comes from motion, and heat comes from both. For just as fire must be in motion (otherwise it goes out immediately), so motion must catch fire, as is seen by striking a flint or rubbing wood together for a long time. Therefore, both are heating agents, but fire is further said to be actually hot, while heating agents are commonly called **potentially hot** <note>original: *hot in potentia*</note>.

**A heatable substance** <note>original: *Calefactile*</note> is that which may easily be heated, such as air, followed by fatty things (oil, butter), then wood, and then water. In these substances, because the parts are somewhat **thin** <note>original: rare; meaning the particles are spread out rather than dense</note>, they are more easily moved to **rapid vibration** <note>original: agitation</note>. Stones and metals, because they have their matter **dense** <note>original: compacted</note>, do not easily admit heat; however, they retain it longer once it is admitted because the heat cannot easily escape through their **narrow** <note>original: straight</note> pores. And this is the

why all things consisting of small particles—such as feathers, hairy skins, and all rough things (and even all sorts of dust)—either always retain heat (by a certain movement <note>original: agitation</note> of the air trapped inside) or at least easily receive it through some release of vapor <note>original: transpiration</note> given off by a living body.

4. We must also note that all these tactile qualities can be described in different ways for the same substance. Specifically, they are relative to another substance. For example, compared to air, water is a dense and heavy substance; but compared to earth or a stone, it is thin <note>original: rare</note> and light. Furthermore, depending on how our sense of touch is conditioned, a substance may seem to be one way or another. For example, lukewarm water feels cold to a hot hand, but hot to a cold hand.

5. The various effects of heat must also be considered according to the different nature of the object being heated.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the effects of heat on various substances, specifically focusing on the process of "attenuation" or the thinning and breakdown of matter.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Of</meta>

The constant effect of heat is **attenuation** <note>original: attenuatiō; the process of making a substance thinner, less dense, or more fluid</note>: but it works in different ways depending on whether the matter is:

<note>The following text is arranged in the original as a branching logic diagram with large brackets.</note>

* **Liquid** <note>Written vertically within a bracket in the original to categorize the following two points.</note>
    * **Sulfurous** <note>original: Sulphury; in early science, "sulfur" represented the principle of inflammability and oiliness.</note>, which heat ignites, turns to flame, and rushes upward.
    * **Mercurial** <note>original: Mercuriall; in early science, "mercury" represented the principle of volatility and vapor.</note>, which heat rarefies <note>makes less dense</note> and expands, as can be seen in the evaporation of water. This is also seen in the desiccation <note>drying out</note> of earth, wood, etc., in which all the fluid <note>original: humour</note> and moisture present turns into air and evaporates.
* **Solid** <note>original: Consistent; meaning a substance that holds its shape.</note>
    * If it has parts that are **glutinous** <note>original: Glutiness; sticky, oily, or resinous</note> or **sulfurous**, heat forces them to melt, as can be seen in suet <note>animal fat</note>, wax, and metals.
    * If it has parts that are **ashy** or **salty**, heat forces them to be condensed <note>made more compact</note> by drying up the moisture. It also causes them to break if you apply more force, such as wood, a clod of earth, a tile, etc. (And so, hardening is an indirect effect <note>original: effect by accident</note> of heat.)

<vocab>attenuation, sulfurous, mercurial, evaporation, desiccation, solid, glutinous, ashy, indirect effect</vocab>

It concludes that while heat generally thins matter, it can indirectly cause hardening by evaporating moisture from ashy or salty materials.</summary>

## Of tastes.

XX. The quality related to taste <note>original: gustatile quality</note> is called **savor** or **taste**, which is a mixing <note>original: tempering</note> of the primary qualities <note>the four qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry</note> by heat and cold.

---

<note>The following diagram explains how different flavors are produced by "Salt"—one of the three primary principles in early modern science—when it is influenced by heat, cold, or combined with "Sulfur" (the principle of oiliness).</note>

**From Salt:**

*   **According to its own nature:**
    *   When **Balanced** <note>original: Temperate</note> and joined with **Sulfur**, it produces: **Sweetness**.
    *   When **Scorched** <note>original: Adult; likely a transcription error for "adust," meaning parched or burnt by heat</note> and joined with **Sulfur**, it produces: **Bitterness**.
*   **When transformed by temperature:**
    *   **Burnt** <note>original: Combust</note> produces: **Saltness** <note>original: Softnesse; likely an OCR error for "Saltnesse," which fits the chemical logic of the text</note>.
    *   **Moderately cooled** <note>original: Indifferently cooled</note> produces: **Sharpness** and **Sourness** <note>original: Sowernesse</note>.
    *   **Extremely cooled** produces: **Bitterness** and **Astringency** <note>original: Austerity; a harsh, puckering taste like an unripe fruit</note>.

<margin>
The biting pungency <note>original: Mordaity/Mordacity</note> found in all these flavors indicates the presence of **Salt**.
</margin>

It appears, therefore, where herbs, fruits, parts of living creatures, and minerals get their flavors <note>original: "favours"; likely a transcription error for "savours," meaning tastes</note>; namely, from **salt** and **sulfur**, which are diffused everywhere, and from which every creature absorbs more or less according to its nature. **Mercury** <note>Along with Salt and Sulfur, Mercury was one of the "Three Principles" (Tria Prima) thought by early modern chemists to make up all material things. Mercury represented the fluid or spirit-like essence.</note> is in itself without taste (as we notice in **phlegm** <note>original: "flegme"; here referring to a watery, tasteless bodily humor or distilled residue</note>), but the other elements are soaked by it, and also affected by various degrees of cold or heat, so that they are more or less sweet, bitter, salty, and so on.

### *Of smells.*

XXI. *The sense of smell <note>original: "olfactile quality"</note> is called odor, which is a very fine vapor <note>original: "thin exhalation"</note> of the taste.*

Yet sulfur-rich <note>original: "sulphury"</note> things produce more smell than salty things, and hot things more than others, because heat thins the substance and spreads it into the air. This is why gardens and perfume boxes <note>original: "ointment boxes"</note> are so much sweeter when the air is hotter. However, the sweeter they are, the sooner they lose their smell, as that fragrant <note>original: "odiferous"</note> sulfurous quality is exhaled little by little.

### *Of sounds.*

XXII. *The sense of hearing <note>original: "audible quality"</note> is called sound, which is a splitting <note>original: "cleaving"</note> of the air when it is struck sharply, flowing in every direction.*

Not every motion of the air produces a sound, but only that motion by which the air is...

### Of colors.

XXIII *The visible quality is called color, which is light received in various ways on the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of bodies and tempered with the opposite darkness, such as whiteness, blackness, greenness, etc.*

Observation 1. That color is nothing in itself, but light reflected in different ways from things, is shown: 1. because just as it is not seen without light, it is also not found to be anything by any other sense or by reason; nor does it exist otherwise. 2. because colors, as well as light, spread <note>original: "diffuse"</note> themselves through the air and are in the eyes of all beholders. Now, we saw before that this spreading motion was proper to the light; therefore, color is in—

indeed nothing but light differently tinted by the various surfaces <note>original: "superficies"</note> of things. 3. Because light, being reflected in several ways in the same matter, produces several colors.

We see this, for example, in a cloud, which is inherently uniform, yet it appears to us sometimes whitish, sometimes blackish, and sometimes reddish <note>original: "ruddy"</note>, depending on how it is positioned against the light. In the same way, we see yellow, green, flame-color, and sky-blue in the rainbow (which is nothing more than the dissolving of a cloud into tiny drops of water). This also appears in crystal dust turned toward the light, which shows plainly that color is nothing other than a different tinting of light resulting from the different angle <note>original: "incidency"</note> at which it strikes a surface. But in those cases (in the rainbow and glass), the colors pass through because the material itself is fluid and transparent. In solid <note>original: "fixed"</note> bodies, colors are also fixed, but in a way known more to God than to us.

Observation 2. That based on how a body receives colors, it is called *pellucid* <note>original: "Pellucid"; meaning transparent</note> or *opaque* <note>original: "Opacous"</note>. *Pellucid* (*transparent* and *diaphanous*) is that which allows light to pass through it and is therefore neither colored nor seen, such as air and, to some extent, water.

glass, crystal, a diamond, etc. (The fact that air is not colored, even when it is tinted <note>original: "tinct"</note> with light, is apparent in a room closed up on every side. An *opaque* <note>original: "Opacous"</note> body is one that does not allow light to pass through it but reflects it, and therefore it is colored and visible. Examples include earth, wood, stone, gems, and colored waters. This *light reflected from an opaque body is properly called color*. Of this, there are six kinds: *white, yellow, green, red, sky-blue, and black.*

*White* is light reflected with its own true appearance <note>original: "proper face"</note>.

*Yellow* is light tinted with a little darkness.

*Green* is light in a middle and most pleasing balance <note>original: "temperature"; referring to the mixing of qualities</note> of light and darkness.

*Red* is light inclining more toward darkness.

*Sky-blue* <note>original: "Skie-colour"</note> is light that is more than half dark.

*Black*, last of all, is the lack of reflection <note>original: "non-repercussion"</note>

## CHAPTER V.
### *On the changes <note>original: "mutations"</note> of things: creation <note>original: "generation"</note>, decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>, etc.*

**F**rom the oppositions <note>original: "contrarieties"</note> of qualities, especially of cold and heat (for these two qualities are the most active), those changes <note>original: "mutations"</note> originate to which all things in the world are subject, as we shall now see.

I. *Change <note>original: "Mutation"</note> is a property <note>original: "accident"; in Aristotelian philosophy, a quality that can change without destroying the object's identity</note> of a body, by which its essence is changed.*

Specifically, whether a thing passes from non-existence to existence; or from existence to non-existence; or from being one way to being another.

### II *All bodies are subject to change <note>original: "mutations"</note>.*
The reason is that they are all composed of matter, spirit, and fire: these three are perpetually mixed among themselves in various ways. Because matter is a fluid and slippery thing, and the spirit is restless, always setting itself in motion; and (heat being raised everywhere by light and motion) it eats into, tears, and pulls apart the matter of things. For this reason, I say, nothing can remain in the same state for long. All things grow, increase, decrease, and perish again. This is why the Scriptures also state that the heavens grow old, just like a garment does, *Psalm 103:27* <note>In modern Bibles, this passage—"they shall grow old like a garment"—is found in Psalm 102:26. The author's numbering reflects an older tradition.</note>.

### III *The change <note>original: "mutation"</note> of a thing is either essential or accidental.*

### IV *Essential change <note>original: "mutation"</note> occurs when a thing begins to exist or ceases to exist: the first is called <term>generation</term>, and the other <term>decay</term> <note>original: "corruption"; in this context, it means the destruction of a thing's form</note>.*
For example: when snow is formed from water, it is said to be "generated"; when it is dissolved back into water, it is said to be "decayed" <note>original: "corrupted"</note>.

### V *An accidental change <note>original: "mutation"</note> of a thing occurs when it increases or decreases, or is changed in its qualities: the first is called <term>augmentation</term> <note>growth</note>, the next <term>diminution</term> <note>shrinking</note>, and the last <term>alteration</term> (each of which we will now examine individually to see how they occur).*

## *On the generation of things.*

VI *Generation is the production of a thing, so that what did not exist begins to be.*

Thus every year, and indeed every day, infinite things are generated throughout all of nature.

VII *For generation, three things are required: seed, a <term>matrix</term> <note>original: "Matrix"; in this context, a womb or a protective environment where something can grow</note>, and moderate heat.*

These three things are necessary for the generation of living creatures, plants, metals, stones, and finally <term>meteors</term> <note>original: "meteors"; in early modern science, this referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including rain, wind, and lightning, not just falling stars</note>, as will be seen in their appropriate sections.

VIII *Seed is a small portion of matter that has the spirit of life contained within it.*

For seed is physical <note>original: "corporall"</note> and visible; therefore, it is made of matter <note>original: "materiate"</note>. It is not truly seed unless it contains the spirit of the <term>species</term> <note>original: "species"; the specific type of organism or substance</note> it is meant to be. After all, what else would form it? Therefore, seeds from which the spirit has escaped <note>original: "exhaled"</note> are useless for generation.

IX *The <term>matrix</term> is a suitable <note>original: "convenient"</note> place to deposit the seed so that it may exercise its power <note>original: "vertue"</note>.*

Nothing exists without a place, nor is anything generated without a suitable location, for otherwise the actions of nature are hindered. Now, a place is suitable for generation if it provides the seed with:
1. A soft location.
2. <term>Enclosure</term> <note>original: "circumclusion"</note>, lest the spirit should evaporate.

It explains that seed must contain a "spirit of life" to be effective and requires a soft, enclosed space to prevent that spirit from escaping.</summary>

### *On the growth of things.*

XI. Everything that is created increases and grows <note>original: "augmenteth"</note> as much as possible. This happens through the attraction of matter and its <term>assimilation</term> <note>original: "assimilation"; the process of making one thing similar to another, or absorbing it into a system</note> into itself.

For wherever there is creation, there is heat; and where there is heat, there is fire; and where there is fire, there is a need for and an attraction to fuel <note>original: "fewell"</note>. Because heat always thins <term>attenuates</term> <note>original: "attenuateth"; to make thinner or less dense</note> the parts of the matter that evaporate <note>original: "exhale"</note>, it seeks and attracts other parts to sustain itself (just as we see in a burning candle). When a portion of matter is attracted and joined to a body, it gradually takes on that body's form until it becomes like it and is made into the same substance. For by the power of heat, things that are diverse <term>heterogeneous</term> <note>original: "heterogeneous"; consisting of different parts or kinds</note> become uniform <term>homogeneous</term> <note>original: "homogeneous"; of the same kind or nature</note>. Meanwhile, the spirit of that body also attracts to itself some of the spirit of the universe, and so multiplies itself as well. This is how stones, minerals, plants, living creatures, and so on, grow.

<summary>This page concludes the requirements for natural creation by emphasizing the necessity of heat and explains the process of growth as a form of "assimilation," where heat draws in new matter and transforms it to match the body's own substance.</summary>
<keywords>generation, growth, heat, fire, fuel, assimilation, heterogeneous, homogeneous, spirit of the universe</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the lifecycle of matter, moving from the process of growth (increase) to the inevitable stages of decrease, change, and decay.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>

### *On decrease* <note>original: "diminution"</note>

XII. Whatever has increased at some time or another ceases to increase and begins to decrease; this occurs through the <term>arefaction</term> <note>original: "arefaction"; the act of drying out or becoming dry</note> of its matter.

Specifically, because the heat that grew along with the body gradually consumes its thin and fatty parts and dries up its solid parts. As a result, the solid parts are eventually unable to <term>assimilate</term> <note>original: "assimilation"; the process of absorbing or incorporating new matter into a body</note> the matter flowing in, due to a lack of <term>gluten</term> <note>original: "gluten"; a binding or glue-like substance necessary for growth</note>. Therefore, the body fades, withers, and at length perishes.

### *On the alteration of things.*

XIII. No body always retains the same qualities, but changes them in various ways.

For example, wood is thin and soft when it grows, but afterward it is condensed and hardened, especially once it is dried. Fruit, on the contrary, grows porous <note>original: "rare"</note> and soft as it ripens, changing its color, flavor <note>original: "savour"</note>, and smell. For it is the law of the universe to be subject to <term>vicissitudes</term> <note>original: "vicissitudes"; regular changes or successions of states</note>, as well as to decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>, which is discussed next.

### *On the decay of things.* <note>original: "corruption"</note>

XIV. Every body is liable to decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>.

Because it is composed of a decaying mat—

<page-num>75</page-num>

matter and a restless spirit, which can be shaped by changes in heat. Therefore, since changes cannot be prevented, neither can perishing. And perhaps for this reason, every material thing is called a **BODY** <note>original: *CORPUS*</note>, as if it were "broken by decay" <note>original: *corrupus*; the author is using a folk etymology, linking the Latin *corpus* (body) to *corruptus* (corrupted/broken) to suggest that the very nature of a physical body is to decay.</note>, because it is subject to decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>.

### XV. *All decay occurs either by drying out or rotting.* <note>original: "arefaction or putrefaction"</note>

We are not speaking here of violent destruction, which happens when a solid object is forced apart (as when something is broken, torn, bruised, burnt, etc.). Instead, we are discussing natural decay, which destroys things from the inside. It is clear that this can happen in no other way than through drying out <note>original: "arefaction"</note> or rotting <note>original: "putrefaction"</note>.

### XVI. *Drying out occurs when the flow of matter is denied to a body, and the internal heat—having consumed its own moisture—dries and hardens the remaining parts, eventually leaving them behind.*

This is how herbs, trees, living creatures, and so on, wither away. <term>Arefaction</term> <note>the process of making or becoming dry; from the Latin *arefacere*</note> was considered a primary cause of aging in early modern science.

### XVII. *Rotting occurs when the spirit evaporates from a body, and the components of the matter are dissolved, returning to their diverse parts.* <note>original: "heterogeneous parts"</note>

<summary>This page defines the two natural forms of decay: arefaction (drying out) and putrefaction (rotting). It explains that these processes are internal and inevitable for all material bodies as they lose their vital moisture or spirits.</summary>

For then the watery parts gather together (which is why rotted things give off a foul smell); the oily parts gather together (which is why rotted things always have some oiliness <note>original: *unctuosity*</note>); and the dregs gather together (which causes the confusion of substances in rotted things and their unpleasant taste, etc.). 

Because of this, it is easy to find the reason *why cold, salt, and drying hinder rotting?* <note>original: *putrefaction*</note> Specifically, it is because cold blocks the pores of a body so that the airy <note>original: *spiritual*; in early science, "spirit" referred to the most active, thin, or gaseous parts of a substance</note> parts cannot escape or evaporate. In contrast, dried things have already been emptied of those thin parts that might rot. Salt, finally, binds the components of the matter inside and holds them as if with bandages so they cannot escape and release the spirit. 

Again, it may easily be concluded from this *why hard and oily things are durable?* It is because hard things contain much salt, which hinders rotting, and they lack moisture <note>original: *humidity*</note> (the trigger for rotting). Oily things are durable because they do not easily release their spirit, due to how well they nourish and gently contain it. (*Suet and fat rot, however, because they have loose pores and some wateriness <note>original: *aquosity*</note>.*)

Note well <note>original: *N. w.*, likely an abbreviation for the Latin *Nota bene*</note>: We must nevertheless observe that not only soft things (herbs, fruits, meat <note>original: *flesh*</note>)

For the rust of these is nothing more than the decay <note>original: rottenness</note> of their internal components <note>original: inward parts</note>, spreading outward through the pores.

### XVIII *From what has been said, it may be concluded that the world is potentially eternal.*

Since not even a single crumb of matter can perish, nor can the spirit be smothered <note>original: suffocated</note>, nor the light be extinguished, nor can any of these escape from the world, it follows that they must necessarily exist together. They must pass through one another and act upon each other. Therefore, it is impossible for anything other than one thing to be born from another, even without end. 

For that old *axiom* <note>original: Axiome</note> of the *philosophers* is most true: *the decay <note>original: corruption</note> of one thing is the creation <note>original: generation</note> of another.* In this way, the *Architect* of the World expresses his own eternity.

## CHAP. VI.

### Concerning the Elements: Sky <note>original: Skie</note>, Air, Water, and Earth.

**W**E have until now contemplated the general parts of the world, namely, the principles along with their common properties <term>accidents: in traditional philosophy, these are non-essential qualities or attributes of a thing</term>. Now follow the types <note>original: species</note> of things, which are derived from these principles through various stages. Here, the elements must be considered first, as they were formed from the first union <note>original: congresse</note> of the principles and serve as the foundations <note>original: bases</note> and hinges of the entire order of the world.

I *An element is the first and largest body in the world that possesses a simple nature.*

A body, or a substance—for although we called matter, spirit, and light "substances" as well (in Chapter 2) because they are not merely properties, yet because none of them exists by itself and in isolation, but instead they jointly make up other substances, the elements and the creatures that follow may with better right be called substances. Now, an element is said to be of a simple nature in respect to the sub-

substances <note>original: stances</note> following, which have compound natures, as it shall appear.

## II The constitution of the elements is made by light.

For light being sent into the world, by its motion and heat began to rouse up the chaos of the matter of the world. And when it turned itself round (as it still turns), it purified part of the matter and made it more subtle <term>subtle: in early science, this meant thinner, finer, or more refined in substance</term>; the rest of the matter by necessity settled and gathered itself into density elsewhere.

## III There are four Elements: Sky, Air, Water, Earth.

That is, there are four faces <note>original: faces; meaning appearances or states</note> of the matter of the world reduced into forms (for at first it was without form), differing especially in their degree of rarity <term>rarity: the state of being thin or less dense, the opposite of density</term> and density. 

Note: *The Peripatetics <note>original: Peripateticks; the followers of Aristotle</note> substitute a "sublunary fire" <term>sublunary fire: in Aristotelian physics, a theoretical layer of fire existing between the Earth's atmosphere and the Moon</term> for the sky, and call the sky a "Quintessence" <term>quintessence: literally a "fifth essence" or element, distinct from the four terrestrial elements</term>. But that same sublunary fire is a mere fiction; the heaven itself, furnished with fiery light, is the highest element of the world, as both the Scriptures and the senses themselves demonstrate. He that is not satisfied with these explanations of ours, but seeks more detailed <note>original: subtile</note> demonstrations, let him consult* Campanella <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), an Italian philosopher who argued against Aristotelianism</note>, Verulamius <note>Francis Bacon, Lord Verulam (1561–1626), an English philosopher and pioneer of the scientific method</note>, *and* Thomas Lydiat <note>An English scholar and astronomer (1572–1646)</note> *regarding the na-*

### IV *The sky is the most pure part of the matter of the world, spread over the highest spaces of the world.*

It is commonly <note>original: vulgarly</note> called the visible and starry heaven. Due to an error of the Greeks (who, thinking that it was made of a solid substance like crystal, called it a solid foundation <term>original: στερέωμα (stereoma)</term>), it is called the Firmament; but this name is hardly consistent with the truth. It is more appropriately called heaven <term>original: οὐρανὸς (ouranos)</term>, derived from the Hebrew word for light <term>original: אור (or)</term>, meaning light and fire, as if it were "fire above" <term>original: πῦρ ἄνω (pyr ano)</term>, and named for burning—just as "ether" <term>original: αἰθὴρ (aither)</term> is derived from the word "to burn" <term>original: αἴθω (aitho)</term>. For it burns with an inextinguishable light from the stars, by which it is also purified. The linguistic roots of the Hebrew word support this: the word for heavens <insert><term>שמים (shamayim)</term></insert> is composed of the words for fire <insert><term>אש (esh)</term></insert> and water <insert><term>מים (mayim)</term></insert>. The nature of the heaven is to *be liquid, and in the highest degree volatile and hot.*

### V *Air is a part of the matter that is moderately <note>original: indifferently</note> pure, spread over the lower spaces of the world.*

Its nature is to *be breathable and easily passed through in every way.*

### VI *Water is a thicker <note>original: grosser</note> part of the matter of the world, brought into a state of fluidity.*

Its nature is to be *fluid and moistening.*

### VII *The earth is the most solid <note>original: grosse</note> part of the matter, like the dregs and sediment <note>original: setling</note> gathered together at the bottom.*
Its nature is to be dry and *immobile*.

### VIII *The elements, therefore, are all made of one single matter of the world, distinguished only by degrees of density and rarity.*

For where the light rotates <note>original: wheeled about</note>, the matter is at its most rarefied and pure; below that, it is more solid; then solid and fluid; and finally, at the bottom, it becomes dregs and a thick sediment. Therefore, this is a simple progression <note>original: meer gradation</note>. For earth is nothing else but thickened and hardened water; water is nothing but thickened air; air is refined <note>original: subtilized</note> water; and water is liquefied earth.

But from this difference of density and rarity, another difference arises among these same elements: namely, *in regard to motion and rest, heat and cold*. Water is movable (for it flows); air is even more so (for it spreads itself <note>original: transfuseth it self</note> here and there); the sky does nothing but whirl around most swiftly and perpetually. Also, because of its perpetual motion, the heaven is hot (indeed, it burns) continually; the earth, because of its perpetual rest, is continually cold—except where it is warmed by the fire of heaven reaching it, or by fire enclosed within it.

### IX The elements can change <note>original: are transmutable</note> into one another.

This happens because the heat generated within the matter may expand <note>original: extend</note> or condense it. We see this occurring daily with water and air. For who does not know that water evaporates and is turned into air? And that rain teaches us that water is made again from vapor. We can also produce this same transformation with our own hands, or in common <note>original: vulgar</note> <term>alembics</term> <note>An alembic is an early chemical apparatus used for distillation, consisting of two vessels connected by a tube.</note> in which waters or wines are distilled. 

Imagine an <term>alembic</term> empty of all liquid, filled only with air. To the long pipe that hangs out from it, attach a narrow-mouthed glass vessel and seal the pipe’s mouth carefully so that no air can escape in any way. You will see that when the air cannot expand <note>original: dilate itself locally</note>, it will condense <note>original: be coagulated</note> into water in the farthest and coldest corner (that is, in the glass vessel). You will see—I say—that glass "sweat" and drip drops, into which the air, having been heated and thinned <note>original: rarified</note> in the <term>alembic</term>, condensed itself. But if you remove the fire, you will see those drops disappear little by little and return to air.

### X Aristotle thought that the elements were in a tenfold proportion to one another;

but later men have found them to be nearly a hundredfold. 

That is, that one drop of earth, through rarefaction <note>original: rarifaction; the process of becoming less dense, such as a liquid turning into a gas</note>, is made into ten drops of water; and one drop of water into ten drops of air. The truth of this latter assertion is easy to demonstrate in this way: let someone take a bladder (from an ox or a hog) and, having cleansed it, anoint it with oil to stop the pores so that the air may not escape. To the neck of this bladder (having first squeezed out all the air), let him tie the neck of a small glass vessel containing about a hundredth part of the water that the bladder could hold. Let this instrument be set in the hot sun, or in a very hot stove <note>In this context, a "stove" refers to a heated room or a drying oven</note>, where the water is turned into air by the heat; it will then appear that the bladder is full. But if you bring the same bladder, now swollen with air, into the cold, you shall see it collapse again as the vapor turns back into water. 

*Note.* The same hundredth proportion, or nearly so, is also observed among colors; for one drop of ink or red dye will color a hundred drops of water, but not the reverse. This is because blackness represents the earth in its density, while whiteness represents the heavens in its rarity <note>original: rarity; used here to mean thinness or lack of density</note>. But this very proportion varies, because the air is in

## XI *The matter of all the elements, just as it is made up of atoms, is also turned again into atoms; it does so more subtly as the mass itself becomes more subtle.*

For example, the earth and every dry and hard thing can be reduced into a dust that is almost indivisible. This dust may be sifted through a sieve, but it cannot penetrate solid materials. Water, however, may both be strained and penetrate. For instance, it can pass through vessels of earth and wood—and even lead, as we have described in an example in Chapter 4, Aphorism 13 <note>An aphorism is a concise statement of a scientific principle or truth</note>. Air and fire also penetrate through thicker bodies, just as heat passes through furnaces.

## XII *The elements are the four greatest bodies of the world, from which all others are created <note>original: generated</note>.*

The process of breaking things down <note>original: resolution; the chemical or physical analysis of a substance into its component parts</note> shows that the lesser bodies of the world, which are infinite in number and in forms, are actually composed of the elements. For when they decay <note>original: are corrupted</note>, they return into the elements. Our senses also teach us this: all things have some density <note>original: grosseness</note> from the earth, some liquid from the water, some airy quality <note>original: spirituosity</note> from the air, and some heat from the heavens <note>In this context, "heaven" refers to the celestial sphere or the element of fire</note>; and because

## XIII *Elementary matter occupies a place in the world according to its degree of density or thinness <note>original: rarity</note>.*

The earth rests at the bottom, and the water floats upon it. The air moves <note>original: fleets</note> above the water, and finally, the sky is in the highest place. You can see a similar sight if you pour clay, water, wine (especially distilled spirits <note>original: sublimated</note>), and oil into a glass; each of these will take its place according to its nature.

## XIV *Therefore, the elements form the four visible regions or spheres of the world.*

The earth is a globe which the water naturally surrounds; the air surrounds the water, and the sky surrounds the air. This is the same way that in an egg, the yolk is surrounded by the white, and the white is surrounded by the skin and the shell.

## XV *Among the elements, there are two extremes—the sky and the earth—and two middle <note>original: mean</note> elements—air and water.*

They are called extremes and means <note>middle points</note> both because of their locations <note>original: sites</note> and because of their properties <note>original: accidents; in traditional philosophy, an "accident" is a quality or characteristic that does not change the essential nature of a substance</note>

properties. For the sky is in the highest place, the most refined <note>original: thin</note> and hot; the earth is in the lowest place, the most dense <note>original: thick</note> and cold. The sky is the primary moving force; the earth is the primary place of rest. The air and water, because they share characteristics of both extremes, also share their qualities <note>original: accidents; in traditional philosophy, an "accident" is a quality or characteristic that does not change the essential nature of a substance</note>: sometimes being more or less dense or refined, moving or still, hot or cold.

## XVI *But because the elements were created not just to be a passive sight but to interact powerfully with one another, the Creator changed that order slightly and commanded two types of water and two types of fire to be made.*

## XVII *For part of the water is placed above the highest part of the sky; conversely, part of the fire is taken from the sky and locked within the depths <note>original: bowels</note> of the earth.*

Both of these ideas may seem like contradictions <note>original: paradoxes</note> and therefore require proof. Regarding the waters, it is clear from the testimony of Moses that on the second day, God made the expanse <note>original Latin: *Expansum*; often translated as the firmament</note> of heaven to divide the waters which are under the expanse from the waters above the expanse (Genesis 1:6–8). What could be clearer than that? [the rainwater] is signified by the name of the "water in heaven," and therefore [the same is claimed] here also. But I answer:

1. That the *waters in heaven* are one thing, and the *waters above heaven* are another. *Rain* might be called *water in heaven* because the *Hebrews* called the air the "first heaven"; however, it cannot be called the *waters above heaven*, which are the ones *Moses* describes.

2. The waters of the clouds are not water in its actual state <note>original: "waters in act"; a philosophical distinction meaning the substance is currently and fully water, rather than being in a state of potentiality like vapor</note>, but rather vapors. Yet *Moses* speaks of actual water. For he says explicitly that during the first seven days there *was no rain* (Chapter 2, verse 5); but he also says that those waters above the *Expanse* <note>original Latin: *Expansum*; often translated as the firmament</note> were created immediately on the second day. Therefore, they are something other than rainwater.

3. He says that the waters were separated from the waters, but the water in the clouds is not separated from the water of the sea and rivers. Instead, they are perpetually mingled—vapors ascending and rain descending.

4. He says that the *Expanse* was in the middle between the waters and the waters. But how can that be said of the clouds, which are below the *Expanse* and do not even reach a thousandth part of its height <note>original: altitude</note>? ...of Heavens, verse 4; but he lists clouds and rain afterward, among the creations of the earth, in verse 8. Why would we need any other interpretation?

Logic strongly supports this same conclusion. For when we establish the fundamental principles of the world in the order in which we see them laid out by *Moses*, it was necessary that as matter was dispersed by the motion of light, it should fly in every direction and solidify <note>original: "coagulate itself"</note> at the boundaries <note>original: "terms"</note> of the world on both sides. This ensured that in the middle where the light passed (and still passes), there would be pure sky; but on both sides, both above and below, the matter—as it hardened itself—would grow thick. We see this happen here below; why not above as well? Especially since God himself suggests it. Let it be so, for by the laws of nature it could not be otherwise.

But that there is fire contained within the earth is shown by:
1. The eruptions <note>original: "eructations"</note> of fire in *Etna*, *Vesuvius*, and *Hecla* <note>Famous volcanoes located in Sicily, Italy, and Iceland respectively</note>. 
2. The hot water springs found everywhere. 
3. The formation <note>original: "progeneration"</note> of metals, even in cold countries.
And other things which can come from nothing else but fire, which shall be examined in what follows. 
4. Lastly, there is a surviving testimony in the book of *Job*, chapter 28, verse 5.

**XVIII** *The waters above the heaven are placed there for purposes known to God, but the use of fire underground is well enough known to us as well.*

Yet we may say something of these waters by conjecture. Specifically, it was appropriate <note>original: "meet"</note> that there should be visible boundaries <note>original: "termes"</note> of the visible world, and that the heat of the celestial frame—which is constantly rotating—had need of cooling from the other side, and similar reasons. But from the fire underground, mountains, valleys, and caves of the earth are produced; stones, metals, and mineral fluids <note>original: "juyces"</note> are also generated, along with many other things we shall see in the following sections. For without heat, there is no generation <note>The process of formation or creation</note>, because there is no motion.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the celestial sky (Expansum), arguing that the heat of the Sun keeps it thinned and that the stars move the heavens along with them, rather than moving through them independently. It then transitions to discussing the atmosphere or air.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>

For by the motion and heat of the Sun, which is always present, it is perpetually thinned <note>original: "attenuated"</note> to an extreme degree of fineness <note>original: "subtlety"</note>.

**XXI** *The whole sky is moved around because that burning and ever-flying light of the stars carries it along with it.*

This is evident for several reasons: 

1. **By logic** <note>original: "reason"</note>: for if the stars moved within a stationary heaven (in the same way that birds are carried through the air and fish through the water), that penetration of the heaven would not occur without force. Neither could it be performed with such great speed <note>original: "celerity"</note>, nor with such a steady <note>original: "æquable"</note> course, because of the resistance they would encounter. Therefore, the stars are carried in heaven in every respect like clouds in the air—that is, along with their vehicle <note>original: "charriot," referring to the medium that carries them</note>. 

2. **By observation** <note>original: "sense"</note>: for we see that our own fire carries away with it the matter which it has caught and thinned—namely vapors, smoke, and flames. Why would the heavenly fire not do the same? Comets also show this to the eye, which we shall see more of in Chapter 8. 

3. The same is to be gathered from the words of Moses, when carefully considered (Genesis 1:14 and 17).

In Scripture, air is referred to by the name of the "first heaven." Yet it penetrates water and earth to fill up their cavities, because there is no such thing as a total vacuum <note>original: *vacuum*; the author follows the "plenist" theory that nature contains no empty space</note>.

**XXIII** *Air has a middle nature between the heavens and water, regarding both its location and its qualities.*

Still, air is thicker where it joins to the earth and water, and thinner toward the heavens. Therefore, on the highest peaks of some mountains, neither humans can live nor trees grow because of the thinness of the air. Due to this lack of density, the air is neither sufficient for the breathing of living creatures nor for the growth of plants.

**XXIV** *In summer, the air near the earth is hot due to the intense reflection <note>original: "vehement repercussion"</note> of the Sun’s direct <note>original: "vertical"</note> beams. In winter, because of the slanted angle <note>original: "obliquity"</note> and indirect <note>original: "obtuse"</note> reflection of the beams, the air cannot be heated. Higher up, the air is always cold, especially in summer, when that cold is trapped <note>original: "penned in"</note> on both sides by the heat of the heavens above and the earth below.* <note>This refers to the theory of "antiperistasis," the idea that a quality (like cold) is intensified when surrounded by its opposite (heat).</note>

Observation 1. The fluidity of water is such that if you give it even the slightest slope <note>original: "never so little declivity"</note>, it flows. However, the moisture <note>original: "humidity"</note> is unequal according to the degree of thinness <note>original: "rarity"</note> and density. For example, a ship does not sink as deep in the sea as it does in a river, because sea water is thicker and "drier" <note>In early science, salt was considered a "dry" element, making salt water denser and less "moist" than fresh water</note>.

Observation 2. Many commonly add that water is naturally cold, using a twofold argument: 1. because it cools things, and 2. because it extinguishes fire. But I answer that it cools not by its inherent coldness, but by its raw nature <note>original: "crudity"; a term used to describe matter that is unrefined or lacking internal heat</note>. It quenches fire in the same way that hot water and wine do (even though they are hot): not because they are the opposite of fire, but because fire is nourished by the thinner parts of the wood. If an abundance of water is poured on—or any fluid thing, even oil—the pores <note>the tiny openings or spaces through which air and heat move</note> are blocked, and the fire is quenched. On the other hand, fires made of bitumen <note>original: *Bitumen*; a naturally occurring, oily petroleum substance like asphalt or tar</note> (which is not a porous material) burn even in the water, as we also see happen with lime. Finally, great fires are actually nourished by water <note>A reference to the observation that adding small amounts of water to intense fires can cause a flare-up or "water-gas" reaction</note>. We also see that there is sometimes hot and sometimes cold water, not only in rivers but also breaking out of springs <note>original: "fountains"</note>, depending on how it is influenced. Yet, it may not be hidden in the meantime that air is more...

XXVII. *The water at first covered the earth entirely; but (on the third day of the creation) it was gathered into certain channels (which are called seas, lakes, pools, rivers, etc.).*

That this was done at the command of God, Moses testifies in these words: "Let the waters be gathered together into one place, that the dry land may appear" (Genesis 1:9). But David (relating the process of the creation) describes the manner also in Psalm 104:6–9: *That thunders were raised, by which the mountains rose up and the valleys sank down, and the waters were carried steeply down into their channels; and that in this way, a boundary was set for them, so that they might not return to cover the earth.* 

From this, it is very likely that the uncovering of the surface of the earth was caused by an earthquake. That earthquake was produced by fire trapped <note>original: "sunk"</note> within the earth which, fighting against the cold concentrated there <note>original: "conglobated"; gathered into a mass</note>, shook the earth and either caused it to swell in various places or tore it apart. This is how the rises and falls in the surface of the earth (that is, mountains and valleys) were created; but

This humble theory <note>original: "pious conjecture"</note> will remain valid as long as no more probable interpretation of this Scripture can be provided. 

And why use many words? Common sense proves that mountains are certainly high, while valleys and plains are low. Therefore, by necessity, it must have been arranged this way at some point. **XXX. The sea is a single body of water. However, because it winds <note>original: "insinuates"</note> into the continents here and there, like strong arms, it has been given several names in different places.**

That great sea surrounding the earth is called the Ocean. Those "arms" that divide the continents are called bays or gulfs. It is likely that the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the earth covered by water is just as uneven as the land we live on. Specifically, in some places, there are vast plains; in other places, there are valleys and depths; and in still others, there are mountains and hills. If these peaks stand above the water, they are called islands, but if they are hidden beneath the water, they are called shoals <note>original: "shelves"; meaning submerged sandbanks or reefs</note>.

## Of the Earth.

**XXXIII. The Earth is the most dense body in the world, acting as the dregs and sediment <note>original: "settling"</note> of all matter.**

Therefore, it is thick <note>original: "gross"</note>, opaque, cold, and heavy.

**XXXIV. It hangs in the middle of the universe, surrounded by air on every side.**

Because it is surrounded on all sides by the heavens and is pressed by their heat from every direction, it has nowhere to go or rest except in the equilibrium <note>original: "aquilibrium"</note> of the universe.

**XXXV. The Earth is round in every direction.**

It still retains the shape it first received from the light of heaven revolving around it. The only exceptions are that in some places it is raised into mountains and hills by the thunder sent into its depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> on the third day <note>A reference to the third day of Creation in Genesis, where the dry land was separated from the waters.</note>; in other places, it is pressed down into valleys and plains to allow for the flow of rivers. However, these features do not significantly hinder its overall roundness <note>original: "globosity"</note>.

**XXXVII. *The earth’s surface is flat in some places and mountainous in others, but inside it is solid in some places and hollow in others.***

These must be considered the work of the thunder sent into the earth on the third day of Creation, which, by penetrating and piercing the earth’s depths <note>original: "bowels"</note>, tore them open. Currently, there are not only spacious caves and holes within the earth, but also an infinite number of narrow veins and pores, as is plain enough from experience.

## CHAPTER VII.
### *Of Vapours.*

**I**f the Light of Heaven had accomplished nothing else upon matter but to melt it together into the forms of the Elements—as it was variously rarefied <term>rarefied: thinned out or made less dense</term> or condensed—the world would have remained empty of other living creatures. But it does not stop passing through the Elements themselves to scorch

<meta>catchword: scorch</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the exploration of the Earth's interior, attributing subterranean fire to the remnants of creation-era lightning, and begins a chapter on how celestial light interacts with matter to create vapours.</summary>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the end of Chapter 6 regarding how celestial light acts upon the elements to transform them into new substances.</meta>
