scorch them; and by scorching them, to thin <note>original: "attenuate"</note> them; and by thinning them, to dissolve <note>original: "resolve"</note> them into *vapours*. From these vapours, when condensed again, many different species of things are produced <note>original: "progenerated"</note>. Now, the nature of vapours shall be explained in the following aphorisms <term>aphorisms: short, concise statements of a principle or truth</term>.

### I. *Vapour is an element thinned <note>original: "rarified"</note>, mixed with another element.*

For example: what is the vapour of water but water thinned and scattered in the air? What is smoke but an exhalation of wood or other matter that has been dissolved?

### II. *Vapour is generated from the denser <note>original: "grosser"</note> elements—earth, water, and air—as well as from all mixed bodies.*

Regarding water, the matter is obvious. For when placed over a fire, it evaporates visibly. When placed in the sun, it evaporates perceptibly, because even entire pools, rivers, and lakes are dried up bit by bit by the heat of the sun. That the earth also exhales, you may know through your senses if you put a clump <note>original: "clot"</note> of earth into a dish (made of clay or pewter <term>pewter: a gray alloy of tin with copper and antimony</term>) and pour water onto it repeatedly, letting it evaporate with heat until nothing is left—neither the water nor the clay. For what has become of the clump? It has certainly been turned

into air, along with the particles <note>original: "parts"</note> of the water.

The vapour of air is invisible, yet it is evident that it exists. 

1. In a living body, where everyone acknowledges that there are evaporations through the skin and the hair. For the vapours that escape—what are they but the vapours of the internal vapours, which are far more subtle <note>original: "subtle" (here meaning refined or thin)</note> than the vapours of water? 

2. Fruits, herbs, spices, and so on, even when they are dried—truly, very dry—release an odor from themselves. Now, what is an odor but an exhalation <term>exhalation: a release of gas or vapor</term>? But in this case, it is not a watery exhalation (since there is nothing watery left in them); therefore, it is an airy one.

There is no doubt that mixed bodies <term>mixed bodies: substances composed of multiple elements</term> produce vapour, because the elements they are made of also produce vapour. This includes not only soft bodies (such as sulfur, salt, herbs, or flesh), but also the very hardest substances. For how could a thunderbolt <note>historical belief held that "thunderstones" or physical bolts were forged in the atmosphere from earthy vapours</note> be generated in the clouds if stony vapours did not rise into the cloud? 

Furthermore, it is certain that stones exposed to the air for many years (as seen in high towers) become porous. How could this happen except by evaporation? And what is the melting of metals but a type of evaporation <note>original: "vaporation"</note>? For although the metal returns to its solid state <note>original: "consistency"</note>, yet not

### III *Heat is the efficient cause of vapour*
Wherever heat spreads, it thins the matter of bodies and turns it into vapour. 

For this is the perpetual power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of heat: to rarify, thin, and spread.

### IV *The entire world is full of vapour.*

For heat, the creator <note>original: "begetter"</note> of vapours, is never missing; so that the world is nothing else but a great <term>Vaporary</term> <note>Vaporary: a room or vessel for steaming; a hot-house or sauna</note>, or stove. For the earth always nourishes an infinite supply of vapours in its depths <note>original: "bowels"</note>; the sea boils daily with internal vapours, and the air is stuffed full of them everywhere. We shall see later that even the sky is not entirely free from them. Furthermore, the living bodies of animals and plants are nothing but workshops <note>original: "shops"</note> of vapours, acting as a kind of <term>Alembic</term> <note>Alembic: an early distilling apparatus used by chemists to turn liquids into vapor</note> that are perpetually steaming as long as they have life or heat.

### V *Vapours are generated to produce other things.*

For all things are made of the elements—as is well known—such as stones, herbs, animals, and so on. But because they cannot be made...

unless the elements themselves are first melted <note>original: "founded"; referring to the process of liquefying or casting materials</note>; they must of necessity be melted, which occurs when they are dissolved into vapours and infused in various ways into things to take on different forms. And for this reason, Moses testifies that during the first seven days of the world—when there was not yet any rain—a vapour rose up from the earth to water the whole ground: that is, all things growing out of the earth. *Read with attention, Genesis 2:4, 5, 6.*

### VI *Vapours are the material for all bodies.*

For who does not know that waters and oils are gathered from the vapours of <term>alembics</term>? <note>alembic: an early chemical apparatus used for distilling liquids through evaporation and condensation</note> Who does not also see that smoke in a chimney turns into soot (which is black dust)? Indeed, that soot sticks to the walls of chimneys and turns into a stone-like hardness. In the same way, it will appear clearer than the light at midday that clouds, rain, hail, stones, and herbs are made from the condensed vapours of the elements. Even living creatures themselves—including their blood, flesh, bones, and hair—are nothing but solidified <note>original: "concrete"</note> vapours.

### VII *Vapours are then solidified <note>original: "coagulated"</note>: some into liquid matter (such as water, saliva <note>original: "spittle"</note>, and soft tissue <note>original: "flesh or pulp"</note>), and some into firm matter (such as stones, bones, wood, etc.).*

This is evident because those liquid substances can be turned into vapors, and solid <note>original: "consistent"</note> substances into smoke. This could not happen if they were not composed of them, for everything can only be broken down <note>original: "resolved"</note> into the very substance from which it was made.

### VIII *The movement of vapors in our environment is upward because, among the denser elements, they take on the nature of thinner ones.*

For certainly the vapor of water is thinner than water itself—indeed, thinner than the air itself—which, although it consists of smaller parts, still has them packed together <note>original: "compacted"</note>. Therefore, vapor does not allow itself to be compressed <note>original: "prest"</note> by either water or air, but frees itself, constantly rising. This is why plants grow upward: because the vapor contained <note>original: "included"</note> within them spreads out and tends to move upward.

### IX *One vapor is moist, another dry; one thin, another thick; one mild, another sharp, etc.*

For those qualities that are later found in physical bodies are initially present in their basic forms <note>original: "rudiments"</note>, which are vapors; we know this by experience. For example, dry smoke causes pain in the eyes, whereas a moist vapor does not; in the smoke, you find sharpness. Likewise, do not smells (which are nothing but the exhalations of things) suffi—

Therefore, all qualities are present in vapors to a greater or lesser degree, from which the bodies created from them later acquire a particular character <note>original: "habit"</note> or form.

X *Vapors gathered together, and not solidified <note>original: "coagulated"</note>, cause wind in the air, disturbances in the sea, and earthquakes in the earth.*

### Of Winds

XI *Wind is a flow <note>original: "fluxe"</note> of the air, established in nature for most beneficial purposes.*

For winds are:
1. the brooms <note>original: "besomes"</note> of the world, cleansing the elements and keeping them from rotting <note>original: "putrefying"</note>;
2. the fan of the spirit of life, causing it to grow <note>original: "vegetate"</note> in plants and all living things;
3. the chariots of clouds, rain, smells, and even heat and cold, wherever they need to be carried;
4. finally, they provide powerful motion for human use (such as grinding and sailing).

XII *The common cause of wind is an abundance <note>original: "store"</note> of exhalations in one place, forcing the air to flow elsewhere.*

We may create a kind of wind with our hands in four ways; namely by forcing, compressing, thinning <note>original: "rarifying"</note>, or thickening <note>original: "densifying"</note> air (which will be shown by examples—

This can be shown to children through visual <note>original: "ocular"</note> experiments: for if you move the air with a fan, does it not produce a gust? Therefore, winds are created under the heavens in many ways: either because the air is thinned <note>original: "rarified"</note> by the heat of the Sun and spreads out; or because it contracts itself from being cold and draws in air from elsewhere to fill the empty space; or because a cloud is scattered or falls downward; or because gusts breaking out from the earth somewhere compress the air and make it spread. Finally, wind occurs because one part of the air, being moved, drives other parts before it. 

(For here you must remember what was said before: first, that a drop of water turned into air requires a hundred times more space; and second, that the air is a very fluid and movable element, and therefore moves a long distance even when only lightly pushed.) Nevertheless, it is clear that all these motions of the air originate from vapors. Since the world is a massive globe, it also provides a great supply of gusts, as both the heat of the sun above and the scorching fire underground produce various vapors.

This explains why a wind arises immediately after a great fire (even in still air): specifically, because much solid matter (such as wood, stone, etc.) is broken down into vapors, and the surrounding air is thinned <note>original: "attenuated"</note>.

### XIII Winds in some countries are predictable <note>original: "certain"</note>, coming at a specific time of the year and from a specific coast; others are variable <note>original: "free"</note>, coming from any place.

Note: they call these <term>annual winds</term> <note>original Greek: "ἐτησίας" (etesias)</note>, which is to say they are yearly. But you must note that those Etesian winds are for the most part weak and gentle, and give way to the variable winds.

Those who sail around the **equator** <note>original: "æquator"</note> testify to it. 2. In the seas of *Europe*, when a specific wind stops, they say that a certain gentle breeze is perceived from the east. 3. Therefore, mariners are constantly of the opinion that sailing from east to west is performed most quickly. 4. Lastly, with

### XIV *A gentle wind is called <term>aura</term> <note>original Latin: "aura"; a breeze or gale</note>; a violent wind overthrowing everything it meets is called <term>procella</term> <note>original Latin: "procella"; a storm or tempest</note>; if it is wound into itself, it is called <term>turbo</term> <note>original Latin: "turbo"; a whirlwind</note>.*

It is plain that various <note>original: "sundry"</note> winds may arise in various places at the same time, according to how the matter of vapors <note>original: "exhalations"</note> is provided here and there, and the opportunity for it to turn itself this way or that. This is a battle between winds until the strongest prevails and is carried forward with a terrible violence, knocking down everything in its path. Conversely, winds of equal strength coming from opposite sides create a whirlwind; when neither side will yield, they both whirl upwards in a violent spinning motion <note>original: "gyration"</note>.

->*Of the Sea Tide*<-

XV *The sea tide is the daily flow <note>original: "flux"</note> of the sea toward the shore and its retreat <note>original: "reflux"</note> back again.*

The sea has its flows, which are less unpredictable than those of the air, for it flows only toward the shores and back again the same way; it flows up twice a day and ebbs twice a day. The purpose <note>original: "end"</note> of this is undoubtedly to keep the seawater from rotting <note>original: "putrefying"</note> through that continual motion. But its primary cause <term>efficient cause</term> <note>original: "efficient cause"; the agent or force that actually produces an effect</note>—which was previously considered one of the secrets of nature—is now being investigated through the most reliable principles of <term>natural philosophy</term> <note>original: "natural Philosophy"; the study of nature and the physical universe, a precursor to modern science</note> and more precise observations.

XVI *The cause of the sea tide is vapors*

For it is impossible for water not to be turned into vapors by heat, and it is impossible that these vapors should not seek a path upward toward their natural environment <note>original: "connaturals"; elements of the same nature, in this case, the air</note>. Yet, it is also impossible for them to have an easy escape from the water. This is because the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the water—indeed, its entire mass—is a dense liquid <note>original: "diffused liquor"</note> like molten glass, having fewer pores than earth, wood, or stone. 

Therefore, it is impossible for the water not to swell, rise up, and dash against the sides of the kettle, eventually breaking through in a thousand openings to allow the dancing and evaporating heat to escape. This occurs because of the vapors being generated and multiplied within, striving to move upward—just as we see in a boiling pot. In the same way, the sea swells because of the vapor that multiplies at the bottom of its depths <note>original: "gulfs"</note>. ### XVII *Vapors within the sea are chiefly generated by subterranean fire.*

People commonly attribute this to "celestial fire"—the Sun and the Moon. But that is likely to be as true as saying we might see a pot of water boil when set in the sun, no matter how hot it is. For who has ever seen that? The Sun may "lick" the surface of the water, consuming it little by little and turning it into vapor, but nothing can make it boil at the bottom except for a fire placed underneath it. Therefore, the cause of the vapors within the sea must necessarily be placed underneath: namely, that subterranean fire which the entire nature of physical things <note>original: "inferiour things"; referring to the earthly or sublunary realm below the heavens</note> demonstrates to be shut up there.

### XVIII *The vapors and tides of the sea are provoked by the heat of heaven (the Sun).*

A working man or a traveler sweats easily enough from his internal heat, stirred up <meta>catchword: up</meta>

In the same way, the sea vapors and boils within, yet it follows the harmony of the superior fire <note>The "superior fire" refers to the heat and influence of the stars and the Sun</note> which comes from the stars. God indicated both of these things in the Bible: *Genesis 7:11* and *Deuteronomy 28:23.* The cause of this is the *harmony of fire to fire*—the connection of the celestial fire to the subterranean fire, etc., as it shall appear elsewhere.

### XIX *The Sea flows twice a day, according as the Sun comes and goes.*

As it descends toward the West, it allows them to fall again. The reason the waters swell again at sunset and fall as the Sun hastens toward the East is the same as what occurs in boiling pots. XXI *An earthquake is the shaking of the surface <term>superficies</term> of the earth in any country; arising from subterranean vapors <term>exhalations</term>, gathered together in great abundance and seeking a way out.*

Therefore, it does not stop until the said vapors are either scattered through the cavities of the earth or else break forth.

XXII *Earthquakes are sometimes so terrible <note>original: "horrible"</note> that they overthrow <note>original: "subvert"</note> cities, mountains, and islands with a hideous bellowing, howling, and crashing.*

These frightening effects lead us to suspect that those vapors are mixed then, much like the ones that cause thunder in a cloud. This suggests they are caused not simply by the force <note>original: "blast"</note> of the vapors, but by their burning, so that they are a kind of subterranean lightning. # CHAPTER VIII.
## On Concrete Substances: Namely, Stars,
## Meteors, and Minerals.

I **A** *concrete thing is a vapor that has solidified <term>coagulated</term> and is gifted <note>original: "endued"</note> with a specific form.*

<summary>This page concludes the author's explanation of earthquakes, which he attributes to the combustion of subterranean vapors similar to underground lightning, and introduces the study of "concrete substances"—solid forms like stars and minerals created from condensed vapors.</summary>
<keywords>earthquakes, subterranean vapors, seismic activity, natural philosophy, concrete substances, stars, minerals, meteorology</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of "concrete substances" from the end of the previous chapter, moving into the specific causes (cold and heat) and classifications (celestial, aerial, aquatic, and terrestrial) of these solidified forms.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>

For example: soot, clouds, snow, etc.
Note that these names, *concrete* and *concretion* <note>original: "concreture"</note>, are new, yet fit to describe this level <note>original: "degree"</note> of creation <note>original: "creatures"</note>, which involves nothing but solidification <term>coagulation</term> and shape <note>original: "figure"</note>.

### II
The primary cause of the solidification <term>concretion</term> of vapors is cold, which, wherever it finds a vapor, condenses and solidifies <term>coagulates</term> it.
This is evident in alembics <term>distilling apparatuses</term>, where the vapor—raised by heat and carried into the highest part of the device where it is cold—turns back <note>original: "resolves itself"</note> into water. To that end, distillers occasionally wash the uppermost cap of the alembic with cold water and make the pipes, through which the condensed <note>original: "concrete"</note> liquid drips <note>original: "distils"</note>, pass through a vessel of water. Yet heat also helps the solidification <term>concretion</term> of things by consuming the thinner part of the substance and forcing the rest to harden, which we see happen in the formation <note>original: "generation"</note> of metals.

### III
Some solidified substances <note>original: "concretes"</note> are *ethereal*, others *aerial*, others *watery*, and others *earthly*.
Specifically, this is because some are formed in the sky, such as stars; others in the air, such as clouds, etc.; others in water, such as bubbles, etc.; and others in the earth, such as stones, etc. Each of these will be considered separately.

It categorizes these substances into four types based on their location of origin: the heavens, the air, the water, or the earth.</summary>

### IV
*Ethereal solidified forms <term>concretes</term> are stars and comets.*

### V
*Stars are fiery globes, full of light and heat, with which the sky glitters on every side.*

The beauty <note>original: "ornament"</note> of the world required this, so that hanging lamps would not be missing from such a majestic palace. This was also necessary for the lower <note>original: "inferiour"</note> world, which is addressed in the following aphorism <term>aphorisme</term> <note>a concise statement of a scientific principle or truth</note>. We categorize stars as solidified forms <term>concretes</term> because it is certain that they are composed of matter and light.

### VI
*Stars were produced in such great numbers out of a very great necessity.*

Specifically:
1. To warm the earth with varying temperatures.
2. To create the varied harmony of seasons and time.
3. To instill <note>original: "inspire"</note> a variety of forms into living creatures.

For such great variety could not be introduced into the lower world without a corresponding variety in celestial things.

### VII
*God placed the greatest number of stars in the highest heaven all around, so that they might shine upon <note>original: "irradiate"</note> the earth from every side, and carry their sphere about with a rapid motion of heat.*

Regarding this starry sphere, consider these following aphorisms:

1. That the movement of this sphere is completed within the space of twenty-four hours.
2. And because that motion is circular, it

6 That the distance of this starry sphere <note>The outer shell of the universe containing the fixed stars.</note> from the earth is found to be above two hundred thousand semidiameters <note>A semidiameter is the radius of the Earth.</note> of the earth, and a semidiameter of the earth contains 3,600 of our miles.

### VIII *A very great portion of most ardent light is gathered into a ball <note>original: "conglobated"</note> in the sun, so that it may seem to be the only fountain of light and heat.*

For were it not for the *sun*, we would have perpetual night despite all the rest of the stars; since even at high noon, we are in darkness immediately if the *sun* is merely covered.

->Now touching the *sun*, these following axioms are to be noted:<-

1 That it was made so large as to be sufficient both to illuminate the whole world and to heat and cause evaporation <note>original: "vaporate"</note> upon the whole earth: that is, 160 times larger than the earth.

2 That it is elevated to such a distance from the earth as to serve its purpose, so as neither to burn the earth nor leave it destitute. *Psalm 19, verse 7* <note>In modern Bibles, the description of the sun’s heat and circuit is found in Psalm 19:6: "there is nothing hid from the heat thereof."</note>; for it is placed almost in the middle space between the starry sphere and the earth.

3 That it is carried with a slower motion than the stars in their highest sphere. For whereas it seems to be turned about equally, just as the starry sphere is, yet it is every day

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Sun's movement relative to the stars, explaining how its gradual lag creates the solar year and how its path along the Zodiac creates the seasons.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

left behind by almost a degree (out of the 360 degrees in the sphere's full circuit). Because of this, over the course of 365 days, the sun travels across the entire sphere as if it were moving backward, and after that many days, it returns to the same star again. This is what we call the *time of a year, or a solar year.*

4 To ensure that the sun serves all parts of the earth with its light and heat in turn, this slowing of its movement <note>original: "retardation"</note> does not occur simply through the middle of the world along the *Equator*. Instead, it moves along the *Zodiac*, tilting toward the North on one side and toward the South on the other. This explains why the year is divided into four parts (*Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter*) and why day lengths are unequal for those living away from the equator <note>original: "equinoctiall"</note>. When the sun moves toward the North, it creates summer there with the longest days, and the opposite occurs in the South. The more directly the sun is overhead <note>original: "vertical"</note> to any part of the earth, the more it heats it, due to the direct angle <note>original: "incidence"</note> and the reflection <note>original: "repercussion"</note> of its rays.

XI *Three of the planets—Saturn* (♄), *Jupiter* (♃), *and Mars* (♂)—*are above the sun. Venus* (♀), *Mercury* (☿), *and the moon* (☽) *are below it. In this way, they are arranged in a very orderly <note>original: "decent"</note> manner, as if surrounding the sides of their King.*

It is likely that the stars are held higher or lower in the heavens for the same reason that clouds float in the air or wood floats in water: that is, according to their different degrees of density or lightness <note>original: "rarity"</note>. ### XIII *The higher any planet is and the closer it is to the highest sphere, the faster it moves; the lower it is and the closer to the earth, the slower it moves.*

For Saturn, because he is next to the eighth sphere <note>The "eighth sphere" refers to the firmament of fixed stars</note>, is rolled about almost as quickly as it is. Yet, he also lags behind <note>original: "fals back"</note> bit by bit, so that he travels through the Zodiac—moving backward <note>This refers to the planet's "proper motion" from west to east, which appears as a slow lagging against the daily 24-hour rotation of the stars from east to west</note>—over the course of almost thirty years. Jupiter completes this in twelve years; Mars in almost two; the Sun (as was said) in one year; Venus circles <note>original: "encompasseth"</note> the Sun in 583 days; Mercury in 115 days. The Moon, because she is the slowest of all, lags behind <note>The "slowest" here refers to the Moon's progress relative to the daily rotation; she falls behind the stars much faster than any other planet, losing about 12-13 degrees of ground every day</note>...

### XIV *The upper planets follow the Sun in such a way that when they approach near to him, they move to their highest position; as they move away from the Sun, they sink lower toward the Earth.*

For this reason, both their size <note>original: "magnitude"</note> and their motion appear to change to our eyes. When they are nearer to the Earth, they seem larger; but when they are further away, they seem smaller. Furthermore, the higher they are, the slower they move, and in that state they are called **direct**; the lower they descend, the faster they move, so that they appear either **stationary** (keeping pace for some weeks with the same fixed stars) or else **retrograde** <note>meaning they appear to move backward against the backdrop of the stars</note>, sometimes moving faster than the stars in their course.

### XV *Venus and Mercury do not move away from the Sun, except to either side: Venus by 47 degrees and Mercury by 23 degrees. Thus, they sometimes go before the Sun, sometimes follow after him, and sometimes lie hidden within his rays.*

Note: When **Venus** is the morning star and precedes the Sun, she is called **Phosphorus** <note>original Greek: φωσφόρος; translates to "Light-bringer"</note> or **Lucifer**; when she is the evening star, she is called **Hesperus** <note>Greek for "Evening"</note>.

fiery and heat-producing <note>original: "calefactive"</note>; <term>Saturn</term> <note>symbol: ♄</note> is pale and very cold-producing <note>original: "frigifactive"</note>; <term>Jupiter</term> <note>symbol: ♃</note> and <term>Venus</term> <note>symbol: ♀</note> possess a gentle light; <term>Mercury</term> <note>symbol: ☿</note> is changeably sparkling; and the <term>Moon</term> <note>symbol: ☽</note> shines only with a borrowed light—about which more will be said shortly.

**Note:** The stars and planets do not sparkle because of their great distance (for if they did, <term>Saturn</term> would sparkle more than *Mercury*, whereas we find the opposite), but rather because of their flaming nature. Since fire or light cannot remain still, the polar stars sparkle the most because they are moved the least by the common motion of the heavens.

### XVII *Because the Moon is near to the Earth and situated in dense air, she moves most slowly; her body is also thick and dark, like a spherical cloud.*

For she is not distant from the Earth by more than 60 of the Earth's radii <note>original: "semidiameters"</note>.

Because of her opacity, the Moon does not shine by her own light—or if she does, it is very weak. However, on the side illuminated by the Sun, she shines like a mirror <note>original: "looking glass"</note>, while the other half remains dark.

**Note:** *Because the Moon was intended to rule the night, she was given a weak and borrowed light. Furthermore, because she was appointed to mark shorter periods of time (months), her motion* <meta>The text continues on the next page with the word "different."</meta>

different from the Sun was given to her, so that by her departure from the Sun and by her return, she might mark <note>original: "designe"</note> the progress of the months. To make this more obvious, she was placed below the Sun so that she might appear to us with her face illuminated in various <note>original: "divers"</note> ways. For when she travels with the Sun in the same sign of the Zodiac, she does not appear to us; this is because her illuminated face is turned toward the Sun, while her dark face is toward us. But when she is opposite to the Sun, and we view her from the same side that faces the Sun, we see her entire luminous face. Finally, in the intermediate positions, we see her increasing or decreasing in light, depending on whether she turns her illuminated face toward us or away from us, due to the different positions she occupies in relation to the Sun and ourselves.

From this, it appears that the Sun is not darkened in the same way as the Moon

is. For the Moon is really obscured—that is, deprived of light—because it has fallen into the Earth's shadow. However, the Sun is not actually deprived of its light; it is merely covered from our perspective, so that it cannot enlighten the Earth with its rays at that moment. Therefore, the Earth is more truly eclipsed than the Sun <note>The author argues that in a solar eclipse, the Sun remains bright but the Earth is placed in shadow, making "Earth eclipse" a more technically accurate term than "solar eclipse."</note>.

Now God ordained eclipses for three reasons:
1. So that we might understand that all our light comes from the Sun.
2. So that the size <note>original: "magnitude"</note> of the Sun and Moon <note>original: "Luminaries"</note> and of the Earth might be discovered.
3. To find the true longitude of countries; though this belongs to astronomers, and the previous point to geographers.

->*Of Comets.*<-

XX *Comets are secondary <note>original: "accessory"</note> stars that sometimes shine and then disappear <note>original: "go out again"</note>; for the most part, they appear with tails or "bushes of hair" <note>The word "comet" comes from the Greek "kometes," meaning "long-haired."</note>.*

We classify them among the heavens and stars, not among the air and meteors <note>In the early modern period, "meteor" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including rain, wind, or rainbows.</note>. This is because they are not created in sublunary <term>sublunary: the region below the Moon's orbit, traditionally thought to be the realm of changing weather and decay.</term> places (as *Aristotle* thought), but in the highest heavens, even above the Sun. This is shown by: 
1. Their motion, which is always swifter than the Moon itself. 
2. Their parallax <term>parallax: the apparent change in the position of a celestial object when viewed from different points on Earth. A smaller parallax indicates the object is further away.</term>, which is less than the Moon’s, and sometimes non-existent.

XXI *Comets are not ignited vapors; rather, they are the reflection of the Sun’s light upon vapors that have risen to a great height.*

<summary>This page explains that solar eclipses are actually "Earth eclipses" and lists the scientific and theological reasons for eclipses, then argues that comets are celestial bodies located far above the Moon rather than atmospheric events.</summary>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that comets are celestial bodies located in the heavens, rather than atmospheric phenomena (meteors) as previously believed by Aristotelians.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

The first point is easily proved. For if a comet were a lit vapour <note>original: "kindled"</note>, it could not last for even half an hour. (Because nothing can be ignited except for sulfurous matter, which is consumed in a moment—as is seen in gunpowder, lightning, a chasm <note>original: "Chasme"; a term used in early modern meteorology to describe an opening or void appearing in the sky, often associated with the aurora.</note>, a falling star, etc.) But historical records show that comets have lasted as long as three years. 

The second point is shown because: 
1. Comets cast a tail away from the Sun, just as the Moon casts a shadow (for those dry vapours are not an opaque <note>original: "opacous"</note> body like the Moon, but are semi-transparent <note>original: "semidiaphanous"</note>). 
2. They are eclipsed by the shadow of the Earth just like the Moon (as *Campanella* <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), an Italian philosopher and astronomer who defended Galileo.</note> testifies). This would not happen if they burned with their own internal fire.

W.* <note>These initials likely refer to a commentator or the author providing a specific observation.</note> Regarding reports of sulfurous matter, or a stone, that supposedly fell from a burning comet: if this is true, it should be considered to have been formed from some fiery meteors <note>In the 16th and 17th centuries, "meteor" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including weather, lightning, or falling rocks.</note>, not from an actual comet.

### XXII *The purposes <note>original: "ends"</note> of comets are to demonstrate:*
*1. That the entire heaven moves, not just the stars.*
*2. That the heavens are liquid and permeable <note>original: "transmeable"</note>, not hard like crystal <note>A reference to the "crystalline spheres" of older astronomy, which imagined the heavens as solid, nested shells.</note>.*
*3. That vapours ascend that high, and that there are changes <note>original: "mutations"</note> everywhere in this visible world.*

I am speaking of vapours, whether they exhale from...

### *Of Aerial Concretes <note>Substances formed by the gathering together of matter.</note>, that is, Meteors.*

XXIII *Due to the perpetual flow of vapors <note>original: "exhalations"</note> into the air from all the elements, many things are daily formed <note>original: "concreted"</note> there, though they last only a short time.*

For the air is full of vapors, even when it seems clear. For it cannot be so pure here near the earth that it does not always have something watery, oily, or salty mixed in with it. Things formed from these were anciently called "Meteors" because they are made on high; for *meteoros* <note>original: μετέωρος</note> signifies "high" or "raised up."

XXIV *Watery meteors are made from moist vapors; fiery meteors are made from dry ones.*

XXV *Watery meteors include mists, clouds, rain, hail, snow, dew, and frost.*

We must examine each of these individually to see how they are formed.

XXVI *A mist is a watery vapor that is partially condensed <note>original: "half concrete"</note>; because of its density, it cannot rise upward and instead creeps along the ground.*

XXVII *A cloud is a gathering together of thin*

thin vapors, raised upward into the highest part of the air.

They gather together most often over the sea and stagnant waters because that is where most <term>exhalations</term> <note>original: "exhalations"; vapors or gases rising from the earth or water.</note> are produced. From there, they are driven through various parts of the world by the winds and increased by <term>exhalations</term> arising elsewhere. Consequently, in every region, rain usually comes from the direction nearest to the sea; for us, it comes from the West.

*XXVIII Rain is the <term>resolution</term> <note>original: "resolution"; the process of a substance breaking down or returning to a liquid state.</note> of a cloud into water and its falling in drops.*

Note 1: That <term>resolution</term> is always caused by the <term>condensation</term> of the <term>vapor</term>, but the efficient cause <note>The direct or immediate agent that produces an effect.</note> of its condensation is not always the same. For sometimes cold condenses a <term>vapor</term>, as we see in the head and pipe of an <term>alembic</term> <note>An early laboratory apparatus used for distillation, consisting of a gourd-like container and a cooling cap.</note> (which must be kept cool). Other times, the cause is the compression itself, or <term>conspissation</term> <note>original: "conspissation"; the act of thickening or making something dense.</note>, as is clearly seen on the ceilings of bathhouses or the lid of a boiling pot. 

Neither of these causes is missing when rain is formed. Since the middle region of the air <note>In traditional natural philosophy, the atmosphere was divided into three layers; the middle region was believed to be perpetually cold.</note> is cold, and the cloud is pressed together by the vapors constantly ascending from below, it must necessarily dissolve. This is also why intense heat...

*Note 2.* That rain is better for fields and gardens than river water, because it has a kind of "fatness" <note>original: "fatneſſe"; here meaning nutrients or fertile organic matter</note> mixed with it from the evaporations of the earth, minerals, plants, and animals. Through this, it gives the earth a most profitable <term>tincture</term> <note>original: "tincture"; a restorative essence or nourishing quality.</note>.

*Note 3.* Sometimes worms, small fish, frogs, etc., fall with the rain. These, as is very likely, are suddenly generated within the cloud from vapors of the same nature gathered together, by virtue of a "living spirit" <note>Early modern natural philosophy often held that life could spontaneously arise from inanimate matter through a vital "spirit" or life-force.</note> mixed into them; just as in the beginning, at the Command of God, the waters brought forth creeping things and fish in a moment.

### XXIX Hail is frozen rain.

For when the sun's beams in the greatest heat of summer have driven all cold away from the earth and into the <term>middle region of the air</term> <note>The layer of the atmosphere traditionally believed to be perpetually cold.</note>, it happens that this intense cold violently hardens the drops of rain passing through it and forces them to turn to ice. Therefore, hail cannot be <term>procreated</term> <note>original: "procreated"; produced or formed.</note> in winter, as the cold then remains near the earth rather than high above.

### XXX Snow is the breaking down of a cloud into...

...into very small drops, and furthermore, a thickening of them with a gentle cold.

*Note 1.* It falls only in winter because the vapors are not lifted up by the sun’s weak rays as far as the <term>middle region</term> <note>The "middle region" of the air was thought by early scientists to be the layer where clouds and weather formed, characterized by perpetual cold.</note> (which is the cold region). Here, then, near the earth, the <term>resolution</term> <note>original: "resolution"; the process of a substance breaking down or changing state, in this case, a cloud turning into snow.</note> occurs in a milder cold, and furthermore, the <term>congelation</term> <note>original: "congelation"; the process of freezing or thickening into a solid.</note> is very mild.

*Note 2.* The whiteness of snow comes from the joining together of the parts of the water; the same thing happens in crushed ice and in the froth of water.

### XXXI *Dew is a thin vapor (or perhaps the air itself) attracted by the leaves of plants and condensed into water by their coldness.*

For it is found nowhere else but upon plants, and that during the heat of summer when the plants are colder than the air itself. Now, this results in a great benefit to the plants; for by that means they are moistened at the very driest time of the year. Therefore, dew is also produced in those countries that see no rain.

### XXXII *Frost is frozen dew.*

Therefore, there is no frost except in winter, when cold prevails because of the sun's absence.

### *Of Fiery Meteors*

Fiery meteors are those which arise from fatty fumes kindled in the air. There are seven principal kinds: *a falling star*, *a flying dragon*, *lightning*, *flying sparks*, *ignis fatuus* <note>original: "ignis fatuus"; literally "foolish fire," commonly known as a will-o'-the-wisp.</note>, *a torch*, and *ignis lambens* <note>original: "ignis lambens"; literally "licking fire," a type of atmospheric electricity—similar to St. Elmo’s Fire—that appears to glow around objects without consuming them.</note>.

### XXXIII *A falling star is a fatty and sticky fume, ignited at its upper end by <term>antiperistalsis</term> <note>original: "antiperistafis"; a philosophical concept where a quality is intensified by being surrounded by its opposite, such as heat being compressed and ignited by the surrounding cold air.</note> (that is, the resistance of the surrounding cold). The flame follows its fuel and is carried downward until the fuel runs out and it is extinguished.*

These are to be seen every clear night, and more often in winter than in summer. You may see a similar spectacle if you ignite the fatty smoke of a blown-out candle by holding another lit candle above it. This falling star is made of a dense vapor; because of its density, it holds together like a cord. It burns so violently that if it falls upon a man, it can burn through his clothing. Whichever direction it moves, it foretells that wind will come from that part of the sky.

### XXXIV *A flying dragon is a long, thick, fatty fume that is lifted up in all its parts. For this reason, when it is ignited, it does not dart downward but moves sideways like a dragon or a sparkling beam.*

<summary>This page defines and classifies "fiery meteors," explaining phenomena like falling stars and "flying dragons" as ignited atmospheric vapors. It provides early scientific explanations for their motion and composition, including the use of the candle-smoke analogy to describe ignition.</summary>

<keywords>fiery meteors, falling stars, flying dragons, antiperistalsis, ignis fatuus, ignis lambens, atmospheric vapors, weather lore, early modern physics</keywords>

This type of meteor is not seen very often; therefore, those who are ignorant of natural causes think that the Devil is flying.

### XXXV *Lightning is fire ignited within a cloud. Fleeing from the opposing cold, it breaks out with a horrible noise and usually casts its flame all the way down to the earth.*

The world is the <term>alembic</term> <note>original: "alembick"; an apparatus used in distilling, consisting of a gourd-shaped vessel and a cap or head. The author uses it as a metaphor for how the sun's heat "distills" vapors from the earth.</note> of nature, and the air is the cap of this alembic. The sun is the fire. The earth, water, minerals, plants, and other materials are the substances which, being softened by this fire, perpetually exhale vapors upward. 

Thus, salty, sulfurous, and nitrous vapors ascend. Once wrapped up in clouds, they produce various effects. For example: *When sulfurous exhalations are mixed with nitrous ones* (the first being of a very hot nature, the second very cold), they tolerate each other only until the sulfur catches fire. 

As soon as that happens, the same effect follows as with gunpowder (which is also composed of sulfur and <term>niter</term> <note>original: "nitre"; also known as saltpeter, a key ingredient in explosives.</note>): a conflict, a sudden rupture, a noise, and a violent expulsion of matter. For from this, a sticky, flaming matter is cast out, which immediately ignites anything it touches that is prone to catch fire. Striking into the earth, it turns

XXXVI *Flying sparks* are sulfurous vapors scattered into many small parts and ignited.

These are rarely seen, as are the phenomena that follow.

XXXVII <term>*Ignis fatuus*</term> <note>original: "Ignis fatuus"; Latin for "foolish fire," commonly known as a will-o'-the-wisp. This was thought to be ignited swamp gas that led travelers astray.</note> is a thick and sticky vapor which, because of its density, does not rise far from the earth. Once ignited, it wanders here and there, sometimes leading travelers out of their way and into danger.

XXXVIII *A torch* is a vapor similar to the one above, but thinner, and therefore it rises higher. Once ignited, it burns for a while like a candle or lamp.

XXXIX <term>*Ignis lambens*</term> <note>original: "Ignis lambens"; Latin for "licking fire." A phenomenon where light appears to flicker over a person or animal, often caused by static electricity or bioluminescence.</note> is a thick exhalation coming from a living body that has been heated by motion and ignited at the head or near it.

It sometimes happens to men and horses who are breathing heavily after running that the burning vapors they exhale are turned into flames.

### *Of Apparent Meteors* <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon. "Appearing" or "apparent" meteors are those created by light and reflection rather than physical fire or matter.</note>

Apparent meteors are images of things in the clouds, created in various ways by the light falling upon them. Among this type, the following are observed:

[the] following seven are recognized: <term>*Chasma*</term> <note>original: "Chasma"; Greek for a yawning gulf or gap.</note>, <term>*Halo*</term> <note>original: "Halo"; Greek for a circular threshing floor.</note>, <term>*Parhelion*</term> <note>original: "Parelius"; a mock-sun.</note>, <term>*Paraselene*</term> <note>original: "Paraselene"; a mock-moon.</note>, *Rods*, *Colors*, and the *Rainbow*.

XL. **Chasma** (a pit) is the hollowness of a cloud that gives the appearance of a great hole.

It occurs because of a shadow in the middle of a cloud, the edges of which are illuminated. You may see a similar effect at night by holding a candle near a wall that has any indentation in it, even if the wall is white.

XLI. **Halo** (a threshing floor) is a luminous circle formed when the vapors beneath the sun or moon are illuminated by the rays of that celestial body.

You may see the same effect at night in a bathhouse or any other steamy place, surrounding a burning candle. It is most often seen around the moon because the sun, with its stronger rays, usually either pierces through or scatters the cloud.

XLII. **Parhelion** <note>original: "Parelius"; a mock-sun, caused by the refraction of sunlight through ice crystals.</note> (a false sun) is the image of the sun appearing upon a bright cloud placed by its side.

In the same way, if you stand on the opposite bank of a river, you will see two suns: the true one in the heavens, and the other reflected in the water. Sometimes three suns are seen, if two of

**XLIII** **Paraselene** <note>original: "Paraselene"; Greek for "beside the moon," commonly known as a mock-moon or moon dog.</note> (a false moon) is the image of the moon reflected in the same way, upon a cloud positioned to the side.

**XLIV** **Rods** <note>likely referring to crepuscular rays, which are sunbeams that appear to radiate from a single point in the sky.</note> are beams of the sun covered by a cloud, yet shining through the thin parts of the cloud and stretching toward the earth like rods.

**XLV** **Colors** are those that appear diverse in a cloud, depending on the various ways it is turned toward the sun and toward us; as a result, the cloud seems sometimes yellow, and sometimes red and fiery.

**XLVI** Lastly, the **Rainbow** is a <term>halo</term> <note>In this context, the author defines a halo as a luminous circle; see page 135.</note> opposite to the sun or moon in a dewy cloud, representing a bow of various colors.

For there are lunar rainbows also. Now, it is plain that the rainbow is an appearing <term>meteor</term> <note>In historical science, a "meteor" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon (weather, light, etc.), not specifically a space rock.</note> simply because it moves backward and forward along with the eye of the observer. Because of this, it appears to be in different places to different people looking at it from various locations, just as the reflection or brightness of the sun appears to move for those walking up and down along a shore. I say that it is *a meteor similar to a halo* because it is equally circular. <meta>This page continues the discussion of atmospheric optics, specifically comparing the geometry of halos and rainbows, and explaining why rainbows appear as arcs rather than full circles from the ground.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>

[...the center of the luminary and the cen]ter of our eye are in one straight line, just as in a rainbow. The only difference is that in the first (a halo), the <term>luminary</term> <note>original: "luminary"; a light-giving body like the sun or moon.</note> and the eye are at the ends, with the *halo* in the middle. Here, the luminary and the bow are at the ends, and the eye is in the middle. 

Now, a whole circle does not appear in the rainbow because its center, from our perspective, falls upon the earth; thus, only the upper half of the circle appears. If anyone could lift themselves into the cloud, or above the cloud, they would undoubtedly see the whole circle of the rainbow. 

This also explains the clear reason why at the *sun's rising* or *setting*, there appears a *whole semicircle raised straight up toward heaven*; but *when the sun is high, the rainbow appears low*. *Finally, it explains why there can be no rainbow at all when the sun is directly overhead* <note>original: "verticall."</note>. 

The *lunar rainbows* are only pale, like a *halo*. The *solar* rainbow shows forth the most beautiful colors from a stronger light, reflected in various ways from a million <note>original: "thousand thousand"</note> drops (from a dissolving cloud). The colors are arranged in order, just as can be seen in a *crystal prism*. Certainly, the rainbow *was given to us for this very reason: so that we might learn to study the nature of colors*. 

There is also a *secondary rainbow* <note>original: "contra iris"; Latin for "against the rainbow," referring to a double or reflected rainbow.</note>, specifically when the rainbow reflects...

### *On Watery Concretions* <note>original: "Concretes"; referring to physical forms or substances that have solidified or coalesced from water.</note>

XLVII *Watery concretions are: a bubble, foam, ice, and several appearances in the water; also the saltiness of the sea, spring waters, and medicinal waters.*

XLVIII *A bubble is a thin watery skin, filled with air.*

It is made when a small portion of air, thrust down below the water, is carried upward. The water—being somewhat oilier on its surface <note>original: "ſuperficies"</note>—does not allow the air to fly out immediately, but instead covers it with a thin skin like a little bladder. The more oily the water is, the longer the bubbles last. This can be seen in those playful <note>original: "ludicrous"; here meaning "for the purpose of play or sport."</note> round bubbles which boys are accustomed to blow out of water and soap, which fly through the air for a long time without breaking. From the bubble, we learn to what an extreme delicacy <note>original: "ſubtilty"</note> water may be reduced. For the skin of a bubble is a thousand times thinner than the thinnest paper.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "watery concretions" or substances formed from water, moving from the physics of bubbles to foam, ice, reflections, and the salinity of the ocean.</meta>

<page-num>(139)</page-num>

XLIX *Foam is a collection of very small bubbles, raised by the sudden falling of water into water.*

The beating of the water into small parts causes whiteness in the foam, just as ice, wax, pitch, and other things appear whitish when they are beaten <note>original: "beaten"; referring to being crushed or agitated into small particles, which scatters light and makes the substance appear white.</note>. The durability of the foam is also greater in an oily liquid <note>original: "liquour"</note>, such as beer, etc.

L *Ice is water hardened together by cold.*

LI *Watery impressions are images of clouds, birds flying overhead, men, trees, and any things placed in front of them <note>original: "objected"; here meaning placed in the line of sight or reflected.</note>.*

It is known that water is the first mirror <note>original: "mirrour"</note>, receiving the images of all things. This occurs because of the smoothness of its surface <note>original: "superficies"</note>. For light, colored by the objects falling upon the water, cannot be dispersed (as happens in another body with a rough surface); instead, because of the water's exceeding smoothness, it is entirely reflected and presents itself whole with that image to the eye of the viewer. This is the basis of all mirrors. But let us come to physical substances <note>original: "reall concretions"</note> in the water.

LII *The saltiness of the sea comes from subterranean <note>original: "subterrane"</note> fire, which, by heating a bituminous <term>bituminous: relating to bitumen, a naturally occurring oily or tar-like substance</term> material, spreads salty vapors <note>original: "exhalations"</note> through the sea.*

<summary>This page explains the physical formation of foam, ice, and reflections, while also presenting an early scientific theory that the ocean's saltiness is caused by underground fires heating oily materials.</summary>
<keywords>foam, ice, reflection, optics, mirrors, salinity, bitumen, subterranean fire</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the properties of water, specifically addressing the origins of saltiness in the sea and the formation of spring water from subterranean sources.</meta>

<page-num>(140)</page-num>

A saltiness, somewhat bitter and with a kind of oiliness <term>oleosity: an oily or greasy quality</term>, was given to the sea for three reasons:
1. So that the waters might not putrefy <note>original: "putrifie"; to decay or rot</note>.
2. For the more convenient nourishment <note>original: "nutriment"</note> of fishes.
3. For strength to bear the weight <note>original: "burdens"</note> of ships <note>Early modern thinkers correctly observed that saltwater is denser and provides more buoyancy than freshwater.</note>.

Now, the sea is salty—not (as *Aristotle* thought) because the sun's beams draw out the thinner parts of the water and scorch the rest. (For our own fires would do the same, as would the sun in lakes and pools, yet neither happens. In fact, the more saltwater is heated by our fire, the saltier it becomes; but heated freshwater only becomes fresher). Instead, it is because of the heat contained within the depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> of the earth and the deep. When this heat cannot escape <note>original: "exhale"</note>, it sharply scorches the fluid <note>original: "humour"</note> there, so that it turns into something like urine. We see this very same thing in our own bodies (and in all living creatures), for urine and sweat are similarly salty.

LIII *Spring waters are made of vapors condensed in the caverns of the earth, in the same way that drops gather on the lids of pots.*

It is certain that beneath the earth there lies a "great deep" (*Genesis* 7:11). That is, a mighty mass of water spread through the hollows

...hollows of the earth. The fact that these connect with certain gulfs of the ocean is proof that the depth of the sea in some places is completely unsearchable <note>original: "insearchable"; unfathomable</note>.

Therefore, just as vapors rise from the open sea into the air—which, when turned into drops, fall as rain—so the subterranean <note>original: "subterrane"</note> waters are thinned <note>original: "attenuated"</note> by heat beneath the earth and release vapors. These vapors gather in the hollows of the earth, collect into drops, and flow out wherever a passage is opened for them. And this is what the Scripture says:

> *All rivers run into the sea, yet the sea is not full; to the place from which the rivers come, there they return again.*

Ecclesiastes 1:7. From this, we can understand why springs produce fresh water, even though they originate from the bitter and salty waters of the sea. This is because they reach the spring’s source through distillation. For they say that when sea water is distilled—that is, first turned into vapors and then into drops in an <term>alembic</term> <note>an early chemical apparatus used for distilling liquids</note>—it loses its saltiness. By the same logic, the "deep" underground evaporates salty water but nevertheless sends it out from fountains as fresh water. What more needs to be said? For clouds gathered from the vapors...

**LIV** *Medicinal waters are made of the various tinctures of the metals and juices of the earth, from which they receive their healing powers and flavors.*

For example, hot springs or baths are created by bitumen <note>original: "bitumen"; a naturally occurring petroleum or tar-like substance</note> burning within the earth; therefore, they clearly exhale sulfur. On the other hand, sharp-tasting waters have the flavor of iron, copper, vitriol <note>original: "vitriol"; a sulfate of a metal, often used to refer to sulfuric acid</note>, alum, and so on. It is now time to speak of these earthly solids <note>original: "concretes"</note>.

->*Of earthly solids, which are called*<-
->*Minerals.*<-

**LV** *Minerals are earthly solids created from subterranean vapors, such as clods, solidified fluids, metals, and stones.*

These are called minerals from the Hebrew words *min* and *eretz* <note>original: מן and ארץ</note>, as if you should say "from the earth." They are also called *fossils* because they are dug up. The fact that all these are created from subterranean vapors and subterranean fire is shown by the example of our own bodies. In the body, blood, yellow bile <note>original: "choler"</note>, phlegm, black bile <note>original: "melancholy"</note>, urine, saliva, fat, flesh, veins, nerves, membranes, cartilage <note>original: "gristles"</note>, bone, and so on—as well as kidney stones and gallstones <note>original: "the stone and gravell"</note>—are all produced from the vapors of food.

<summary>This page explains the origin of medicinal mineral waters and introduces the study of minerals, arguing that they are formed by subterranean heat and vapors much like the various substances in the human body are formed from food.</summary>
<keywords>medicinal waters, bitumen, sulfur, minerals, fossils, subterranean vapors, humors, biology-geology analogy</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy between the human body and the earth, explaining how minerals are formed within the earth just as bodily substances are formed within a person through heat and digestion.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>

food concocted <note>original: "concocted"; the process of being refined or matured by heat</note> and digested as shall be seen hereafter. Now, just as these parts of ours are formed within the body by internal heat, so minerals are generated in the bowels of the earth, and nowhere else. For the earth—with its deep passages and veins winding in every direction, where infinite vapors are generated and perpetually distilled in a thousand ways—is that great workshop of God. Within it, for the space of so many ages, such things are produced that neither human art can imitate, nor human wit can fully understand.

**LVI** *Clods are dug-up earths, permeated only with richness or some color, and capable of being soaked, such as:*
1. Clay.
2. Marl <note>original: "marle"; a type of soil consisting of clay and lime, often used as fertilizer</note>.
3. Chalk.
4. Red earth.
5. Pigments, or painters' colors (*such as lake, vermilion, ochre, azure or blue, and verdigris*).
6. Fuller's earth <note>original: "fuller's earth"; a clay-like mineral used to clean and thicken wool</note> (in Greek, <term>cleansing earth <note>original: σμῆκτις (smēktis); a soap-like earth</note></term>).
7. Medicinal earth, such as sealed earth <note>original: "sealed earth" (terra sigillata); medicinal clay often stamped with an official seal to certify its origin and quality</note>, *Lemnian, Armenian, Samian, etc.*

These colors seem to be nothing else but the soot of subterranean fumes, distilled in various ways; and those earths are nothing else but a various mixture of fluids, also distilled in different ways and brought to a specific quality.

**LVII** *Solidified juices* <note>original: "concrete juices"; mineral fluids that have hardened into solids</note> *are minerals endowed with*

with a flavor, or some *sharp property* <note>original: "sharp virtue"; a potent or acidic chemical quality</note>, capable of being dissolved or ignited; such as *sulfur*, *niter* <note>original: "niter"; saltpeter</note>, *salt*, *alum*, *vitriol* <note>original: "vitriol"; sulfate minerals</note>, *arsenic* (which painters call orpiment), *antimony* or *stibium*, and *similar substances*.

**Note.** These juices seem to be nothing else but *the richest part of underground liquids* <note>original: "the cream of subterrane liquors"</note> distilled in various ways.

**LVIII** *Metals* are *watery fossils* <note>original: "watery fossiles"; at this time, "fossil" referred to anything dug out of the earth, and "watery" referred to their ability to melt into liquid</note>, capable of being melted, cast, and hammered: such as *gold*, *silver*, *brass* (or copper), *iron*, *tin*, *lead*, and *mercury* <note>original: "quick-silver"</note>.

**Note 1.** That they are generated by fire is sufficiently evidenced by the fact that they are often taken hot out of the veins, so hot that the hand cannot endure touching them. For in winter, when all herbs are white with frost, those plants which grow over the metal veins do not freeze because the hot vapors within prevent solidification <note>original: "concretion"</note>. Similarly, trees reveal the veins of metals by the blueness of their leaves.

**2.** Now, since metals are made of vapors, this is an argument that they are also commonly produced within the clouds themselves. For examples are known, even in our own age, of masses of brass or iron of significant weight falling from heaven <note>The author is likely referring to meteorites</note>.

**3.** That metals are made of watery vapors,

**4.** Mercury <note>original: "Quicksilver"</note> alone is always liquid and never solid—a constant witness to the watery nature of metals. Other metals float upon it because it has the most compressed <note>original: "compacted"</note> substance of all, except for gold. Because of this density, mercury only absorbs gold into itself.

**5.** Whether metals differ in their actual species, or only in their degree of purity, hardness, and heat, we will leave undecided <note>original: "in suspense"</note> for now.

**LIX** *Stones are minerals <note>original: "fossiles"; in this period, "fossil" referred to anything dug out of the earth</note> made of earth, firmly compressed, and capable only of being broken into pieces.*

Bricks and pottery teach us that stones are earth solidified by water and fire; in this case, human art imitates nature. Yet the various forms of stones show that they are not simply solid earth, but a mass formed from various thick <note>original: "grosse"</note> earthly vapors, combined with different mixtures of fluids <note>original: "humours"</note>.

**LX** *Stones are either common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> or precious.*

**LXI** *A common stone is earth that is very firmly compressed. ## Chapter 9
### Of Plants

**S**o much for concretes <term>concretes</term> <note>solid substances formed from various parts; here used to describe inanimate matter like stones or minerals</note>. Here follow plants, which besides their physical form <note>original: "figure"</note> possess life.

1 *A plant is a living concrete <note>original: "vitall concrete"</note> growing out of the earth, such as a tree or an herb.*

Some concretes (stars, meteors, minerals) lack <note>original: "want"</note> life and lie or remain where they were formed. But plants, endowed with an inward vigor, break out of the earth and spread themselves across a flat surface <note>original: "in plano"</note>; for this reason, they were called "plants" <note>likely connecting the word to the Latin "planta," meaning the sole of the foot or a sprout</note>.

<summary>The author concludes the discussion on minerals and stones found in animals before introducing plants as "living concretes" that possess an internal vigor to grow from the earth.</summary>

II *Plants are created <note>original: "generated"</note> both to be an ornament to the earth and to yield nourishment, medicine, and other uses to living creatures.*

For we have enough <note>original: "sufficient"</note> experience in winter of what a sad appearance <note>original: "face"</note> the earth would have if it were not clothed every year with those diverse-colored tapestries of herbs. And where would living creatures get food, medicines, and pleasures if we lacked <note>original: "were destitute of"</note> the roots, leaves, seeds, and fruits of plants—not to mention the benefit <note>original: "commodity"</note> of shade and the infinite uses of wood?

III *The essential parts of a plant are the root, the trunk (or stalk), and the branches or leaves.*

<note>original: "N. w."; Note well</note> The elements, vapors, and solid objects <term>concrete things</term> <note>inanimate substances like minerals or water</note> consist only of uniform <note>original: "similar"</note> parts. For every part and particle of water, earth, vapor, a cloud, or iron, etc., is called—and indeed is—water, earth, vapor, a cloud, or iron, etc. But more perfect bodies (those of plants and living creatures) consist of non-uniform <note>original: "dissimilar"</note> parts (that is, members), every one of which has both its own function <note>original: "office"</note> and its name, differing from the rest. For example: In a plant, the *root* is the part sticking in the ground and sucking in the juice of the earth; the *trunk*...

IV *The Spirit of a plant is called a vegetable or vital spirit, which puts forth its power <note>original: "virtue"</note> in three different ways: in nutrition, growth <note>original: "augmentation"</note>, and reproduction <note>original: "generation"</note>.*

For here that universal spirit (the spirit of life) begins more clearly to display its power, preparing a portion of matter so gently for its purpose that it may make it workable <note>original: "tractable"</note> to perform the functions <note>original: "offices"</note> of life. It is therefore called "vital" in plants, specifically because of its more obvious signs and effects of life. ...fresh nourishment to replace what is lost. To this end, every plant has a body that is either hollow or else pithy and porous, so that the nourishing vapor may pass through and water all the parts. Indeed, everything in a plant—even the very hair or down—is hollow and porous. Therefore, in a man, the head is relieved when the hair is cut, because the <term>smoky vapors</term> <note>original: "fuliginous vapours"; in early modern medicine, these were "sooty" waste products produced by the body's internal heat that needed to be vented.</note> of the brain, or the excess fluids under the skin, evaporate more easily. For the same reason, every plant rests upon its root so that, by sucking the moisture of the earth through its fibers <note>original: "strings"</note>, it may be nourished; therefore, it perishes when it is plucked up. The moisture <note>original: "humour"</note> then, or the rich sap <note>original: "fat juice"</note> of the earth, is a suitable nourishment for plants. Dry earth is not suitable because it cannot pass through the fibers and pores of a plant, nor is water alone, because it cannot be hardened <note>original: "concrete"</note> into a solid body. Therefore, the moisture of the earth—which is a mixture of <term>Mercury</term>, <term>sulfur</term>, and <term>salt</term> <note>In alchemy and early chemistry, these three substances (the *Tria Prima*) were thought to be the fundamental building blocks of all matter.</note>—nourishes plants.

This happens every day when the sun is hot, but the tender parts which grow up are hardened and made solid by the cold of the night. Through these successions of day and night, the plants increase in size all spring and summer long. Now, notice that however much moisture is lifted upward through the stem each day, just as much follows after it by the *principle of continuity* <note>original: "motion of continuity"; the law of physics stating that matter must remain connected to prevent empty space.</note>, lest there should be a vacuum...

Now there is a question: why, when a leaf or a branch is plucked off—even when the trunk <note>original: "stock"</note> is cut in two—does the vital essence <note>original: "spirit"</note> not evaporate <note>original: "exhale"</note>, but instead contains itself and continues to grow? 

**Answer 1:** Because the vital essence has its proper seat fixed in the root, which it does not abandon even though a passage is opened by a wound. In fact, fearing a loss of continuity, it gathers and balls itself together <note>original: "conglobates"</note> when it perceives an opening and the danger of scattering <note>original: "dissipation"</note>. 

**Answer 2:** Because the wound is immediately overspread with the moisture of the plant, which, being hardened by the cold outside air, covers the wound as if with a crust and prevents a total loss <note>original: "expiration"</note> of the essence.

VII. *Generation is a power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of a plant by which it gathers and concentrates <note>original: "conglobates"</note> its vital essence into a specific part of itself, creating a seed or kernel (from which a similar plant may later grow).*

X. *The properties <note>original: "proprieties"</note> of plants are variety, heat, and the tenacity of their spirit.*

### XVII. *Vital spirit* (as also natural spirit) *holds so fast to its matter that it scarcely ever forsakes it.*

This is demonstrated by four examples (in addition to the fact that we see the spirit driven every year by the cold of winter out of the stalks <note>original: "stocks"; the main stems or trunks of plants</note> to be hidden in the root, and to put itself forth again at the beginning of the spring).

1. That however much the matter of fruits or herbs is processed <note>original: "vexed"; meaning physically manipulated or agitated</note>, the spirit still contains itself within. This can be seen in things that are smoked, toasted, roasted, soaked, pulverized, etc., which still retain their potency <note>original: "virtue"; the inherent power or medicinal property of a substance</note>.

2. That even when driven out of the greater part of the matter by the force of fire, the spirit still sticks to the portion that is left. There it is gathered together and thickened <note>original: "inspissated"</note>, so that it allows itself to be compressed into a drop or a little powder <note>original: "poulder"</note> rather than forsake the matter. This appears in distilled waters, which are therefore called *spirits.*

3. That when its matter is distilled several times and transferred into different forms through various alembics <note>an early chemical apparatus used for distilling liquids</note>, the spirit still does not fly away...

## CHAPTER 10.

### Regarding living creatures.

**S**o much for plants; next follow living creatures.

1. *A living creature is a moving plant, endowed with senses—such as a worm, a fish, a bird, or a beast.*

For if a stone or an oak tree could move itself freely, or had senses <note>original: "fence," likely a typo for "sense" or meaning perception</note>, it would be a living creature also.

2. *The principal difference between a living creature and a plant is self-movement <term>self-movement</term> <note>original Greek: "αὐτοκινησία" (autokinesia)</note>, which is...*

<summary>The author concludes that the "spirit" of plants and minerals remains in their extracts and can recreate their forms, then begins Chapter 10 by defining a living creature as a "moving plant" distinguished by its senses and the ability for self-movement.</summary>

<keywords>vital spirit, palingenesis, creation, biology, living creatures, autokinesia, motion, senses</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of a living creature (animal), focusing on the concept of self-movement and the role of the "vital soul" as the governing force of the animal body.</meta>

<page-num>160</page-num>

...is a free moving of itself to and fro.

To better express the power of the spirit of life—which is God’s representative <note>original: "Vicar"</note> in all created things—it was necessary that bodies be produced which that spirit, by inhabiting them, could make obedient to all its actions. Now, seeing that the basis of action is motion, bodies had to be designed that could move freely. These are called animals or living beings <note>original Latin: *Animalia* or *Animantia*</note>, named for the soul which powerfully demonstrates life within them. 

2. Therefore, mobility exists in all living creatures, but in different ways. For some move only by opening and shutting, without moving from their spot, such as oysters and cockles. Others creep little by little, like snails, earthworms, and other worms. Some have a long body that creeps by winding itself about, like snakes. Some are given feet, like lizards, beasts, and birds—though these last also have wings to fly through the air. Fish imitate this in the water, moving themselves by swimming.

### III. The moving principle in a living creature is the vital soul, which is nothing more than the spirit of life—concentrated <note>original: "thick"</note> and strong—powerfully filling and governing the bodies which it inhabits.

### IV. Now, because voluntary and agile motion can only be performed within a refined substance, living creatures are given bodies that are much more delicate than plants, yet much more complex. For they consist of spirit, flesh, blood, membranes, veins, nerves, cartilage <note>original: "gristles"</note>, and lastly bones, which act as props and pillars to prevent the body's frame from collapsing.

Understand this description applies to "perfect" <note>In the biology of this era, "perfect" referred to higher-order, complex animals, while "imperfect" referred to simpler organisms like invertebrates.</note> living creatures. For more "imperfect" living creatures, in which we observe only the basic beginnings of nature, have neither bones, nor flesh, nor blood, nor veins. Instead, they have only a white fluid <note>original: "humour"</note>, covered with a skin or shell <note>original: "crust"</note> as if by a sheath, which the indwelling spirit stirs or moves; this is seen in worms, snails, oysters, and so on. But regarding perfect living creatures:

1. To allow them to have a more refined spirit, *blood* and brains were provided.
2. So that these elements would not be scattered <note>original: "dissipated"</note>, they were given vessels and channels: *veins, arteries, and nerves*.
3. So that a living creature might stand upright <note>original: "erected"</note>, *bones* were given to it.
4. To prevent the bones—as well as the veins, arteries, and nerves—from being easily injured, everything was covered with either fat or flesh.
5. And so that the limbs <note>original: "members"</note> might move, tendons and muscles were interwoven.

2. Organs of movement; that is, *feet, wings, feathers, and so on*.

3. And lest they should run into objects or fall off cliffs <note>original: "precipices"</note>, it was necessary to provide them *with sight*.

4. For that reason, it was given *a mouth, teeth, a stomach, a liver, a heart, veins, and so on.*

VI. Therefore, the entire study of a living creature is completed by explaining:
1. *The nutritive faculty* <note>The ability to take in food and grow.</note>.
2. *The vital faculty* <note>The power that sustains life, heat, and the heartbeat.</note>.
3. *The sensitive faculty* <note>The ability to perceive the world through the senses.</note>.
4. *The locomotive faculty* <note>The ability to move from place to place.</note>.
5. *The communicative faculty* <note>original: "enuntiative faculty"; the ability to express sounds or signals.</note>.
6. *The defensive faculty*.
7. *And lastly, the generative faculty* <note>The ability to reproduce.</note>.

For whoever understands these seven knows the entire mystery of nature in living creatures. Whatever exists in the body of a living creature serves these faculties; if a part does not serve them, it is *useless* <note>original: "in vain"</note> and creates a <term>deformity</term> <note>original: "monster"; in early modern science, this referred to any biological abnormality or part that did not fit the natural design.</note>. It should also be observed that the first three faculties are governed by three specific <term>spirits</term> <note>In historical medicine, "spirits" were thought to be invisible, refined substances that traveled through the body to power different functions.</note>: *The nutritive faculty by the natural spirit, the vital by the spirit of life, and the sensitive by the animal spirit* <note>The "animal spirit" (from the Latin *anima*, meaning soul or breath) was believed to control the brain and nerves.</note>. The other four faculties are governed by those three spirits working together.

### *Of the Nutritive Faculty*

VII. *Every living creature needs daily food to repair the physical matter that is lost every day.*

For life consists of heat. And because heat is essentially fire, it requires fuel—which is moist, <term>spirituous</term> <note>Full of "spirits" or life-giving essence.</note>, and fatty matter. Heat in a living...
