### *VIII That nourishment is suitable for a living creature when it supplies it with a spirit like its own spirit.*

For since life comes from the spirit, matter by itself does not nourish life, but rather a "spirituous" matter <note>original: "spirituous matter"; matter containing vital spirits or life-energy.</note>. And indeed, the spirit of the nourishment must necessarily be like the spirit of the living creature. Therefore, we are not nourished by the basic elements as plants are, because those elements have only a *natural spirit*, not a *vital spirit* <note>In early modern biology, "natural spirits" governed basic growth, while "vital spirits" were necessary for sentient life and movement.</note>. Instead, we are nourished by plants or by the flesh of other living creatures because those provide a vital spirit. Furthermore, there is a particular balance of spirits which explains why a horse chooses oats, a pig chooses barley, a wolf chooses flesh, and so on. Even a pig has an appetite for human waste because it still finds parts there that are suitable for it.

### *IX Nourishment turns into the substance of that which is nourished.*

This appears to be true, first, because he who feeds on dry foods <note>original: "meats"; in this period, "meat" referred to food in general.</note> has a dry temperament <note>original: "complexion"; in humoral theory, this refers to the physical and mental makeup of a person.</note>. He who feeds on moist foods becomes phlegmatic <note>original: "flegmatick"; dominated by the humor of phlegm, often associated with being cool or sluggish.</note>, and so on. Second, it is because for the most part, a person retains the qualities of those living creatures on whose flesh he feeds. If anyone were given the brains of a cat or a wolf to eat, he would partake in the mental traits <note>original: "phantasies"; the instincts or behaviors associated with those animals.</note> of those living creatures, and so forth.

### **X *Nutriment must be assimilated so that it may turn into the substance of a living creature.***

For a thing is neither applied well nor does it join together effectively <note>original: "cohereth commodiously"</note> with that which is unlike it—much less could one thing turn into the other. Therefore, flesh or bone is not immediately made from food or drink; rather, it happens through many stages <note>original: "gradations"</note>, as shall appear.

### **XI *Assimilation is made by the transformation of the nourishment taken, repeated so often until it reaches the likeness of the substance being nourished.***

It is well known from metaphysics <note>original: "Metaphysicks"</note> that all action tends toward this: that the "patient" <note>original: "Patient"; the object or substance being acted upon.</note> may become like the "agent" <note>original: "Agent"; the force or being performing the action.</note>. This is evident everywhere in natural things.

### XII *The principal <term>transmutation</term> <note>The process of changing one substance into another; in this context, the conversion of food into bodily fluids.</note> of the nourishment is by the production of the four vital humors: blood, phlegm, yellow choler, and black.*

The fattest part of it is turned into **blood**; a part into **spittle** or **phlegm**; a part into **yellow choler** (or bile); and a part into **black choler** (or melancholy). 

**Melancholy, by its thickness, represents the earth; phlegm represents water; blood represents air; and choler represents fire.** 

But they differ in color and in flavor; for melancholy is black and bitter; phlegm is white and without taste; blood is red and sweet; and choler is yellow and bitter. Now it is to be noted that among these four, blood is most abundantly generated, because it contains the very substance of the nourishment: to which

### XIII *The generation of vital humors is accomplished through <term>concoction</term>.* <note>In early medicine, "concoction" refers to the process of "cooking" or ripening substances through internal bodily heat, essentially what we now call digestion and metabolism.</note>

For concoction alters the matter by the force of heat.

### XIV *Concoction in a living creature is done in the same manner as <term>distillation</term> in <term>alembicks</term>: namely, by heating the matter, resolving it into vapors, mixing those vapors together, and by a new <term>coagulation</term> of them again.* <note>An alembick is an early chemical apparatus used for distilling liquids; the author is comparing the stomach and organs to laboratory equipment.</note>

For every living body is essentially an alembick, full of perpetual heat and vapors. For life is heat, and heat cannot help but boil the matter that is put in, and by thinning it out <note>original: "attenuation"</note>, turn it into vapors.

### *XVI Every <term>concoction</term> <note>From the Latin "concoctio," meaning a "cooking" or digestion. It was the process of refining food into blood using internal heat.</note> leaves behind it unprofitable dregs; which are called excrements and dross.*

Thus we see it come to pass in the **decoction** <note>The process of boiling a substance to extract its essence, common in both herbal medicine and metallurgy.</note> of metals. Now we must note that plants make little or no excrement, because they are nourished with a simple and uniform juice which goes entirely into their nature; or, if anything remains, it sweats forth in gum. But living creatures—because they consist of very dissimilar parts—have need of a compound **nutriment** <note>Nourishment or food.</note> that is solid and soft, dry and moist, hot and cold, etc. In this way, the more solid parts may have nourishment as well; whereby through **assimilation** <note>The biological process where nutrients are converted into the actual substance of the body's organs and tissues.</note>, every part draws that which will profit itself, and the rest must of necessity be strained out. Another reason is because plants are sustained with a little spirit, and that which does not evaporate; but living creatures

### *XVII The principal <term>concoction</term> <note>From the Latin "concoctio," meaning "cooking." In early medicine, digestion was viewed as a process of refining food through internal heat.</note> in a living creature is threefold: <term>chylification</term>, <term>sanguification</term>, and <term>membrification</term>.*

The first occurs in the stomach; the second in the liver; and the last in all the parts of the body <note>original: "members."</note>.

### *XVIII Each of these digestion processes has three types of vessels: 1. for intake, 2. for processing, and 3. for discharge.*

### *XIX The vessels of <term>chylification</term> <note>The process of turning food into "chyle," a milky fluid of digested nutrients.</note> are: 1. the mouth and the throat; 2. the stomach or <term>ventricle</term> <note>From the Latin "ventriculus" (little belly); here referring to the main cavity of the stomach.</note>; and 3. the intestines and the anus.*

For when food is received at the mouth, it is chewed with the teeth or jaws and passed through the throat. It is "boiled" in the stomach as if in a sealed <term>alembic</term> <note>A chemical apparatus used for distillation; the author uses it as a metaphor for the stomach refining food.</note> for several hours. From there, by a process of refinement, it passes into the intestines (as the opening of the stomach leading back toward the throat is shut) and becomes **chyle** <note>original: "Chylus"; the white, milky fluid produced during the first stage of digestion.</note>, which is a kind of fermented substance like porridge or white broth.

For it takes a white color from the stomach by assimilation. The more refined parts of this **chyle** are attracted to the liver, as material suitable for blood; but the waste <note>original: "excrements"</note> of this first digestion <note>original: "concoction"</note> are thick dregs, which are driven out through the intestines <note>original: "guts"</note> and the rear passage. This happens not by the simple motion of **yielding** <note>original: "*Cession*"; a passive moving aside.</note>, but by the motion of **antipathy** <note>In early modern science, "antipathy" referred to a natural repulsion between substances; here, the body is seen as actively "hating" and rejecting waste.</note>. For the natural spirits located in the fibers of the intestines suck out what is profitable, but they turn away from what is unprofitable and hateful to them, contracting the fibers of the intestines and thrusting those burdens forward toward the exit.

### XX The vessels of **sanguification** <note>The process of making blood.</note> are: 1. the **mesentery**; 2. the **liver**; 3. the **ureters**, the **spleen**, and the **gallbladder** <note>original: "gall."</note>.

For the **mesentery**, surrounding the intestines <note>original: "entrals"</note> with its strings (which they call the **mesenteric veins** <note>original: "*Mesaraicall veins*"; the veins that carry nutrients from the digestive tract to the liver.</note>), sucks the best part of the **chyle** out of the intestines and carries it to the liver by the **portal vein** <note>original: "*Vena Porta*"</note>. Now the liver processes <note>original: "concocts"</note> and separates that fluid again, for it assimilates the sweeter parts into its own color and turns them to **blood**, swelling with natural spirit. Along with this blood, however, there is phlegm and yellow

Finally, the gallbladder <note>original: "gall"</note> (the little bag hanging at the liver) attracts those parts of the blood that are too sharp and fiery. It sends these, mixed together, back into the intestines through ducts <note>original: "strings"</note>; this is the source of the bitterness and foul smell of dung.

This happens in exactly the same way that the sap <note>original: "juice"</note> of a tree is changed into wood, bark, pith, leaves, and fruits by mere assimilation. The waste products <note>original: "excrements"</note> of this third and most refined <note>original: "subtle"</note> digestion <note>original: "concoction"</note> are also refined: namely, sweat and vapor, which always breathe out through the pores. XXII *To encourage nourishment, a stimulus <note>original: "spur"</note> is added: namely appetite, or hunger and thirst. These are nothing more than a twitching <note>original: "vellication"</note> of the fibers of the stomach, arising from the sharp pulling <note>original: "sucking"</note> of the <term>chyle</term> <note>original: "Chylus"; the milky fluid of digested food</note>.*

For when the organs and limbs are deprived of the fluid <note>original: "juice"</note> with which they are watered, they draw from <note>original: "solicite"</note> the veins of blood. The veins (by the connection of the whole system) draw from the liver; the liver draws from the <term>mesentery</term> <note>original: "Mesenterie"; the membrane that attaches the intestines to the abdominal wall</note>; the mesentery draws from the intestines <note>original: "entrals"</note>; and the intestines draw from the stomach. If the stomach has nothing to provide, it contracts and wrinkles.

### XXIII The whole body is nourished all at once, by the motion of distribution <note>original: "libration"; referring to a balanced swinging or weighing out of nutrients</note>.

That is to say, in the same way that the root of a plant equally nourishes itself, the trunk <note>original: "stock"</note>, and all the branches. Therefore, no part <note>original: "member"</note> nourishes itself alone, but nourishes others along with itself, and thus all are preserved. Otherwise, if any part robs the rest of their nourishment, or conversely refuses it, an imbalance <note>original: "distemperature"</note> of the whole body follows, then decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>, and eventually death.

### XXIV A living creature, being nourished in this way, is not only sustained <note>original: "vegetated"</note> but also (as long as its parts are soft and capable of stretching) grown <note>original: "augmented" </note>. As soon as the body parts <note>original: "members"</note> are hardened (after youth), the living creature stops growing. Yet it continues to increase in density and strength as long as the three stages of digestion <note>original: "concoctions"; referring to the three-stage process of transforming food into blood and then into tissue</note> are correctly performed. But when the vessels involved in digestion also begin to dry up, the living creature begins to wither away, and life grows feeble until it fails and is extinguished.

### Concerning the Vital Faculty <note>The "vital faculty" was traditionally understood as the power or soul-force that maintained life, distinct from the mental or physical functions.</note>

XXV **Life** in a living creature is a specific mixture of the vital spirits <note>original: "spirits"; subtle, vaporous substances believed to flow through the body to provide life and movement</note> with the blood and the body parts, so that they are all warm, possess sensation, and move themselves.

Therefore, the life of living creatures consists of heat, sensation, and motion; this is clear because if any creature lacks motion, sensation, or heat, it is not alive.

XXVI Therefore, every living creature is full of heat—sometimes stronger, and sometimes weaker.

For every living creature is nourished. As was shown previously, nourishment is only possible through digestion <note>original: "concoction"</note>. Reason teaches that digestion cannot occur without heat and fire. It remains, therefore, to explain where a living creature gets this heat and fire, and by what means it is ignited.

XXVII **The heart is the furnace <note>original: "forge"</note> of heat in a living creature, burning with a perpetual fire and producing a small flame called the spirit of life; it transmits <note>original: "communicates"</note> this spirit to the entire body.**

Because of this, it is commonly said that the heart is the first part to live and the last part to die.

XXVIII **The vital spirit in the heart has blood as its fuel <note>original: "matter"</note>; the lungs as its bellows; and the arteries as the channels through which it distributes itself to the whole body.**

Our hearth fire requires three things: 1. fuel <note>original: "matter"</note>, which must be oily; 2. fanning <note>original: "blowing,"</note> which stirs up its power; and 3. free passage <note>original: "transpiration"</note>, so that it may spread itself. The Creator of all things has ordained these same three requirements for every living creature. For the heart, located slightly above the liver, absorbs a very pure portion of blood through a branch of the veins. Because this blood is spirited and oily, it produces a very gentle flame. To ensure this flame is not extinguished, the *lungs* lie near the heart; acting like bellows by expanding and contracting, they blow upon and fan that fire of the heart perpetu-

### XXXVII The organs of the senses are parts of the body, in which the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>Thought to be a refined fluid or "spirit" that traveled through the nerves to carry sensory information to the brain.</note> receives the objects that present themselves; namely, the eye, the ear, the nostrils, the tongue, and all that is <term>nervy</term> <note>The "nervy" parts refer to the nervous system and tissues connected to nerves.</note>.

### XXXVIII The <term>medium</term> <note>The substance or element that carries information from an object to our sense organs.</note> of conjoining them, is that which brings the object into the organ: in light, the light; in hearing, the air moved with breaking <note>This refers to the vibration or "breaking" of air that produces sound.</note>; in smells, the air vaporing; in taste, the water melting; in touch, the quality itself inherent in the matter.

Generally speaking, there is only one sense, and that is touch. For nothing can be perceived unless it touches us, either directly or from a distance. There is no sense at all of things that are absent.

### XL *Therefore, in every sensation, the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>Thought to be a refined fluid or "spirit" that traveled through the nerves to carry sensory information.</note> <term>suffers</term> <note>original: "suffers"; in this context, it means to be acted upon or to experience "passion," the state of being the receiver of an external effect.</note> by the thing being sensed.*

It is quite evident that there is no sensation without <term>passion</term> <note>The philosophical opposite of action; a state where the subject is being affected by an external object.</note>. For we do not perceive heat or cold unless we become hot or cold; nor sweet and bitter unless we become sweet or bitter; nor color unless we are colored by it. Our spirit, I say, residing in the organs, is touched and affected. Therefore, those things which are just like us are not perceived; for example, heat that is the same as our own heat does not affect us. But we must observe that in order for the organs to perceive any qualities of objects, they must lack those qualities themselves; for example, the <term>apple of the eye</term> <note>The pupil or the eyeball itself, which was thought to be a clear, colorless sphere.</note> lacks color; the tongue lacks <term>flavor</term> <note>original: "favour."</note>; and so on.

### XLII *The effect of sensation is pleasure or pain.*

Pleasure occurs if the sense is affected gently and easily by something agreeable to it, causing a <term>pleasant stimulation</term> <note>original: "titillation."</note>. <term>Pain</term> <note>original: "griefe." ### XLIII *Sleep was given to living creatures so that the animal spirit—which is always busy with the actions of the senses—may sometimes rest and be refreshed. Sleep is a gathering together of the animal spirits at the center of the brain, while the sense organs are temporarily blocked by vapors rising from the stomach.* <note>original: "ventricle." 3. *What it means to be awake, and how it happens?* Namely, when the spirit, being strengthened in...

### XLIV

The sense of touch has *as its instrument the nervous skin, as well as all the nervous and membrane-filled parts of the body.* <note>original: "membranaceous." In early science, "nervous" meant "full of nerves" rather than "anxious."</note>

Therefore hair, nails, and bones do not feel, etc., even if you cut or burn them, because they have no nerves running through them. Yet they do feel in that part where they connect to the flesh because they have a nerve-like substance for their <term>binding tissue</term> <note>original: "gluten." Here, it refers to the biological "glue" or connective substance that joins body parts together.</note>. For this reason, the pain under the nails and in the membranes of the bones is most intense. Now, since the skin of the body is very connective and entirely full of nerves, it is covered with a thin skin called in Latin <term>the cuticle</term> <note>original: *cuticula*. This refers to the epidermis or the outermost layer of skin.</note> to prevent the living creature from constant pain and trouble by being too sensitive. We sometimes see this thin skin peel off from body parts <note>original: "members."</note> that are scorched or bruised; it lacks sensation so that it may restrain the intensity of the sense.

### XLV

*The taste has the tongue for its **organ**, a porous part of the body and always moist, so that even dry things that touch it may melt and give off a flavor* <note>original: "savour"</note>, *which, penetrating the tongue through the nerves located at its roots, is immediately communicated to the brain.*

When the tongue is dry (as during great thirst), the sense of taste perceives nothing. Therefore, God in His wise counsel has provided that in every <term>complex animal</term> <note>original: "perfect living creature."</note>, the vapors rising out of the <term>stomach</term> <note>original: "ventricle." In early modern medicine, the stomach was often referred to as a ventricle or chamber.</note> should be gathered together into saliva <note>original: "spittle"</note> within the hollow of the mouth to water the tongue perpetually. The porosity of the tongue serves this purpose very well. Furthermore, two little kernels were added, called the **tonsils** <note>original: *Tonsillæ*</note>, which are also spongy and always preserve saliva for the use of the tongue.

### XLVI

*The nostrils are the **organs** of smelling, along with the <term>sieve-like bone</term> <note>original: "cribrous bone." This refers to the ethmoid bone, which contains tiny holes like a sieve for olfactory nerves to pass through.</note> placed over them; through which, as if through a sponge, the smell coming from things may enter the brain.*

Therefore, when a <term>cold</term> <note>original: "Catarrhe."</note> flows and fills the nostrils, the sense of smelling is hindered. This is the most direct path to the brain, and it is therefore the most powerful way to affect the <term>animal spirits</term> <note>original: "animall spirit." In historical science, these were believed to be refined fluids that carried sensory information through the nerves.</note>, being either immediately pleasing, or—

<meta>catchword: recre-</meta> <note>original: likely "refreshing" or "recreating."</note>

<summary>This page explains the biological mechanisms of taste and smell, detailing how moisture in the mouth and the porous structure of the ethmoid bone allow sensory information to reach the brain.</summary>

### XLVII

*Hearing has the ear for its **organ**. It contains a passage leading to the brain, along with a gristly <note>original: "gristly" (cartilaginous)</note> border that winds around like a snail's shell; this is attached to the outside to catch the movement of the air when it is struck and turn it inward. At the center of these windings is a little drum <note>original: "drum," referring to the eardrum or tympanic membrane</note>, with a little piece of flesh standing by it like a hammer <note>original: "hammer," referring to the malleus, one of the tiny bones in the middle ear</note>. When the air that enters strikes this hammer, it also beats the drum. The spirit, perceiving this, judges the volume or magnitude, and the nearness or distance of the object that struck the air; through repeated experience, it learns what kind of thing is moving the air.*

This wonderful organ is easily damaged from both the inside and the outside. From the inside, it is damaged if the passages are blocked with phlegm <note>original: "flegme"</note>, and much more so if the hammer or the drum are harmed by decay <note>original: "rottennesse"</note>. From the outside, it is damaged if the ear (that is, the gristly border) is cut off; in that case, the sound either slips past the ear or is received irregularly <note>original: "inordinately"</note>, creating only a disorganized noise. To help prevent this, the Creator gave living creatures two ears each.

Now it is clear from this: 1. *Why sounds that are too high-pitched or too low offend the ear, while moderate ones please us?* It is because they harmonize better with our **spirit** <note>The "animal spirit," a subtle fluid thought to carry sensory information to the brain.</note>.

2. *Why does sound also travel indirectly?* Because when the air is moved, it moves the air next to it in all directions through the motion of **diffusion** <note>The outward spreading of waves.</note>.

3. *Why does a sound spreading in all directions gradually fade away?* It is just like when the water of a pool is moved by a stone falling into it. However, while the water vibrates for a long time in that whole circle, the circle of the air <note>OCR reads "rain," but the context of "air" vs "water" indicates "air" is intended here.</note> moved by sound passes away all at once; for the sound of a bell does not remain in the air, but is spun around within the ringing bell itself.

4. *Why is it that when one person hears, everyone hears the same thing?* Because a sound is a physical **commotion** <note>A disturbance or vibration.</note> of the air, which reaches the ears of all those who are within that circle.

5. *What is an Echo?* It is a sound reflected from hollow places, in the same way that ripples of water in a container, after hitting the sides, return again toward the center.

The structure <note>original: "fabrick"</note> of the eyes is wonderful. For beneath the forehead of every living creature, God has hollowed out two windows in the skull, into which the outermost membrane of the brain sends two pouch-like structures filled with the humors <note>The fluid substances of the eye, such as the aqueous and vitreous humors.</note> that come from the brain. In the middle of these, there is a tube woven from a thin, opaque membrane, yet filled with a very pure, crystalline humor. They call it the *apple of the eye* <note>In this period, the "apple of the eye" could refer to the pupil or the lens; here it is described as the primary site of vision.</note>, where vision is properly formed. This is surrounded by a network <note>The retina.</note> filled with a watery or glassy humor. And finally, there is that membrane which common people call the *white of the eye*; but philosophers (because it is hard and polished like a horn) call it the *cornea*. This is transparent directly in front of the "apple" and the network, but elsewhere it retains its whiteness. Now, beneath the base of the "apple" lies the *optic nerve*, through which the image of the thing perceived passes directly to the center of the brain.

50. <note>The "L." in the original likely indicates the Roman numeral 50 in a list of principles.</note> <term>Vision</term> is threefold: straight, reflected, and refracted.

*Straight* or direct vision is that by which the light is seen—for example, the Sun or fire. For here, the light presents itself to the eye in a single line.

*Reflected* vision is that by which other things are seen in the open air. There, the light reflected from objects comes to the eye by a second line (for by the first line the light falls upon the object, and by the second line it travels from there to the eye).

*Refracted* vision is that by which things are seen through a double medium, and thus by bent <note>original: "refracted"</note> lines. This occurs when an oar or pole seems broken in the water. It also happens when a piece of money at the bottom of a vessel full of water seems larger and closer to the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note>, so that one may move back and still see it.

# On the Ability to Move
<note>original: "motive faculty"</note>

51. <note>The "L I" in the original indicates the number 51 in a sequence of principles.</note> *Motion was given to a living creature:*
1. *To seek its food.*
2. *For those actions to which each individual is destined.*
3. *To preserve the vigor of life.*

Because a living creature has a more delicate <note>original: "tender"</note> constitution than a plant, it would more easily decay <note>original: "putrify"</note> and perish if it were not energized <note>original: "quickened"</note> by very frequent motion. Therefore, the **Creator** has most wisely provided for our benefit that we cannot even take our food <note>original: "meat"</note> without labor and movement.

52. <note>The "L II" in the original indicates the number 52.</note> *The principle of movement is the <term>animal spirit</term>.* <note>In early modern science, "animal spirits" were thought to be a refined, fluid-like substance that traveled through the nerves to enable sensation and movement.</note>

Therefore, a body without life—no matter how well-equipped with **organs**—does not move. When the brain, which is the seat of the *animal spirits*, is poorly affected (for example, with either dizziness or <term>overindulgence</term> <note>original: "surfeit," meaning sickness caused by excessive eating or drinking</note>), the limbs <note>original: "members"</note> immediately fall, or at least stumble and totter. Furthermore, when the nerve of any limb is blocked, it is immediately deprived of both motion and sensation, as can be seen in cases of <term>palsy</term> <note>paralysis or tremors</note> and <term>apoplexy</term> <note>a stroke</note>.

53. *Now the <term>animal spirit</term> moves either itself alone, or the <term>vital spirit</term> along with it, or lastly, the members of the body as well.* <note>In this period, "animal spirits" were thought to be the fluid of thought and nerve signals, while "vital spirits" were related to the heart, blood, and life-force.</note>

54. *The animal spirit moves itself perpetually, sometimes more, sometimes less: namely, running in and out of the organs of the senses, or however it stirs itself in its workshop <note>original: "work-houſe," referring to the brain as the center of cognitive activity</note>.*

For from this inward motion come perpetual fantasies or imaginations, even during sleep, which we then call **dreams**.

55. *It carries the vital spirit along with it when, at the sensation of something either pleasing or displeasing, it conveys itself back and forth through the body, taking the vital spirit with it as if for assistance; this happens in joy and sorrow, hope and fear, gratitude <note>original: "gratulation"</note> and repentance, and finally, in anger.*

For joy is a motion in which the spirit pours itself out at the sensation of a pleasant object, as though it would unite <note>original: "couple"</note> itself with the thing that it desires. From this comes the lively color in the face of a joyful man, caused by the vital spirit flowing there with a very pure portion of the blood. This is the reason why moderate joy purifies the blood and is helpful for prolonging life. See *Proverbs 15:13 and 17:22.*

**Sorrow** is a motion where the **vital spirit**, upon sensing an object that displeases it, runs to its center. This is why we turn ourselves often in our sleep.

### Of the defensive faculty.

LX. *If the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>Historical term for the subtle fluid believed to carry signals between the brain and the body</note> perceives any hostile thing approaching, it immediately turns to its weapons to either defend itself (by raising its hair, bristles, scales, or prickles) or to attack <note>original: "offend"</note> and hurt its enemies (using its horns, claws <note>original: "nailes"</note>, wings, beak, hands, etc.).*

The strength by which this is accomplished can already be understood from what has been said previously.

### Of the generative faculty.

Since living creatures, like plants, are mortal beings, they must necessarily multiply to preserve their species. Regarding this, take note of the following <term>axioms</term> <note>Fundamental principles or established truths</note>:

LXI. *Because the reproduction <note>original: "generation"</note> of living creatures—due to the large number and delicacy <note>original: "tendernesse"</note> of their body parts—could not conveniently be carried out in the depths of the earth, they were given different sexes. It was also ordained that the new living creature should be formed within the body of the parent creature itself.*

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "generative faculty" (reproduction), specifically addressing why complex animals require two sexes and internal development rather than being "born" from the earth like simpler organisms.</meta>

Just as the sun, by its heat, produces plants in the womb of the earth, it may also produce those living things whose formation is completed within a few days—such as worms, mice, and various insects. This occurs either by the "seed" of those same living creatures falling into suitable scattered matter, or by the "spirit of the universe" falling into suitable matter. 

However, the sun cannot produce more complex <note>original: "perfect"—meaning biologically advanced or fully developed</note> living creatures which consist of many solid body parts and require a long time for their formation (such as a human, a horse, or an elephant). Because the sun cannot remain in the same region <note>original: "coast"</note> of the heavens for that long, the developing young would be ruined before it could reach maturity. 

Therefore, the most wise Creator of all things appointed the place of formation to be within the living creature itself, rather than in the earth. He formed two sexes so that one might play the part of the plant bearing the seed, and the other might play the part of the earth—nurturing and, in a sense, hatching the seed. This, and nothing else, is the purpose <note>original: "end"</note> of the different sexes in all living creatures. 

Woe to the recklessness and madness of men who abuse these functions, which is something no beast does! The organs <note>original: "members"</note> by which the sexes differ are the same in number, location <note>original: "site"</note>, and form, and differ in nothing almost...

...almost, except in regard to the **external** <note>original: *exterius*</note> and the **internal** <note>original: *interius*</note>. That is to say, the greater force of heat in the male pushes the genitals outward; however, in the female, because of a weaker heat, those same organs remain contained within—as anatomists are aware.

LXII. *The vital spirit <note>original: "spirit"—referring to a life-force believed to animate the body</note> is the director of all development <note>original: "generation"</note>, just as it is in plants. When this spirit is heated within the seed, it first forms a place to live for itself—that is, the brain and the head. From there, it makes excursions to form the rest of the body parts <note>original: "members"</note> gradually and gently. By returning to its seat in the head to rest and then working again in turns, we see the origin of waking and sleeping.*

Therefore, the formation of a living creature does not begin from the heart, as Aristotle thought, but from the head. For the head is, in a sense, the entire living creature; the rest of the body is nothing but a structure of organs for various functions <note>original: "operations"</note>. This appears plain because some living creatures (such as fish) were thought to have no heart, but none are found without a head and brain.

->*On the Types of Living Creatures.*<-

This concludes the general discussion of living creatures; the specific types follow.

LXIII. *A living creature, categorized according to the difference in its motion, is either:* 1. *A reptile <note>original: "Reptile"—referring to any creature that crawls</note>.* 2. *A walker <note>original: "Gressile"—from the Latin 'gressus', meaning to step or walk</note>.* 3. *A swimmer <note>original: "Natatile"—from the Latin 'natatilis', meaning able to swim</note>.* 4. *A flyer <note>original: "Volatile"—from the Latin 'volatilis', meaning winged or able to fly</note>.*

LXIV. *Rep-*

LXIV. A **reptile** <note>original: *Reptile*</note>, or a **creeping thing**, is a living creature with a **long body** that lacks feet, yet is made up of **joints** (or **gristly rings**). By the **contraction and extension** of these rings, it **winds itself up and reaches out**: such as **worms and serpents**.

LXV. A **walker** <note>original: *Gressile*—from the Latin *gressus*, meaning a step or gait</note> is that which has feet (two or more) and walks; such as a **lizard**, a **mouse**, a **dog**, etc.

LXVI. A **swimmer** <note>original: *Natatile*—from the Latin *natatilis*, meaning able to swim</note> is that which passes through the water by the help of **fins**. It is called a **fish**, among which **crabs** and various **sea monsters** are also included.

LXVII. A **flyer** <note>original: *Volatile*—from the Latin *volatilis*, meaning winged or flying</note> is that which moves itself through the air by the shaking of its **wings**; it is called a **bird**.

The **lightness** of birds, which allows them to **fly**, comes from their **featheriness** <note>original: *plumosity*</note>. For every **plume** or **feather** is **hollow** and full of **spirit and vapor** <note>The author refers to the "vital spirits," a historical medical concept of a life-force that was thought to fill the body and provide buoyancy</note>—not only in the **stalk**, but through all its parts and the smallest particles of those parts. For this reason, **no birds urinate** <note>original: "pisse"—Modern science clarifies that birds excrete nitrogenous waste as a semi-solid paste (uric acid) rather than liquid urine to conserve water and weight for flight</note>, because all their **moisture** continually **evaporates into their feathers**. It is **impossible**, therefore, for a **man to fly**, even if he **fits himself with wings**, because he **lacks feathers to lift him**. The feathers that he might attach to himself are **dead** and **devoid of heat and spirit**.

### LXVIII *Small living things are by a special name called insects; such as flies, worms, etc.*

They are called **insects** from the **incisions** by which their bodies are, as it were, cut into sections <note>The word "insect" comes from the Latin *insectum*, meaning "cut into," referring to their segmented bodies.</note>. These may be divided in the same manner as the larger animals. For worms are **creeping things** <note>original: *Reptile*</note>; lice, fleas, **bedbugs** <note>original: *Punies* — an archaic term for bedbugs, from the French *punaise*</note>, spiders, etc., are **walkers** <note>original: *Gressile*</note>; the water-spider and the horse-leech, etc., are **swimmers** <note>original: *Natatile*</note>; and flies and gnats, etc., are **flyers** <note>original: *Volatile*</note>. All of these exist with infinite varieties, so that here also there is no lack of a most clear mirror <note>original: "glasse"</note> of the Creator’s admirable wisdom. They also serve as a school for man to learn virtues and forget vices; a clear image of both is found in these living creatures, a point which the Scriptures often emphasize <note>original: "inculcates"</note>.

Long-lasting trances are commonly known to happen this way; some people were ready to be buried as though they were truly dead—and some were even buried—yet they lived again. 6. Finally, it is worth observing that the animal spirit <note>original: "animall spirit"—the vital life force believed to animate all living things</note> forms new living creatures of a different kind rather than completely abandoning decaying matter: namely, worms and similar creatures. It is certain from experience that, from the bodies of creatures that have died and decayed, those specific living creatures are born which the original animal used to eat when it was alive. For example, serpents are born from the flesh of storks, spiders from hens, frogs from ducks, and so on. John Poppus <note>Johann Popp (1577–1644), a German physician and chemist known for his works on "chymical" medicine</note>, a distiller from Coburg, following the work of others, has taught that this will happen if the remains are buried in dung <note>This refers to the theory of "spontaneous generation," the historical (and now disproven) belief that living organisms could arise naturally from non-living or decaying matter.</note>. It appears, then, that the animal spirit is everywhere and is very diligently occupied with giving life to bodies.

### CHAP. XI.
### *Of Man.*

I. **A** human being is a living creature, endowed with an immortal soul. For the Creator breathed a soul into him from Himself (Genesis 2:7), and this soul is also called the *mind* and *reason*, in which the *image of God shines*.

<summary>This page discusses the theory of spontaneous generation, where the "animal spirit" creates new life from decaying matter, and introduces the definition of humanity as a creature possessing an immortal soul and divine reason.</summary>
<keywords>animal spirit, spontaneous generation, John Poppus, immortality of the soul, Genesis 2:7, reason, image of God, putrefaction</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-num>(211)</page-num>
<sig>P 2</sig>
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of humanity, specifically the "tripartite" (three-part) structure of a human being—comprising body, spirit, and soul—and how these components interact.</meta>

<margin>a</margin> II. Therefore, he is composed <note>original: "compounded"</note> of three things: a body, a spirit, and a soul.

So the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul</note> testifies in *1 Thessalonians 5:23*: *May your whole spirit, soul, and body be kept blameless.* And so in *1 Corinthians 14:14*, he distinguishes between the *spirit and the mind*. And indeed, so it is: we have a body composed of the elements <note>The four classical elements: earth, water, air, and fire</note> just like the brute animals <note>original: "bruits"</note>; we have a spirit from the spirit of the world <note>The "spiritus mundi," a vital life force believed to animate the physical universe</note> just as they do; but the soul or mind is from God. The first (the body) we carry about us as a mortal thing; the second (the spirit) is capable of being dispersed <note>original: "dissipable"—able to be scattered or dissolved at death</note>; but the last (the soul) endures forever without the body, as we are assured by faith. Therefore, when you see a man, think that you see a king, royally clothed and sitting on his royal throne. For the *mind* is a king, his robe is the *spirit*, and his throne is the *body*.

*III. The body is the instrument <note>original: "Organ"—here meaning a functional tool</note> and dwelling of the spirit; but the spirit is the dwelling and home <note>original: "mansion"</note> of the soul.*

For just as the spirit dwells in the body and guides it—as a pilot <note>original: "Pilot"—one who steers a ship</note> does a ship—so the soul dwells in the spirit and rules it. And as a body without a spirit neither moves itself nor has any sense of anything (as is seen in a dead carcass), so the spirit without the mind has no reason, nor un- <meta>catchword: un- [likely "understanding"]</meta>

<keywords>tripartite nature, body spirit soul, St. Paul, 1 Thessalonians 5:23, 1 Corinthians 14:14, vital spirit, immortality, reason, philosophy of mind</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "tripartite" nature of man—body, spirit, and soul—specifically examining the hierarchy of these parts and how physical health affects mental and spiritual states.</meta>

<page-num>212</page-num>

...understands anything, as we see in brute <note>original: "bruit"</note> beasts. Therefore, the soul uses the spirit as its chariot and instrument; the spirit uses the body; and the body uses the aforementioned instruments.

IV. *As the spirit is affected by the body, so is the mind affected by the spirit.*

For just as when the body is diseased, the spirit is immediately saddened or hindered from its actions; so when the spirit is poorly disposed, the mind cannot perform its functions skillfully <note>original: "dextrously"</note>—as we may see in drunken, melancholy, or insane men, etc. Because of this, the gifts of the mind follow the temperament <note>original: "temperature"—referring to the balance of bodily "humors" or physical constitution</note> of the body, so that one person is more intelligent <note>original: "ingenious"</note>, courteous, chaste, or courageous, etc., than another.

Hence arises that internal struggle within us which the Scripture so often mentions and which we ourselves feel. For the body and the soul—being that they are extremes (the one earthly, the other heavenly; the one brute, the other rational; the one mortal, the other immortal)—are always contrary to one another in their inclinations. Now the *spirit*, which is placed between them, ought indeed to obey the superior part and keep the lower part in order at its command <note>original: "as its beck"</note>. Yet nevertheless, it often happens that the spirit is carried away by the flesh and becomes brutish.

<summary>This page explains the hierarchy of the human condition, arguing that the mind's health depends on the spirit, which in turn depends on the body. It describes the "internal struggle" of man as a conflict between the mortal, earthly body and the immortal, rational soul.</summary>

V. *Such a body was given to man that it might fitly serve all the uses of his rational soul; and therefore, it is: 1. Furnished with many organs. 2. Erect. 3. Naked and unarmed, so that it might be free in itself and yet could be clothed and armed in any way as the occasion required.*

For the *hand*—the instrument of instruments, the most diligent <note>original: "painful"—meaning painstaking or hardworking</note> doer of all works—was given to man alone. He alone has obtained an erect stature, lest he should live unmindful of his true country: Heaven. Again, he alone was made naked and unarmed; but both of these conditions were the result of the singular favors of God. For as for other living creatures, while they always carry their garments (hair, feathers, shells) and their weapons (sharp prickles, horns) upon them, what do they carry but burdens and hindrances to various actions? The liberty granted to man, and his industry in providing, fitting, and storing <note>original: "laying up"</note> all things for his use and pleasure, is something more divine.

VI. *A more abundant and pure spirit was given to man, and therefore his inward operations are more excellent—namely, a quicker attention, a stronger imagination, a more reliable memory, and more powerful <note>original: "vehement"</note> emotions <note>original: "affections"</note>.*

For example, when I see something moving a great distance away, I conclude <note>original: "gather"</note> it to be a living creature; when I come near, I know it to be a man, and at length this or that specific man, etc.

## XII *There are three faculties of the mind of man: the Understanding, the Will, and the Conscience.*

These correspond to the three functions of the animal spirit, or to the **inward senses**, <note>In traditional psychology, "inward senses" were internal faculties like imagination and memory that processed information from the "outward" senses like sight or touch.</note> from which they also result. For we have said that just as the spirit uses the body as its organ, so the soul uses the spirit. Therefore, the three inward senses—**attention, judgment, and memory**—are instruments by which the soul uses the understanding, the will, and the conscience. For by diligent attention, it produces an understanding of things; by imagination or judging, it produces choice (that is, to will or to refuse <note>original: "nill"; to be unwilling or to reject</note>); and by remembrance, it produces conscience.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mind's faculties, specifically defining the "Understanding" and the nature of human "Reason" as distinct from the memory-based instincts of animals.</meta>

XIII *The understanding is a faculty of the rational soul, gathering unknown things from known things; and by comparing uncertain things together, it draws certain conclusions through reasoning.*

XIV *To reason is to inquire into the reasons and causes of why anything is, or is not, by thinking upon it.*

For the mind or reason, by gathering together experiences from the senses, first forms certain general notions for itself. For example, when it sees that fire scorches all things, it forms this rule for itself: *All fire burns, etc.* These kinds of experimental notions are called **principles**, from which the understanding frames logical reasoning <note>original: "discourse"; in this context, it refers to the process of the mind moving from one premise to another to reach a conclusion</note> as the occasion arises. 

For example, if gold melts with fire, then it is also hot and burns when it is melted. From this follows this conclusion: therefore, if the workman pours gold into his hand, he is burned by it. Look, here is understanding—and of a thing never before seen! This is something a brute <note>original: "bruite"; a non-human animal lacking the power of reason</note> cannot achieve. For animals do not reason, but rely simply upon experiences. For instance, if a dog is beaten with a staff, he runs away afterward at the sight of a staff because the memory of his recent suffering comes back to him.

<vocab>understanding, reason, rational soul, experiences, senses, notions, principles, logical reasoning, animal, memory</vocab>

memory. However, an animal cannot reason (for example: "a staff is hard, and a staff caused me pain; therefore, every hard thing struck against the body causes pain"). This he cannot do. XV *When reasoning <note>original: "ratiocination"</note> is consistent in every way, it produces truth <note>original: "verity"</note>; if it has any gaps, it produces error.*

XVI *Quickness of reasoning is called ingenuity; soundness is called judgment; and the lack of it is called dullness.*

For a person is *ingenious* if they perceive and reason <note>original: "discourseth"</note> quickly. One is *judicious* if they use a certain natural speed to pay attention to whether the reasoning is sufficiently consistent in every way. One is *dull* if they have neither of these qualities. The first two qualities come from the *balance* <note>original: "temperature"; referring to the Four Humors theory of medicine</note> *of blood and melancholy* <note>original: "melancholy"; black bile, thought to govern thought and seriousness</note>; the last comes from an abundance of phlegm <note>original: "flegme"; the humor associated with sluggishness</note>. For melancholy (meaning not the coarse kind full of dregs, but the pure kind) balanced with much blood gives a nimble wit; but when mixed with less blood, it results in a piercing and steady judgment. But a person is wiser in proportion to how many particulars he knows, and how he sees they depend upon their general categories. Therefore, *Aristotle* did not speak correctly when he said, *“That sensory perception is of particulars, but understanding is of universals.”*

### XVIII *The will is a faculty of the rational soul, inclining it toward a known good and turning it away from a foreseen evil.*

For the soul performs that toward which the will inclines, and the will inclines where the understanding leads it. The will follows the understanding as its guide everywhere, and it does not err unless the understanding errs. For example, when a Christian chooses drunkenness rather than sobriety (even though he is taught otherwise), he does it because the intellect, deceived by the senses, judges it better to please the palate than to be tormented with thirst,

### XIX *If the will wisely follows things that are truly good, and wisely avoids things that are truly bad, it produces virtue; if it does the contrary, it produces vice:*

For virtue is nothing else but a wise, constant, and passionate <note>original: "ardent"</note> shunning of evil and embracing of good. Vice, on the contrary, is nothing but a neglecting of good and an embracing of evil.

### XX *The conscience of a person is an intellectual memory of those things which reason dictates either to be done or avoided; it records what the will has done or not done according to this rule, and what God has decreed for those who do or do not do them.*

Therefore, its function in the soul is threefold: to warn, to testify, and to judge all things that are done or are to be done (see the Wisdom of God as an inward witness).

### XXI *It appears from what has been said that man is well-named a* <term>microcosm</term> <note>original Greek: "μικρόκοσμος"; meaning "a little world"</note>. *Because:*

1 *He is composed of the same things as the* <term>macrocosm</term> <note>original Greek: "μακρόκοσμος"; meaning "the great world" or the universe</note>: *matter, spirit, and light.*

2 *He resembles the universe in the arrangement <note>original: "site"</note> of his members. Just as the universe is divided into three parts—the elementary <note>the physical realm of the four elements</note>, the celestial <note>the realm of the stars and planets</note>, and the super-celestial <note>the spiritual realm beyond the stars</note>—so too a man has three cavities <note>original: "ventres or bellies"</note>: the lowest, which serves for nutrition; the middle (or the breast), which is the workshop of life and the fountain of heat; and the highest (or the head), in which the vital spirits <note>original: "animall spirits"; in early modern medicine, these were subtle fluids thought to transmit sensation and thought</note> reside, and within them reason, the image of God.*

3 *There is an analogy between the parts of the world and the parts of the body.* For example: flesh represents the earth; bones represent stones; blood and other humors <note>referring to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that were thought to determine health and temperament</note> represent waters; the vapors with which the body is full represent the air; and the vital spirit represents the heavens.

### XXII *Also, man is not without reason called the All <note>original Greek: τὸ πᾶν (to pan)</note>, because:*

1 *He has his body from the elements; his spirit from heaven; and his mind from God:* and so, in himself alone, he represents the visible and the invisible world.

2 *Man is everything, because he is capable of becoming everything; that is, either most excellent or very base. For if he gives himself to earthly things, he becomes like a beast <note>original: "brutish"</note> and falls back again to nothing; but if he gives himself to heavenly things, he is in a manner deified <term>made godlike</term> and rises above all other creatures.*

## CHAPTER 12.

### *Concerning Angels.*

**W**e join this study <note>original: "treatise"</note> concerning Angels with Physics <note>the study of the natural world</note> because they are also a part of the created world and stand next to humans in the hierarchy <note>original: "scale"</note> of creatures. By understanding human nature, the nature of *Angels* is easier to explain. Therefore, we will conclude this section with a few brief *Principles* <note>original: "Aphorisms"</note>.

I. *Angels exist.*

Divine testimonies <note>scriptural evidence</note> and appearances <note>original: "apparitions"</note> testify to this, as does a *three-fold reasoning*: 

1. Vapors, solid objects <note>original: "concretes"</note>, plants, and living creatures are mixtures of water and spirit. Now, since there is matter without spirit (such as a pure element), there must also be spirit without matter.

2. *Just as the matter of the world is divided into four kinds (the four elements), we already see the spirit of the world distinguished into the natural, vital, animal, and mental spirit. Now, the lowest degree of spirit is found alone (as in solid objects). Therefore, the highest degree may also be found alone—namely, in the Angels.* 

3. *Every creature is*

<summary>This page introduces the study of Angels as a branch of natural science, providing logical arguments for their existence based on the balance between matter and spirit in the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Angels, Physics, hierarchy of creatures, matter and spirit, elements, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 279 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical argument for the existence of Angels. It concludes the "three-fold reasoning" begun on the previous page, specifically arguing that the scale of creation requires a being of pure spirit to balance the scale between absolute nothingness and pure existence.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>
<sig>Q 3</sig>

is composed of Entity <note>being or existence</note> and Nothingness <note>original: "Nihility"</note>. (For they were nothing before the creation, but now they are something, because the Creator has bestowed on them a portion of His Being, more or less by degrees. The more "being" a thing possesses, the further it is from nothingness; and conversely, the less it has, the closer it is to nothing.) Seeing then that there is a first degree away from nothingness (which is *Chaos*, the raw beginning of an Entity), without doubt there is also a final degree, which comes closest to a pure Entity <note>pure existence or spirit without any material</note>. But man is not such a being, because having matter mixed in, he still shares much with nothingness. Therefore, of necessity, there must be a creature who, with materiality removed, retains all other perfections. *And that is an Angel.*

### II. *An Angel is an incorporeal <note>without a physical body</note> man.*

*An Angel* may be called a man in the same sense that man himself is called an animal, and an animal is called a plant, and a plant is called a solid object <note>original: "concrete"</note>, and so on (as we have set down in their definitions). That is, by reason of including the nature <note>original: "form"</note> of the preceding level of existence, with only a new perfection added on top. For a man is a rational creature made in the *Image of God* and immortal; so is an *Angel*, but for the sake of greater perfection, they are free from a body.

<vocab>Entity, Nothingness <note>Nihility</note>, Chaos, Angel, incorporeal, rational creature, Image of God</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that Angels must exist to complete the hierarchy of creation, serving as the highest form of creature—one that possesses the rational "image of God" without the limitations of a physical body.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, Great Chain of Being, Angelology, Entity, Nihility, Chaos, incorporeal, Image of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical argument regarding the nature of Angels. The previous page concluded that there must be a creature who possesses all the perfections of a human being but lacks a material body, identifying this being as an Angel.</meta>

body. Therefore, an *Angel* is *nothing but a man without a body; and a man is nothing but an Angel clothed with a body.*

But that Angels are incorporeal <note>original: "incorporous"; lacking a physical body</note> is shown by these reasons:
1. Because although they may be present, they are not perceived by sight or any other sense. 
2. Because they can take upon themselves earthly, watery, airy, fiery, or mixed bodies as the situation requires, and put them off again; which they could not do if they had fixed bodies of their own as we have. Yet, they usually appear in a human form because of the similarity of their natures to ours, as we have already mentioned.

### III. *Angels were created before all visible things.*

This was shown in the Appendix of the first chapter; you may look it up again if necessary. And the words of Moses <note>referring to the Book of Genesis in the Bible</note> are clear: 
> *In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth: and the earth was void.* 

See how the earth was (in that first production) empty and void! Therefore, heaven was not void; it was already filled with its "host" <note>original: "host," meaning a great multitude or army</note>, the Angels.

### IV. *The Angels were created out of the Spirit of the world.*

Just as Moses seems to include the creation of *Angels* under the name of *Heaven,*

heaven, so also the universal Spirit. For he <note>Moses, as the presumed author of Genesis</note> does not say that this was created with the earth; instead, he abruptly announces after the creation of the earth *that the Spirit of God moved itself upon the waters*. This suggests that the Spirit was already in existence. We conclude, therefore, that the Angels were formed out of that Spirit. Thus, part of that spirit was left in the invisible heaven and shaped into purely spiritual substances—**Angels**—and part was sent down into the material world below. This happened in the same way that fire was later partly left in the sky and fashioned into shining globes <note>the stars and planets</note>, and partly sank into the depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> of the earth for the formation of minerals and other uses. The following points make this opinion probable (if not demonstrable):

1. Principles should not be multiplied without cause <note>A reference to the philosophical rule known as Occam’s Razor, which suggests that one should not make more assumptions than necessary</note>. Since the Scripture does not say they were created out of nothing, nor does it name any other source <note>original: "principle"</note>, why should we not be satisfied with the principles that *Moses* has set down?

2. Angels govern the bodies which they assume, just as our spirit inhabiting the

<meta>catchword: matter</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Angels were created from the "Spirit of God" mentioned in Genesis, suggesting they are composed of the same universal substance as the "heavenly" part of the world, much like stars are made of celestial fire.</summary>

matter does. Therefore, they are similar to it.

3. There is in Angels a sense of things, just as there is in our spirits. (For they see, hear, touch, and so on, even though they themselves are invisible and cannot be touched.) They also have a sense of pleasure and grief; for joy is said to be prepared for the Angels, and fire for the devils (into which wicked men are also to be cast). Therefore, although they perceive things without physical organs, we must conclude that they are not unlike our own spirit, which perceives through organs.

### V *The Angels were created perfect.*

That is, they were completed in the same moment, so that nothing is added to their essence by later <note>original: "adventitious"</note> growth. Because they are immaterial, they are also free from the laws of matter—which require a thing, as it tends toward perfection, to be condensed, fixed, increased, and thus enlarged and made solid by specific additions.

### VI *Angels are not begotten.*

Humans, animals, and plants are generated because the spirit contained within their matter spreads itself through that matter and attempts <note>original: "essays"</note> to create new entities. But an Angel

<summary>This page explores the sensory nature and immediate perfection of Angels, arguing that they possess senses without physical organs and do not undergo biological growth or reproduction.</summary>
<keywords>Angels, spiritual senses, perfection, immateriality, generation, essence, spirit, creation</keywords>

--- Page 283 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Angels, specifically addressing why they do not reproduce or die, their immense numbers, and their dwelling place in the highest heaven.</meta>

<page-num>233</page-num>

Since it [the Angel] is without matter, and its essence cannot be broken down <note>original: "dissipated"</note>, it has no way to transfer <note>original: "transfuse"</note> itself into another. For this reason, Christ says that in Heaven we shall be like the Angels, without reproduction <note>original: "generation"</note> or the desire for reproduction (Matthew 22:30).

### VII *Angels do not die.*

The spirits of animals and plants perish because when their matter (which is their vehicle or "chariot") is broken down, the spirit is also broken down. But an **Angel**, having its essence held together by itself without physical matter, cannot be broken down and therefore endures.

### VIII *The number of Angels is virtually infinite.*

See Job 25:2–3; yet Daniel names "thousands of thousands" and "myriads of myriads" <note>original: "myriads of myriads"; a myriad is ten thousand</note> (Daniel 7:10), as does John (Revelation 5:11).

### IX *The home of the Angels is the "Heaven of Heavens."* (Matthew 18:10 and 6:10).

Therefore, they are called the **Angels** of Heaven (Galatians 1:8) and the Host of Heaven (1 Kings 22:19). For it was fitting <note>original: "meet"</note> that just as the earth, sea, air, and sky have their inhabitants, the "Heaven of Heavens" should not be left empty. Yet they are sent out from there for the following duties <note>original: "Ministeries"</note>.

### X *God created the Angels so that they might be:*
1. *The delight of their Creator.*
2. *The supreme spectators of his glory.*
3. *His assistant ministers in governing the world.*

The Scripture teaches this everywhere, but the names given to them also point toward this. The first name <note>original: "appellation"</note> of Angels is in **Genesis 3:24**: **Cherubim**, which means **Images**. This implies that they were made in the image of God, just as humans were. **But note what it means to be made in the image of God.** 

The essential image of God, or the exact representation <note>original: "character"</note> of his substance, is the Son, his eternal Wisdom (**Hebrews 1:3**). Humans and Angels are said to be created in his likeness; that is, they are made as creatures with understanding. Because of this, they are also called the **Sons of God** (**Job 1:2**). Since an image brings delight to the person it represents, it is implied that God made the Angels primarily for himself—so that he might have companions to live with him, who could behold his glorious majesty face to face and share in eternal happiness <note>original: "beatitude"</note>. Now, the most common name for Angels in the Old Testament...

Testament is <term>מלאכים</term> *Malachim* <note>Hebrew: "messengers" or "ambassadors"</note>, that is, **Ambassadors**. In the New Testament, they are called *Angeloi* <note>original Greek: "ἄγγελοι"</note>, which means **messengers**. They are given these names because God created them to be rulers and governors of the world. 

Whenever the course of nature is to be interrupted, or whenever anything is to be performed beyond the ordinary order of nature, God uses their assistance. For example:

*   When the fire was to be cooled so that it would not burn (**Daniel 3:25, 28**).
*   When the mouths of lions were to be stopped so that they would not tear Daniel to pieces (**Daniel 6:22**).
*   When the schemes <note>original: "enterprises"</note> of the wicked were to be hindered (**Numbers 22:22**).
*   When anyone was to be struck by a sudden death (**Exodus 12:23; 1 Chronicles 21:15; 2 Chronicles 32:21; and Acts 12:23**).
*   When the godly were to be delivered from danger (**Genesis 19:1**).
*   When travelers were to be guided on their way (**Psalm 91:11**).
*   When people were to be protected from any accident, lest they should be dangerously hurt (**Psalm 91:12**).
*   When someone was to be warned of something in a dream or by other means (**Matthew 1:20, etc.**).

Because of this, it is also believed that they are assigned to certain individuals specifically (**Hebrews 1:14; Matthew 18:10; Acts 12:15**), so that they may

### XI Angels can act upon bodies, but they cannot be acted upon by bodies.

Both of these are apparent by their effects. For angels carry, move, and govern the bodies which they assume; but those bodies which are separate from them, they can overthrow, stop, and move from place to place with external force <note>original: "violence"</note> at their pleasure. Yet, in the meantime, they themselves can be hindered or stopped by no physical body. <note>In traditional philosophy, "to suffer" (Latin: *pati*) means to be the passive recipient of an action; here it means angels can move objects but objects cannot physically affect or harm the angelic essence.</note>

### XII The power of angels exceeds the strength of any physical creature.

This power operates:
1.  Without resistance from objects, by passing through them.
2.  Without effort or exertion <note>original: "endeavour or enforcing"</note>, because they are not detained or hindered by their own body as our spirit is. Since our spirit is tied to a body, it must of necessity drag it along laboriously, just as a snail does its shell. 

Because of this, the angels are called **"mighty in power"** (**Psalm 103:20**) and **"powers, principalities, and dominions"** (**Colossians 1:16**).

<summary>This page explains that angels act as guardians of kingdoms and witnesses to human life, possessing a supernatural strength that allows them to move physical objects without being hindered or harmed by them.</summary>

## *XIII The agility of angels is greater than that of any physical <note>original: "corporeall"</note> substance.*

For this reason, they are compared to wind, fire, and lightning (**Psalm 104:4; Ezekiel 1:13; Luke 10:18**), and they are called **Seraphim**, which means "**flaming**" <note>original: "flamy"; from the Hebrew *saraph*, meaning "to burn"</note> (**Isaiah 6:2**). Yet it is certain that they move swifter than wind or lightning when they travel anywhere. While wind and lightning penetrate the air with some resistance, an **angel**, being a pure spirit, does so without any resistance at all. It appears, then, that although an **angel** is not in many places at once (**Daniel 10:13, 20**), they can move themselves wherever they wish in a single moment. This is why one **angel** was able to slay a whole army in a single night, and also to strike the firstborn of the **Egyptians** throughout the entire kingdom (**Isaiah 37:36; Exodus 12:23; 2 Samuel 24:6**).

## *XIV The knowledge of angels is far more exalted <note>original: "sublime"</note> than that of humans.*

This is true for three reasons:
1. Because of the clarity of their understanding, which nothing obscures <note>original: "obumbrates," from the Latin *obumbrare*, meaning to cast a shadow over or darken</note>.
2. Because of their power to penetrate any place and see things clearly.
3. Because of their long experience over so many ages (whereas we have only existed for 

<summary>This page describes the supernatural speed of angels, noting they move without physical resistance, and begins to explain why their intellect and knowledge far exceed human capabilities.</summary>
<keywords>angels, Seraphim, agility, speed of angels, angelic knowledge, biblical citations, Egypt, plague of the firstborn</keywords>

<page-num>( 238 )</page-num>

yesterday; **Job 8:9**) and yet they are not omniscient <note>original: "omniscious"</note>. For they do not know:
1. The decrees of God before they are revealed.
2. Future contingent events <note>Events that depend on free will or chance and are not yet certain</note>.
3. The thoughts of a human heart (**Jeremiah 17:9–10**)—that is, as long as they are concealed within the heart. 

For when thoughts are discovered by gestures and outward actions <note>original: "effects"</note>, the angels discern them. If we, by observing such actions, are not altogether ignorant of their thoughts (**2 Corinthians 2:11**), why should the angels not be a thousand times more perceptive <note>original: "quick sighted"</note> of us?

Now, how those angels who fell into evil engage in constant hostility with mankind, and how God uses them as executioners of judgment upon wicked men—though in the future He will condemn both the fallen angels and the wicked—just as good people are to enjoy the fellowship of good angels; and lastly, how the deceptions <note>original: "frauds"</note> of the fallen angels are to be avoided, while the presence of the good ones is to be sought: to teach these things belongs to the study of sacred Theology <note>original: "Divinity"</note>.

And we have seen that all these visible things are made out of three principles: Matter, Spirit, and Light. This is because He who is the beginning and the end of all things, the Alpha and the Omega <note>original: "α and ω"</note>, that thrice-blessed and all-powerful <note>original: "omnipotent"</note> God, three in one, is He **of whom, and through whom, and in whom are all things (Romans 11:36)**.

But now, what do the seven planets in the heavens mean? What mean the seven continents on Earth? Indeed, he himself makes a *mystical eighth* <term>mystical eighth</term><note>A theological concept where God is the transcendent source beyond the seven-fold order of creation, both including and exceeding it.</note> with every level <note>original: "degree"</note> of his creatures. Rather, because all these things possess some likeness <note>original: "effigies"</note> of the divine essence and act as they do by virtue of it, it follows that because he is above all, outside of all, and beneath all, he is the true mystical eighth of all things.

Concerning him—so that *Sirach* <note>original: "Syracides"; the author of the deuterocanonical Book of Sirach or Ecclesiasticus</note> may conclude our meditation—*though we say much, we still shall not reach him. The sum of the doctrine is that he is everything. For what power do we have to praise him? For he is greater than all his works. The Lord is awesome <note>original: "terrible"; meaning awe-inspiring or formidable</note> and very great, and his power is marvelous. Exalt the Lord in praise as much as you can, for he will still be greater than all praise* (Ecclesiasticus 43:27–30). *Therefore, let every spirit praise the Lord! Hallelujah!* (Psalm 150:6). *And you, my soul, praise the Lord* (Psalm 103:1). *Holy, holy, holy, Lord of Hosts! Heaven and earth are full of his glory* (Isaiah 6:3). *Hallelujah!*

### *A Short*
# APPENDIX
#### TO
## PHYSICS. <note>original: "PHYSICKS"; in this context, the term refers to natural philosophy or the study of the physical world and the laws of nature.</note>

---

->Concerning the Diseases of the<-
->Body, Mind, and Soul, and their<-
->general Remedies.<-

->*I.*<-

***A*** *disease is the corruption of an entity in some part of it, and a tendency <note>original: "disposition"</note> toward total perishing (that is, death).*

Therefore, the body, the mind, and the soul all have their diseases.

->*II.*<-

*The diseases of the body are various, almost impossible to count, and often mixed.*

A disease added to another disease is called a symptom <note>original: "symptome"</note> of a disease.

<vocab>Physics, Disease, Body, Mind, Soul, Symptom</vocab>

III. A disease of the body is caused either by a breach <note>original: "solution"</note> of that which is continuous <note>original: "continued"; a "solution of continuity" is an archaic medical term for a break in skin, bone, or tissue</note>, or by an imbalance <note>original: "distemper"</note> of the bodily fluids <note>original: "humours"</note>.

IV. A breach of tissue <note>original: "solution of that which is continued"</note> is caused either by a rupture or a wound. A rupture is prevented by being careful of falls and violent motion. A wound is avoided by shunning those things which can cleave, cut, prick, rip <note>original: "rent"</note>, tear, bruise, or cause injury in any way. Both are to be cured by the surgeon <note>original: "Chirurgion"</note>.

Note well <note>original: "N. B." or "nota bene"</note>: 1. The cure of a wound is hopeless <note>original: "desperate"</note> if any vital organ <note>original: "member"</note> is injured, such as the heart, the brain, the liver, the intestines <note>original: "entrals"</note>, etc. In these cases, the vital functions <note>original: "actions"</note> are hindered and soon after cease. 2. If any limb <note>original: "member"</note> is completely lost, it cannot be reattached, because the life force <note>original: "spirit"</note> has no way <note>original: "wherewithall"</note> to pass into the part that has been severed.

V. The imbalances <note>original: "distempers"</note> of the bodily fluids <note>original: "humours"</note> and the diseases that result from them always proceed from one of these six causes: namely, from:

->1. Indigestion <note>original: "Crudity"</note><-
->2. Swelling or Gas <note>original: "Inflation"</note><-
->3. Catarrh or Leakage <note>original: "Distillation"</note><-
->4. Blockage <note>original: "Obstruction"</note><-
->5. Decay <note>original: "Putrefaction"</note><-
->6. Inflammation<-

VI *Indigestion <note>original: "Crudity"</note> in the body is nutrition that has not been sufficiently digested <note>original: "concocted"</note>; namely, either the milky fluid produced during digestion <note>original: "chyle"</note> or the blood. This condition arises:*

1. *From the quality of food and drink: when they are consumed while too raw, phlegmatic <note>original: "flegmatick"; in the theory of the four humours, this referred to cold and moist substances that produced excess mucus</note>, or unwholesome, so that the digestive power <note>original: "concoctive faculty"</note> cannot properly overcome them.*

2. *From the quantity: when more food and drink is consumed than the body is able to transform and absorb <note>original: "assimilate"</note>. Because of this, undigested and unabsorbed fluids <note>original: "humours"</note> burden the body like intruders that do not belong there.*

3. *For want of exercise: when the natural heat is not stimulated or strengthened to vigorously perform its duty in the digestion <note>original: "concoction"</note> of food.*

*Various inconveniences follow from such types of indigestion.* For 1. if the indigestion is in the stomach, it causes a loathing of food; for as long as the first meal is not digested, there can be no appetite for another. Additionally, children may develop a craving to eat earth, chalk, coals, etc., depending on how the undigested matter <note>original: "crudities"</note> is transformed. This is because "like desires like" <note>an ancient medical belief that the body craves substances similar in nature to the imbalanced "humours" within it</note>. 2. If there is a sticky <note>original: "viscous"</note> undigested mass clinging to the stomach <note>original: "ventricle"</note> or in the intestines, it becomes animated <note>original: "takes spirit"</note> when warmed and is turned into worms <note>this refers to the historical belief in spontaneous generation, where parasites were thought to be created directly from decaying matter or stagnant fluids</note>. These worms, by gnawing at the bowels, stir up evil vapors by
