### VIII *Distillation is the condensation of raw <note>original: "crude"</note> vapors into phlegm <note>original: "rheume"</note>, which is the cause of many evils.*

For raw <note>original: "crude"</note> vapors rise up to the head. When, by reason of their great quantity and thickness <note>original: "grossenesse,"</note> they cannot be purged <note>original: "expurgated"</note> through the ordinary passages, they become phlegm <note>original: "rheume"</note> that flows in several directions, causing various diseases.

1. If they flow abundantly and run from the nose, they cause the *cold* or *nasal congestion* <note>original: "the Murre or Pose"; archaic terms for the common cold or a stuffed nose</note>.
2. If the distillation falls into the throat, it causes *catarrh* <note>original: "the Catarrhe"</note>.
3. If it falls into the glands <note>original: "kernels"</note> of the jaws, it causes *quinsy* <note>original: "the Quinsie"; a painful abscess or severe swelling of the throat/tonsils</note>.
4. If it falls into the lungs, it causes difficulty breathing and *asthma*.
5. If the distillation is salty and sharp, ulcerating the lungs, it causes a *cough*.
6. If this happens often and the lungs become filled with abscesses <note>original: "apostemes"</note>, it causes *consumption* <note>original: "the conſumption"; historically, a wasting disease often identified as tuberculosis</note>.

For when the ulcerated lungs cannot effectively <note>original: "with dexterity enough"</note> perform their duty <note>original: "office"</note> of cooling the heart, the "vital spirit" <note>original: "vitall spirit"; in early medicine, a refined substance in the blood believed to regulate life and body heat</note> is generated hotter than it should be. This heat does not nourish <note>original: "cherish"</note> the body but instead feeds upon the flesh and blood, and at length burns out the very factory <note>original: "workhouse"</note> of the blood itself, which is the liver: from which...

X. **Putrefaction** <note>The decay or rotting of organic matter within the living body</note> is the corruption of a fluid <note>original: "humour"</note> in the body: specifically, either of phlegm, yellow bile <note>original: "choler"</note>, or black bile <note>original: "melancholy"</note>. *The cure is:* 1. Cleaning out <note>original: "Expurgation"</note> the affected area; 2. A good diet; 3. Physical activity <note>original: "Motion"</note>.

XI. **Inflammation** is a burning of the vital spirit <note>The life-force believed to circulate through the body and sustain heat and movement</note> or of the blood. It is caused by too much activity—either of the body (by exhausting it) or of the mind (through brooding and anger). It can also be caused by putrefaction or by a blockage <note>original: "obstruction"</note>.

For it is known from natural science <note>original: "physicks"</note> that motion creates heat, even to the point of ignition, and that a blockage intensifies the heat through a surrounding reaction <note>original: "Antiperistasis"; a philosophical and medical term for the intensification of a quality when surrounded by its opposite, such as heat becoming more intense when surrounded by cold</note> that provokes <note>original: "exasperate"</note>—

XII. **The fever, so named from its fervency or heat, is of three kinds:** 1. The **Ephemeral** <note>original: "Ephemera"; a fever lasting only a short time, often a single day</note>. 2. The **Ephemeral fever** <note>original: "Ephemera"; from the Greek word for "lasting a day"</note> is a more subtle flame, feeding upon the spirits <note>The vital spirits or life-force</note> only: and therefore it scarcely lasts one or two days, until the harmful cause <note>original: "peccant cause"</note> is consumed by the spirit itself. Hence, either health or death usually follows within two or three days; and therefore it is called the **Ephemeral** or **daily fever** <note>original: "diary Feaver"</note>: also the **malignant fever**. The **pestilential infection** <note>likely referring to the plague or similar contagious diseases</note> is also of this sort, for it develops in the same manner.

***Putrid fevers*** are most common, but with very great differences: for when the humors <note>The body's four vital fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</note> rot <note>original: "putrifie"</note> within their vessels (or organs), especially near the heart (in the liver or the gallbladder), the spirit rises against them and ignites them. It does not cease to assault them until it either expels the rot—having turned it into soot <note>The medical belief that internal "burning" of impurities created "fuliginous" vapors or soot as waste</note>—or until the spirit itself is extinguished. Therefore, this fever is often deadly; it is called the **Continual Fever**.

But if the humors rot outside of their vessels—that is, in the veins or members—it is an **Intermittent Fever**. For the spirit rises up at certain times and opposes that rottenness with heat. And if the *decaying* <unclear>humor</unclear> *is phlegm*, it returns to fight it again the very next day; this results in a *Quotidian fever* <note>original: "Quotidian Feaver"; a fever that recurs every day</note>. If *it is yellow bile* <note>original: "yellow choler"</note>, then the fever returns every third day; hence the *Tertian fever*. If it is *black bile* <note>original: "black choler"</note>, the fever returns every fourth day; hence the *Quartan fever*. 

The cause of these different intervals is that phlegm gathers itself together the fastest and creates new work for the spirits, but it is also more quickly cleared away <note>original: "dissipated"</note>; therefore, a Quotidian fever does not last very long. Melancholy <note>another name for black bile</note>, being a sediment-like humor <note>original: "dreggy humour"</note>, does not recover its strength as quickly. However, because it is heavy and sticky <note>original: "soft and viscous"</note>, it is not easily defeated; this explains the long duration of Quartan fevers. In a Tertian fever, because the spirit fights against yellow bile (which is naturally hot itself), the most intense struggle occurs; for this reason, Tertian fevers are called "burning fevers." These fevers are sometimes changed from one type into another, or occur together, depending on which decaying humors the spirit must fight at different times.

From this, it appears:
1. Why does a fever begin with a cold chill? Because the vital spirit <note>The life-force believed to circulate through the body to maintain health and heat</note>, preparing to fight the decay <note>original: "rottennesse"</note>, draws heat from every part of the body to use as its aid. In the meantime, the outer limbs become cold and numb,

It also explains the physical cause of fever chills as the vital spirit withdrawing heat from the limbs to fight internal infection.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a numbered list explaining the physiological mechanics of fevers, specifically why they involve chills, heat, exhaustion, and why the "vital spirit" must prioritize fighting the disease over digestion.</meta>

<page-num>254</page-num>
<meta>catchword: ri-</meta>

...and <insert>or</insert> shivering with cold. (For even in cases of extreme fear, when the spirit <note>The vital spirit or life-force believed to regulate bodily functions</note> gathers itself into the internal organs, a chilliness in the limbs and shivering with cold usually follows.) *2. Why does heat follow afterward?* Because the spirits, having become hotter from the struggle and movement, return again to the limbs; since these limbs were cold before, they are even less able to endure the heat, which returns now hotter than usual. *3. Why does the fever leave faintness behind?* Because the spirit, exhausted from the struggle, retreats to rest, leaving the limbs weakened <note>original: "destitute"</note>. *4. Why is food harmful at the beginning of a fever?* Because when the spirit is preparing itself for battle, another task is imposed upon it (to digest <note>original: "concoct"</note> the food). But seeing that it is not able to do both, it either attacks the disease more weakly, or else leaves the food undigested; or at the very least, if it does both, it weakens and exhausts itself too much. *5. Why is it dangerous to drive out a fever too soon?* Because the fever is itself a benefit to nature, clearing away the corruption <note>original: "rottenneffe"</note> in time, lest it should eventually overcome and overwhelm the heart. Therefore, a good cure for fevers is not one that simply stops the fits <note>A "fit" or paroxysm is the period of intense symptoms during a recurring fever</note>, but one that...

<vocab>feaver, spirit, motion, battell, concoct, rottenneffe, fits</vocab>

<summary>The author explains five physiological responses to fever, arguing that fever is a natural defense mechanism where the body's "spirit" fights internal corruption. It warns against suppressing fevers too quickly or eating during the onset, as these actions distract the body's vital force from its "battle" against the disease.</summary>

--- Page 305 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the treatment of fevers, concluding with general advice on bodily health before transitioning into a new section regarding mental and spiritual health.</meta>

<page-num>(255)</page-num>
<warning>Ink blots at the top and bottom of the page; text at the inner margin is slightly obscured by the binding.</warning>

...ripens the corruption <note>original: "rottenness"</note> for expulsion and strengthens nature to oppose them, which I leave to Physicians.

Let this be the summary of what has been said: indigestion <note>original: "crudity"; the state of food being undigested or "raw" in the stomach, which was believed to cause many illnesses</note> is the seed of all diseases. For from that, thick <note>original: "gross"</note> vapors arise and cause swelling <note>original: "inflation"</note>; when these same vapors are condensed in the head, they cause a discharge <note>original: "distillation"; a downward flow of excess bodily fluids or catarrh</note>; in the other limbs <note>original: "members"</note>, they cause obstruction, from which flows either corruption or inflammation. 

Therefore, let anyone who prevents indigestion believe this: that he takes the best possible course for his whole body. Now, the way to prevent these issues is through a temperate diet and daily exercises. 

<margin>☛</margin> Oh, the strange virtue of labor, by which we obtain both our bread and our health! If the slothful understood this mystery, they would not waste their lives in idleness.

### Of the Diseases of the Mind.

I. The diseases of the mind are vices, which cause either *disquiet* or *grief* to it.

II. Diseases that disquiet the mind are evil desires—that is, too much *ardency* <note>Intense passion or eagerness</note>. These include: 1. *Of living*. 2. *Of eating* and *drinking*. 3. *Of multiplying itself* <note>referring to procreation or sexual desire</note>. 4. *Of knowing*. 5. *Of having*. 6. *Of excelling*.

Note: These are expressed by their proper names as follows: 1. *Self-love*. 2. *Intemperance*. 3. *Lust* <note>original: "salacity"</note>. 4. *Curiosity*. 5. *Covetousness*. 6. *Ambition*. For those who are given to these things itch and are <unclear>continually</unclear> disquieted.

III. The diseases that cause grief to the mind are *immoderate affections*; that is, *violent alterations*...

<sig>G 5</sig>

<summary>This page concludes the author's advice on bodily health, emphasizing that indigestion is the root of disease and is best prevented by exercise and diet; it then begins a classification of mental "diseases" or vices such as ambition, lust, and greed.</summary>

<keywords>indigestion, distillation, exercise, labor, sloth, diseases of the mind, vice, self-love, intemperance, lust, curiosity, covetousness, ambition</keywords>

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the "passions" or emotional disturbances of the mind before transitioning to the spiritual "diseases of the soul" and providing a final prayer for health.</meta>

<page-num>256</page-num>

<warning>The left edge of the page is heavily frayed and shows the binding. There is a significant dark stain on the lower right corner, though the text remains legible.</warning>

...passions <note>original: "ons," the conclusion of "passions" from the previous page</note>, for <gloss>meaning</gloss> those things which happen to us according to our desires, or contrary to them: but especially sadness, anguish <note>original: "Angor"</note>, and a weariness <note>original: "Irksomnesse"</note> of life.

IV. The remedies for the mind are presented in the study of Ethics. Their summary comes to this: *Love the "Golden Mean" <note>The ideal moderate position between two extremes</note>, and avoid extremes as you would a steep cliff. Never desire to do more than you are able. Remember that you are human; for what can happen to anyone can happen to everyone. There is a constant change <note>original: "vicissitude"</note> in all things; an unconquered mind overcomes all obstacles, etc.*

### Of the Diseases of the Soul.

I. The diseases of the soul are: forgetfulness of God, the torment of conscience, and despair of mercy.

II. Forgetfulness of God is cured by the fear of God—specifically, the fear of that God who sees everything, judges everyone, and rewards each person according to their deeds. It is impossible to escape His hand (for "in Him we live and move and have our being" <note>original: Acts 17:28</note>), but to endure His judgment is unbearable (for He is a "consuming fire" <note>original: Hebrews 12:29</note>, etc.).

III. The torment of conscience is healed by prayers and the *pursuit of innocence (Psalm 26:6, Ecclesiastes 12:13–14)*. For if our heart does not condemn us, we have full confidence before God (*1 John 3:21*).

IV. Despair is healed by the blood of that only Lamb of God, which cleanses <note>original: "purgeth"</note> us from all sin (*1 John 1:7*), reconciles us to His Father (*Romans 3:25*), saves us (*Romans 5:9*), and gives us eternal life (*John 6:54*).
